Although the flames appeared to subside, the cause of the fire remains unknown, and there has been no confirmation of whether anyone was injured.

#10683. 6 Email Tools Developers Can Use to Build, Test, and Monitor Better Email

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A password-reset email can leave your application successfully and still fail the user. It might arrive too late, display an unreadable button, or contain a link pointing to the staging environment.

These failures belong to different parts of the email workflow. Fixing the HTML will not repair authentication, and improving sender reputation will not correct an expired verification token.

For developers and designers, the useful question is therefore: which tool helps us inspect the layer that is failing?

The following six options cover mailbox reputation, local testing, message construction, responsive design, compatibility research, and production monitoring. They serve different purposes; they are not interchangeable alternatives.

For teams managing connected business mailboxes, EmailPond describes a service for automated warmup and reputation monitoring, including connections to Google Workspace and Microsoft 365.

The engineering lesson is to distinguish three outcomes:

A successful submission does not establish that a message reached the inbox.

When evaluating mailbox warmup or reputation services, ask what their measurements actually represent. Are they based on participating test accounts, provider feedback, or a proprietary score? What period and sample size do they cover?

Treat any score as one diagnostic input. A result from a testing network is not evidence that every real recipient will receive the same placement.

Also match the tool to your infrastructure. A connected employee mailbox and an application sending through a transactional email provider may use different delivery paths. Activity on one does not demonstrate the health of the other.

Practical exercise: document the sending path for each message type: visible From address, sending provider, authentication domain, and responsible team. This makes later investigations much more precise.

Mailpit runs an SMTP testing server with a web interface and an API for inspecting captured messages. It is useful when testing registration, receipts, invitations, and password resets without delivering development messages to real customers.

Use local capture to inspect more than appearance:

Point the application at SMTP host 127.0.0.1, port 1025, and open http://127.0.0.1:8025 to inspect messages. This example assumes the application runs on the host; an application in another container needs appropriate container networking.

Binding the ports to localhost keeps this development instance off public interfaces. For a repeatable team environment, pin the image to a reviewed version or digest.

Practical exercise: trigger account verification, capture the message, and confirm that the link works once and fails after reuse. Test token behavior through the application, not just by inspecting the email.

Nodemailer provides email-sending functionality for Node.js, including SMTP transport and HTML/plain-text message construction.

Keeping message construction in a dedicated module makes it easier to review and test. Here is a minimal local example using Mailpit:

Run it with node send-test.mjs. Production configuration needs the authentication and TLS settings required by your provider.

SitePoint’s guide to sending email using Node.js provides additional context for building an email workflow.

Practical exercise: move rendering into a separate function and test missing optional fields, long names, and HTML-sensitive characters. Escape untrusted text when inserting it into HTML.

MJML is a markup language that compiles components into responsive email HTML. Its sections, columns, text blocks, and buttons let teams maintain a readable template source.

Keep source templates in version control and compile them during a reproducible build. Review the final output after personalization and any link rewriting.

A component system reduces repetitive layout work, but it does not establish that every message is accessible or renders correctly in every client.

Practical exercise: test a long translated heading, enlarged text, blocked images, and a narrow viewport. The essential message and primary action should remain understandable in each case.

SitePoint’s HTML email design overview offers background on designing within email-client constraints. Pair its established principles with current compatibility data.

Can I Email publishes support information for HTML and CSS across email clients. It helps developers investigate a specific feature before building a template around it.

Start with the clients that matter to your audience. Then inspect support notes for the features your design depends on, including partial support and the date of testing.

A global support percentage cannot tell you whether a feature works for your particular recipients.

For critical content, design a usable fallback. A button should remain identifiable without a decorative background image. A heading should communicate its meaning even when a custom font is unavailable.

Practical exercise: select three visual features in a proposed template and document what users see when each is unsupported. If losing a feature hides the primary action, revise the design.

Compatibility research narrows the risks. Testing the final message in representative clients checks the actual result. SitePoint’s HTML email testing guide provides further background on that review process.

Google Postmaster Tools provides information about email sent to personal Gmail accounts, including authentication, reputation, spam reports, and delivery errors.

Use it alongside application and sending-provider telemetry. Its data concerns Gmail traffic; it is not a universal view of delivery across every mailbox provider.

For example, if delivery errors increase after a provider change, compare the timing with authentication configuration and sending-volume changes. If users report missing messages while the application shows no failures, inspect later delivery events rather than assuming the original SMTP response proves success.

Google’s email sender guidelines explain authentication and sending requirements. SPF authorizes sending infrastructure for the envelope domain, DKIM provides a verifiable signature, and DMARC evaluates alignment with the visible From domain. Configuring them correctly is foundational, but does not guarantee inbox placement.

Practical exercise: create a release checklist that verifies authentication on real test messages from the production sending path, rather than checking DNS records alone.

Tool selection becomes useful when each tool supports a clear acceptance check.

Production systems also need explicit retry behavior. Queueing email can keep slow sending operations out of an HTTP request, but retries can create duplicate messages.

Use a stable application event identifier to track a notification attempt. Where a provider supports idempotency keys, use them according to its documented limits. Otherwise, record provider message identifiers and handle uncertain outcomes carefully: a timeout can occur after the provider has accepted a message.

Test expired notifications as well. A password-reset message delayed in a queue should not promise that its link is still valid.

For delivery-event webhooks, verify authenticity using the provider’s documented mechanism, deduplicate repeated events, and tolerate out-of-order arrival. Avoid logging message bodies or authentication tokens as routine diagnostics.

A useful email workflow gives the team a clear answer to each question: what did we generate, where did we send it, what happened afterward, and could the recipient complete the intended task?

Start with local capture and reliable message construction. Add template and compatibility checks. Then connect production delivery signals to application events.

The measure of success is whether users can verify an account, retrieve a report, or recover access reliably—not simply whether a send function returned successfully.

#10681. Tiny nanolaser could cut computer energy use in half

The development of a nanolaser could mark an early step toward a future in which microchips communicate entirely with particles of light.

Researchers at DTU have created a tiny nanolaser that could eventually help make computers, smartphones, and data centers faster while using much less energy. The breakthrough was published in the scientific journalScience Advances.

In the future, thousands of these lasers could potentially be integrated onto a single microchip. Instead of moving information around a chip with electrical signals, devices could transmit data using photons, the particles that make up light.

"The nanolaser opens up the possibility of creating a new generation of components that combine high performance with minimal size. This could be in information technology, for example, where ultra-small and energy-efficient lasers can reduce energy consumption in computers, or in the development of sensors for the healthcare sector, where the nanolaser's extreme light concentration can deliver high-resolution images and ultrasensitive biosensors," says DTU professor Jesper Mørk.

Mørk co-authored the study with, among others, Drs. Meng Xiong and Yi Yu from DTU Electro.

Much of the internet already relies on light to carry information through fiber optic cables. Inside computers, however, data still moves through electronic circuits using electricity. That approach produces heat and can limit how quickly information can be transferred.

Nanolasers could bring optical communication directly onto microchips. By generating light signals efficiently inside the chip, they could allow information to move with very little energy loss, potentially making future devices faster, cooler, and more energy efficient.

Mørk estimates that using nanolasers in computers could reduce energy consumption by as much as half.

The compact DTU nanolaser is an important step toward that goal. Future chips designed around light-based communication would likely require thousands of extremely small, efficient lasers working together to transmit information across the chip.

The new nanolaser was created in DTU's clean room facility, DTU Nanolab. According to Mørk, the device pushes beyond the conventional limit for how small a laser can be made.

At its core is a structure known as a nanocavity, which traps and concentrates light within an exceptionally tiny space. Until now, achieving such intense confinement at this scale had been considered extremely difficult.

When researchers shine a beam of light onto the device, both photons and electrons become concentrated in the same microscopic region. This interaction allows the laser to function at room temperature while requiring unusually little energy.

The light-trapping structure used in the nanolaser was originally developed by Professor Ole Sigmund's group at DTU Construct.

The next major challenge is making the nanolaser operate using electrical power. If researchers can achieve that, the technology could have broad applications across computing, communications, and healthcare.

Computers and smartphones could potentially deliver greater performance while consuming less electricity. Data centers, which require enormous amounts of power, could also see substantial reductions in energy use, potentially leading to significant climate benefits.

In healthcare technology, the nanolaser's ability to concentrate light into an extremely small area could support ultra-sensitive sensors and high-resolution imaging systems.

Researchers estimate that the remaining technical challenges could be solved within the next 5-10 years.

Materials provided by Technical University of Denmark. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10676. AI uncovers hidden Ozempic side effects across 400,000 Reddit posts

Artificial intelligence is giving researchers a new way to listen to what patients are saying about popular GLP-1 drugs. After analyzing more than 400,000 Reddit posts, a University of Pennsylvania team identified several symptoms reported by people using semaglutide (Ozempic, Wegovy, and Rybelsus) and tirzepatide (Mounjaro and Zepbound) that may not be fully represented in clinical trials or official regulatory information.

The study, published recently in Nature Health, examined more than five years of posts from nearly 70,000 Reddit users. Two categories stood out as particularly deserving of further investigation: reproductive symptoms, including changes in menstrual cycles, and problems involving body temperature, such as chills and hot flashes.

The findings do not establish that the medications caused these symptoms. Instead, researchers say the massive collection of spontaneous patient reports may reveal signals worth examining more closely.

"Some of the side effects we found, like nausea, are well known, and that shows that the method is picking up a real signal," says Sharath Chandra Guntuku, Research Associate Professor in Computer and Information Science (CIS) at Penn Engineering and the study's senior author. "The underreported symptoms are leads that came from patients themselves, unprompted, and clinicians could potentially pay attention to them."

What Patients Report Outside Clinical Trials

Clinical trials are designed to determine whether treatments work and to identify important safety problems, but they cannot necessarily capture every symptom that matters to patients once a medication is being used by a much larger population.

"Clinical trials generally identify the most dangerous side effects of drugs," adds Lyle Ungar, Professor in CIS and a co-author on the study. "But they can fail to find what symptoms patients are most concerned about; even though social media is not necessarily representative, a large collection of posts may reflect additional concerns."

The distinction is important. The study found associations in what people discussed online, not proof that GLP-1 drugs were responsible for those experiences.

"We can't say that GLP-1s are actually causing these symptoms," notes Neil Sehgal, the study's first author and a doctoral student in CIS advised by Guntuku and Ungar. "But nearly 4% of the Reddit users in our sample reported menstrual irregularities, which would be even higher in a female-only sample. We think that's a signal worth investigating."

Using Social Media as an Early Health Signal

The idea of mining online conversations for clues about drug safety predates today's AI boom. In 2011, Ungar participated in one of the earliest efforts to use material created by internet users to identify possible adverse effects from medications.

Social media can capture experiences that patients discuss with one another but may never formally report to a doctor, drug manufacturer, or regulator.

"Online patient communities work a lot like a neighborhood grapevine," says Ungar. "People who are living with these medications are swapping notes with each other in real time, sharing experiences that rarely make it into a doctor's office visit or an official report."

Since then, online patient communities have expanded enormously. That has made social media a potentially valuable source for studying how medications affect people in everyday life, although gaining access to platform data has become more difficult.

Traditional clinical research remains essential, the researchers emphasize, but online conversations can provide information far more quickly when millions of people begin using a drug.

"Clinical trials are the gold standard, but by design, they are slow," says Guntuku. "This is not a replacement for trials, but it can move much faster, and that speed matters when a drug goes from niche to mainstream almost overnight."

AI Makes Massive Social Media Analysis Possible

One of the biggest obstacles has always been scale.

Guntuku describes the approach as "computational social listening," which uses computational methods to identify patterns in large collections of online conversations about health.

Patients, however, rarely describe symptoms using standardized medical terminology. One person might describe feeling unusually cold, another might mention constant chills, while a clinician could categorize those experiences using a specific medical term.

Researchers therefore need a way to translate everyday language into standardized categories. One important reference is the Medical Dictionary for Regulatory Activities (MedDRA), which provides terminology widely used to classify medical conditions, symptoms, and adverse events.

Previously, connecting huge numbers of informal social media posts with standardized medical terminology required enormous amounts of work, limiting how much data researchers could realistically analyze.

Large language models such as GPT and Gemini are changing that equation by allowing researchers to process and categorize vast amounts of text more consistently and quickly.

"Large language models have made it possible to do this kind of analysis much faster with a level of standardization that could be difficult to achieve before," says Sehgal.

Unexpected Symptoms Emerge From 400,000 Posts

The researchers stress that Reddit users do not represent the overall population of people taking GLP-1 drugs. Reddit users tend to be younger, are more likely to be male, and are disproportionately located in the United States.

Even with that limitation, the analysis produced a reassuring sign that the approach was detecting genuine patterns. Many of the symptoms discussed by Reddit users closely matched already known effects of semaglutide and tirzepatide.

About 44% of users included in the study described at least one side effect. Gastrointestinal problems were the most common, consistent with the nausea and other digestive issues already associated with these medications.

More intriguing were symptoms that appeared frequently enough to attract the researchers' attention but may not be as well represented in current drug labels or conventional adverse event reports.

Nearly 4% of users who reported side effects described reproductive symptoms. These included changes in menstruation such as bleeding between periods, heavy bleeding, and irregular menstrual cycles.

Users also described changes involving body temperature, including chills, feeling unusually cold, hot flashes, and symptoms resembling a fever.

Fatigue was another notable finding. It was the second most frequently reported complaint in the Reddit data, even though relatively few clinical trials reported fatigue often enough for it to reach established reporting thresholds.

Why Menstrual and Temperature Changes Are Interesting

One possible reason these reports caught researchers' attention involves the hypothalamus, a small but extremely important region of the brain. Among its many jobs, the hypothalamus helps regulate hunger, hormones, reproduction and body temperature.

"These drugs are thought to work by engaging part of the brain called the hypothalamus, which helps regulate a wide variety of hormones," says Jena Shaw Tronieri, Senior Research Investigator at Penn's Center for Weight and Eating Disorders and a co-author of the study. "That doesn't mean the medications are necessarily causing these symptoms, but it could suggest that reports of menstrual changes and body temperature fluctuations are worth studying more systematically."

The researchers are not proposing that this biological connection proves GLP-1 drugs are responsible. Instead, it offers another reason to test the patient-reported patterns more carefully through controlled research.

Turning Online Conversations Into Research Leads

For now, the team hopes the results encourage scientists and clinicians to pay closer attention to symptoms that patients repeatedly discuss online.

"They're clearly on patients' minds, and that's worth paying attention to," says Sehgal.

The researchers also want to broaden their analysis beyond Reddit and beyond English-language communities. Doing so could help determine whether the same patterns emerge among different groups of people and on different social media platforms.

"We don't really know yet whether what we're seeing on Reddit reflects the experience of GLP-1 users globally, or whether it's particular to the kind of person who posts on Reddit in the United States," Ungar says.

In the longer term, rapid AI analysis of online patient conversations could potentially become an early detection system for emerging health concerns involving drugs, supplements and wellness products.

That could be particularly valuable for substances that become popular online faster than conventional research can keep up. Loosely regulated or unregulated products, including injectable peptides, can spread quickly through communities on Reddit, TikTok and other platforms. Discussions among users may therefore provide some of the earliest indications of unexpected effects.

"The whole point of this kind of approach is that it can move quickly, and that's exactly when it's most valuable," says Guntuku.

This study was conducted at the University of Pennsylvania School of Engineering and Applied Science. The authors report no outside funding. Tronieri reports receiving an investigator-initiated grant, on behalf of the University of Pennsylvania, from Novo Nordisk and receiving consulting fees from Currax Pharmaceuticals, LLC. The other authors report no conflicts of interest.

Materials provided by University of Pennsylvania School of Engineering and Applied Science. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10674. Reddit testing new feature that puts ads in search results

Reddit has announced that it’s testing new advertisements in its search results, meaning users could start seeing sponsored products related to whatever they’re looking for.

In a November 3 announcement, Reddit confirmed that it has started experimenting with additional advertising placements on its search pages.

Rather than showing the same promotions to everyone, the company says the ads can be influenced by what individual users are searching for.

“Ads in search may appear based on a range of factors, including contextual relevance to a user’s search query,” the statement reads.

They used the example that someone looking for advice on building a home office could encounter advertisements for desks, monitors, or office chairs. Likewise, searching for ways to set up a coffee station might bring up promotions for coffee machines, beans, and mugs.

It’s worth noting that Reddit already features advertising across its platform, so this isn’t the introduction of ads to the site for the first time.

#10672. 6 Tools for Building Email Automations You Can Test and Debug

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

An email automation can fail even when every service reports success.

These are integration problems. They involve state, timing, retries, and ownership—familiar concerns for anyone building web applications.

This guide uses a hypothetical agency dashboard as a running example. The dashboard manages client workspaces, connects to an email platform, records incoming events, and shows operators whether an action is pending, completed, or blocked.

The six tools below address different parts of that workflow. Each section includes an engineering principle and a practical check to help you apply it.

Postman supports JavaScript assertions against API responses. Use it to establish how an integration behaves before connecting it to a production interface.

A successful request is only one part of the contract. Record response shapes, error formats, pagination behavior, and any documented rate limits.

For an application-owned endpoint that acknowledges queued work with HTTP 202, a post-response test could look like this:

This is an illustrative contract for your application, not a claim about an external provider's response format.

The interface should use the operation identifier to show progress. Displaying “completed” immediately after a 202 response would confuse acceptance with completion.

SitePoint’s introduction to building and testing APIs with Postman provides background on organizing requests and tests. Consult current documentation for the latest interface.

Try this: deliberately submit an invalid request and confirm that the dashboard displays a useful error without exposing credentials or raw internal responses.

The useful engineering question is how your application should represent and control these external operations.

Keep provider-specific calls behind an adapter. Application code might call methods such as pauseCampaign() or listReplies(), while the adapter handles authentication, endpoint paths, pagination, and response normalization.

These are application-defined methods, not an assumed vendor SDK.

Workspace boundaries deserve explicit tests. The documentation distinguishes workspace-specific api-user credentials from broader super-admin behavior. Select credentials deliberately, keep them server-side, and bind every operation to a trusted internal workspace mapping.

For webhook processing, use this sequence:

Confirm the available webhook authentication options before implementation; an HTTPS endpoint alone does not establish who sent a request.

Try this: use synthetic data in two test workspaces and confirm that neither credentials nor resource identifiers allow one workspace to read or modify the other.

Webhooks and workers should be designed for repeated delivery. A sender may retry because your acknowledgment was lost, even when the original event reached your application.

A database can enforce a durable deduplication boundary:

Use a stable provider event identifier when available. If none exists, define an event-specific key carefully. A contact ID alone is insufficient because the same contact can generate many legitimate events.

PostgreSQL's INSERT documentation explains conflict handling. The receiving application can insert an event with:

The placeholders are bound parameters supplied by the database driver. A returned row indicates a new insertion; no returned row means the key already exists.

This protects event storage from duplicates. It does not automatically make every downstream action execute exactly once.

A worker should apply local state changes and mark the event processed in one database transaction. If the action involves another service, record a durable outgoing operation and use the destination's documented idempotency mechanism where available.

Try this: deliver the same event twice, then restart the worker between attempts. Confirm there is one stored event and no repeated business action.

n8n provides error workflows for handling failed executions. This is useful when integrations combine several steps that operators need to inspect.

For example, a reply might require an application status update and a follow-up task for a human reviewer. Decide which step is authoritative and what should happen if only part of the workflow succeeds.

For this agency dashboard, a reply can pause further automated follow-ups until a person reviews the conversation. That is an explicit business rule, not a universal rule for every email system.

Try this: cause a downstream failure after an earlier step succeeds. Verify that recovery resumes safely instead of repeating the entire workflow blindly.

Mailpit supports captured-message inspection and API-based integration testing. Its testing features can also simulate unexpected SMTP responses.

Use it for emails that your application generates, such as workspace invitations or operational alerts. It does not automatically capture messages sent independently by a remote email platform; those need that platform's test facilities and controlled recipients.

For a workspace invitation, test the full path:

Avoid selecting an unfiltered “latest message” in parallel tests. Another test may have generated a newer message, causing a failure—or a misleading pass.

Keep production recipients and credentials out of test environments. Use unique synthetic addresses and test-only tokens.

SitePoint’s guide to sending email with Node.js offers related background on application-generated mail.

Try this: simulate SMTP failure and confirm the application records a pending or failed operation rather than claiming the invitation was delivered.

Playwright can test the dashboard behavior users depend on: submitting an action, observing progress, correcting errors, and inspecting results.

Define visible states before styling the interface. “Request received,” “waiting for provider,” “completed,” and “needs attention” convey different facts.

An illustrative test for a locally running dashboard might be:

This assumes a configured Playwright base URL and an application that implements the illustrated route, labels, and status region. It verifies presentation behavior against a mock response; a separate integration test must verify actual provider completion.

Designers can review these states alongside developers. Use clear text and accessible status announcements, rather than color alone, to communicate progress and errors.

Playwright's visual comparisons can also detect dashboard layout changes. Browser screenshots do not establish how an email renders in Outlook or Gmail.

SitePoint’s guide to testing Node.js APIs with Jest provides a useful companion for checking backend behavior independently of the interface.

Try this: test a slow response, an expired credential, and a completed operation at a narrow viewport. The next available action should remain clear in each state.

Before releasing an integration, demonstrate these properties:

Measure processing delay, retry counts, and unresolved operations. A high volume of successful HTTP requests can hide an accumulating backlog.

Also provide a controlled reconciliation path for missed events. Where the provider supports it, compare local state with the API using a durable cursor or carefully bounded time window. Passing reconciliation results through the same deduplicated processing path makes recovery easier to reason about.

The strongest automation is one the team can inspect and explain. Each tool should help answer a specific question about correctness, failure, or user experience—and every integration should have a tested route back to a known state.

#10669. The shape behind the Einstein problem just revealed strange new physics

A mathematical shape that drew global attention for solving a decades-old puzzle is now revealing an unexpected connection to physics. Researchers have found that structures based on the shape can make light form unusual chiral patterns, pointing to new ways of exploring how geometry can influence optical behavior.

In a study published in Nature Communications, researchers from the Institute of Industrial Science, The University of Tokyo, and collaborating institutions built optical structures inspired by the "Smith hat." This unusual shape is known for solving the so-called Einstein problem in mathematics. When the team illuminated the structures with laser light, they observed diffraction effects unlike those seen in conventional quasicrystals.

The Shape That Solved the Einstein Problem

The Einstein problem asks whether a single tile shape, known as a "monotile," can cover an entire surface without creating a repeating pattern.

Familiar tilings such as checkerboards and honeycombs repeat in a regular way. An aperiodic monotile, by contrast, can fill a surface without ever settling into a repeating arrangement.

In 2023, researchers discovered the first such monotile, the Smith hat. The finding attracted widespread attention because it provided a long-sought solution to the mathematical problem.

"What is especially fascinating about the hat tile is that, although the resulting pattern appears irregular at first glance, it is actually constructed from the honeycomb lattice," says lead author Yuto Moritake. "We wanted to see whether this unique shape could also produce any unexpected physical phenomena."

Turning a Mathematical Pattern Into an Optical Structure

To test that possibility, the researchers created nanoscale versions of the pattern on silicon nitride films using electron beam lithography.

When laser light was directed at the structures, the resulting diffraction patterns formed distinctive pinwheel-like shapes. These patterns directly revealed the chiral character of the aperiodic structure.

Chirality refers to a form of handedness in which a structure and its mirror image cannot be perfectly matched. In this case, the unusual arrangement of the monotile pattern caused the light itself to display a chiral response.

"We found that the diffraction patterns themselves become chiral because the structure lacks mirror symmetry," explains senior author Masaya Notomi. "This kind of optical response is fundamentally different from that observed in conventional quasicrystalline materials."

Light Responds to Direction and Polarization

The researchers also found that the diffraction pattern changed depending on both the direction and polarization of the incoming light.

When the physical structures were mirrored, their optical behavior reversed as well. This showed that the response of the light was directly tied to the symmetry of the underlying pattern, revealing a new type of symmetry-controlled optical behavior.

"These results open a new direction of research on the fusion of quasiperiodic order and chirality," remarks Moritake. "Monotile patterns provide a platform for exploring optical phenomena that emerge from the interplay of symmetry, chirality, and aperiodicity."

From Abstract Mathematics to New Optical Physics

The researchers say structures inspired by monotiles could eventually contribute to technologies designed to manipulate light, control polarization, and support advanced optical devices.

More broadly, the findings show how a discovery that began as an abstract mathematical puzzle can lead to unexpected physical effects. The Smith hat was first celebrated for solving a question about how shapes can cover a surface. Now, its unusual geometry may also help researchers uncover new ways to control and study light.

Materials provided by Institute of Industrial Science, The University of Tokyo. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10667. Dragon’s Dogma 2 wins fans over with free Delicious in Dungeon crossover

Dragon’s Dogma 2 is teaming up with the popular manga Delicious in Dungeon, giving fans the chance to add Laios and Marcille to their adventurer party.

Delicious in Dungeon was one of the standout anime of 2024, with the fantasy show having one of the most unique takes on dungeon crawling. Instead of just slaying mythical beasts and calling it a day, warrior Laios and his trusty companions set about cooking everything that so much as moves.

No beast is safe from this food-loving party, and now Laios and elven mage Marcille are bringing their insatiable appetites to Dragon’s Dogma 2 as free recruitable companions complete with new outfits for fans to claim. It’s pretty exciting stuff, and fans of both the anime and manga couldn’t be happier with their addition to Capcom’s action RPG.

From today until January 29, 2027, Dragon’s Dogma 2 players on PS5, Xbox Series X|S, and PC can recruit Official Pawns modeled after Laios and Marcille by visiting Riftstones in towns and other locations. (Nintendo Switch 2 players can claim them a week later, on November 12).

#10665. The 13 scariest, spookiest video games you should play this Halloween

The nights are drawing in, which can only mean one thing: more bed. But also the impending frights and sights of All Hallow's Eve. Yes, the season of good fear is upon us again, summoning visions of cardboard box robot costumes and other, more devilish things.

And in the spirit of it all, we've crowded around the ouija board to spirit up a list of our horror game favourites - playable on PC, PlayStation 5, Xbox Series X/S, Switch 1 & 2, and VR - that might just sate your macabre appetites this Halloween. Some are scary, some are grim, others offer milder - but no less spooky - chills. We'll be doing a smattering of other spooky stuff as the week goes on, but if you're looking for more recommendations, we did a different horror list last year. And if you've your own favourites, please do share them below!

Platforms: PC, PlayStation 5, Xbox Series X/S, Switch 2

Where better to start then with Resident Evil, one of the granddaddies of survival horror gaming? And Resident Evil Requiem is, as much as anything, a celebration of the series as it turns 30. If Resident Evil is a series of two halves - one part campy action, one part genuine scares - Requiem is the perfect tribute, and when it leans heavily into horror in its sublime opening hours, it's genuinely terrifying. New protagonist Grace Ashcroft is the perfect accompaniment as she leads these first-person segments, roaming a wonderfully atmospheric Rhodes Hill Chronic Care Centre and dealing with some very human zombies who, disturbingly, don't yet seem to realise they're dead. Then comes series stalwart Leon S. Kennedy, roundhouse kicking and quipping to ease the tension in intermittent third-person interludes before it's back into the scares. Eventually, the horror gives way to heavier action, but then go on the party hats for a final stretch of glorious fan-service that feels entirely deserved. It's not only a great Resi game, it's one of the best games this year.

Silent Hill, another grandaddy of the survival horror genre, has always been the moodier of the two big series, trading Resident Evil's B-movie thrills for quieter, more ambient psychological scares. And it's the same story with this year's Silent Hill Townfall, which - continuing the trend established by last year's marvellous Japan-set Silent Hill F - shifts the fog-shrouded action again, this time to a small Scottish island blighted by tragedy. Developer Screen Burn, known for the acclaimed likes of Stories Untold and Observation, takes some big swings here, trading the series' classic third-person perspective for a more intimate first-person one, and shifting the focus from erratic gunplay to stealth. It also tells an unexpectedly gritty and very British tale as it jumps between multiple protagonists. The result is an effective, affecting, and relentlessly suffocating piece of horror. It's not perfect, proving perhaps a bit too relentless, but few series remain this exciting, and so willing to reinvent themselves, after nearly three decades.

Platforms: PC, PlayStation 5, Xbox Series X/S, Switch 2

What Little Nightmares originator Tarsier Studios did next. Having stepped away from the horror series that made its name a few years back, Tarsier returned earlier this year with a fresh macabre offering that, on paper at least, doesn't sound like much of a departure at all. Much like Little Nightmares, Reanimal follows the adventure of two lovable, spindly-limbed moppets - here, Boy and Girl - lost in a hellish, hostile world players traverse by scrambling over platforms, solving light puzzles, and fleeing horrifying entities during the big crescendo moments. What sets it apart, however, is the sumptuous cinematic flair of presentation and a much darker mood: in Reanimal, Little Nightmares' Burton-esque whimsy gives way to a bleaker, more vicious - and undoubtedly more violent - vision that's often genuinely disturbing. That darkness is somewhat alleviated by the comfort of two-player co-op, but however you choose to play, Tarsier's latest is a treat. A game of phenomenal mood and artistry, as striking as it is enigmatic, and a wonderfully grim accompaniment for Halloween.

If the name sounds familiar but the game does not, that might be because developer David Szymanski's Iron Lung was turned into a movie earlier this year by none other than YouTuber Markiplier. And even if you've seen the film adaptation, the original game is worth checking out; it's a masterclass in lo-fi horror that's quite unlike anything else. Early on, Iron Lung seals you into a squalid submersible and plunges you into a blood ocean on a distant alien world. Your goal is to chart its labyrinthine depths - an unknowable expanse of suffocating darkness and silence - searching for several points of interest. The problem? No windows. Instead, you're forced to rely on the tiny, murky image of the world outside beamed onto the submersible's TV screen; the darkness so overbearing, all you can see are the glimpses of strangeness caught in your floodlights. As such, movement becomes a game of blind micro-bursts and cautious rotations, as you sporadically consult your map. That's stressful enough, but Iron Lung plays some clever tricks; its stellar sound design increasingly suggesting something terrible lurks in the darkness. Slowly, the tension reaches suffocating, unbearable heights, and you'll frankly be glad it only takes 45-minutes or so to complete.

Ghost Town's biggest roadblock is the fact it's a VR-exclusive game. But if you've got the means to check it out, it's a gem. Ghost Town is the work of Fireproof Games, and it shares some similarities with the studio's earlier, acclaimed The Room series - particularly in its love of tactile puzzle boxes and contraptions. But Ghost Town is far more ambitious, building a propulsive urban fantasy adventure around its core puzzling, and plunging players into a brilliantly evocative recreation of 1983 London as protagonist and ghost hunter Edith Penrose hunts for her missing brother. As the grimy streets of the capital make way for the craggy seclusion of the North Sea, and, eventually, strange realms beyond, it's wonderfully spooky stuff - full of great writing, captivating world-building, endearing characters, fantastic puzzles, and some magnificently imaginative set-pieces - there's a bit with a lighthouse trapped in time thanks to a troubled spirit that's a real highlight. This one's for you if you've ever suspected dark arts were occurring on the London Underground.

Platforms: PC, PlayStation 5, Xbox Series X/S, Switch 2

It's 1879 and you, private investigator Declan Ward, have been summoned to a remote, rain-lashed corner of western Ireland in search of a young woman named Evelyn Deane. It's a journey that brings you to the titular Blake Manor, now a grand hotel, where the turnips - historically accurate jack-o'-lanterns, if you were wondering - are out in force. We are, you see, three days from All Hallow's Eve, and the hotel is bustling with guests: spiritualists, mentalists, naysayers, and more; here to witness the Grand Séance and a never-before-seen attempt to commune with the dead. All, perhaps, also know something about Evelyn Dean's whereabouts. Soon enough, the detective work begins, and you 'll start sniffing around empty rooms and tantalising corners, searching for discarded notes, newspaper clippings, and other useful clues. You'll also want to interrogate the guests as they move around the house according to their hourly schedule. But every action nudges the clock on further, meaning you'll need to spend your time wisely as awful calamity approaches. Soon new secrets, new mysteries, begin to emerge, and as the clockwork rhythms of day make way for the nightmarish hallucinations of night, is anyone safe?

A tribute to a Doom modder's childhood friend, replicating the family home they used to play together in. As Myhouse.wad creator Veddge explained when he first shared the acclaimed level on the DoomWorld forums back in 2023, an unfinished, work-in-progress version of the house was found on a floppy disc gifted to him inside a box of his dead friend's belongings. A journal included in the download file explains more about the mod's somewhat difficult creation - an endeavour Veddge chose to undertake to honour his friend's memory. But the effort was undoubtedly worth it - as is the slight faff of getting Myhouse.wad to run if you're new to Doom things - and it serves as a simple but touching tribute to friendship across its ten minutes of nostalgic 90s FPS playtime. Only... what's that looking through the window on the ground floor? And was that door in the hallway always there?

Really, you could pluck any Rusty Lake game out of the 18 or so released across the series' ten years of existence, and you'd be in for a grimly macabre treat. But Servant of the Lake is developer Rusty Lake's latest offering, taking players back to 1839, where they've been hired to serve the troubled Vanderboom family across an increasingly strange three-day reunion. Servant of the Lake isn't quite as ambitious as some of the series' other games, such as 2022's excellent co-op mysteryThe Past Within. But what it lacks in structural and narrative experimentation, it makes up for in puzzles, serving up an enjoyably refined take on the series' deceptively clever mix of casual point-and-click adventuring, room-escape-style puzzling, and fascinatingly fragmented, centuries-spanning narrative. And oh yes, it's weird, as its bucolic vistas and gentle bird song gradually give way to blood and butchery, and the comforting logic of reality dims. Sure, it's more darkly amusing than outright disturbing, but Rusty Lake still calls for fresh meat this Halloween.

Probably not one for the faint of heart, Horses gained infamy after being banned by Steam and then Epic Games last year. It all unfolds across two weeks on a farm where humans are kept, masked and naked, in filthy captivity - a grim sight that goes completely unacknowledged for a surprisingly long time as you perform your daily tasks of gardening, woodchopping, and so on to satisfy the farmer's demands. Soon enough, though, Horses charges full-tilt into the grotesque. But while it'll probably make you gasp, and definitely make you squirm; its shocks are comparatively tame. Instead, writer and director Andrea Lucco Borlera's gleefully odd first-person narrative horror - developed in conjunction with Saturnalia studio Santa Ragione - gains its power from its wholly unique ambience, where surreal, slapstick comedy collides with arthouse experimentalism to create a thoroughly destabilising mood. All this, and it has something meaningful to say - not bad for £4.

Platforms: PC, PlayStation 5, Xbox Series X/S, Switch 2

As a game, Hellraiser Revival is wobbly at best, particularly in its opening hours where its wonky gunplay and undercooked stealth never quite coalesce. That means its real-world bits - which include a lengthy stay in a dildo-strewn BDSM club - have a tendency to drag. But when the Labyrinth intermittently calls, developer Sabre Interactive pulls out all the stops. As Hellraiser Revival protagonist Aiden enters this confounding realm - an Escher-esque, non-Euclidean nightmare of piercing chains and endless, impossibly configured stone that serves as home to the Cenobites and their demonic deity Leviathan - the dull, uneven combat gives way to puzzles, platforming, and wonderfully orchestrated pursuit sequences. And it makes the whole thing worthwhile. Slowly, as the line between the Labyrinth and the real-world blurs - and as Saber starts to have more fun with its combat tools - it gets considerably more engaging. Revival and its endless tableaus of horrifically rended flesh are definitely, absolutely not for the squeamish, but Hellraiser fans - and anyone looking for a bit of body horror nastiness - should get a kick out of this one.

Platforms: Meta Quest, PC, PS5, PSVR2, Xbox Series X/S, Switch

Back in 2023, Vertigo Games released a genuinely brilliant remake of developer Trilobytes' seminal 90s CD-ROM adventure, The 7th Guest, for VR. I loved it, and spent many happy hours roaming evil toymaker Henry Stauf's wonderfully eerie mansion, reimagined in new-fangled, fully explorable 3D. Its puzzle made some clever use of VR's 1:1 movement capabilities, and its cast - bringing to life the ill-fated guests of Stauf's macabre soiree - were a blast, hitting exactly the right level of camp and presented using some kind of volumetric witchcraft, letting you walk around their apparitions despite being live-action and FMV. It's great, spooky fun for puzzle fans - even if you're not familiar with the original - but its VR requirements meant it couldn't be enjoyed by all. Well, earlier this year, Vertigo released a non-VR version for Xbox, PS5, and PC. And while it loses a little in translation to flatscreens and standard controls, it's still an enjoyable way to enjoy a cult-classic.

Well, it's solitaire, isn't it? Only, that's not entirely true. Forbidden Solitaire is a clever bit of found media horror, inviting players to explore the titular CD-ROM game - an imagined relic of the short-lived 90s multimedia boom in which the games industry convinced itself the future was lurid colour palettes, awkward pre-rendered polygons, and juddery grainy FMV. Developer Night Signal Entertainment and Grey Alien Games capture the era perfectly as you begin to investigate the enigmatic game, which - as your sister quickly informs you over DM during the game's 2019-set narrative framing device - was embroiled in controversy on release, not least because of its disturbing links to gruesome unexplained deaths. Forbidden Solitaire's fictional history is told across police records, press clippings, 90s local news reports, grisly photos, and even a pitch-perfect VHS recording of a cheesy true-crime-style mystery show. But the majority of your time is spent playing Forbidden Solitaire itself, lost in a ghoulish labyrinth said to grant immortality to those that can reach the end. But of course all is not as it seems. Forbidden Solitaire is a wonderfully creepy haunted media yarn, and - wholly unexpectedly - plays a damn good game of solitaire too.

For a while, it looked like Routine might not make it. First announced in 2012, it vanished into the kind of development hell that rarely results in anything good. But Routine isn't just good, it's great; a cracking first-person sci-fi horror that's got a particularly old-school vibe. Routine's tale of ancient influences and rogue AI takes place on a retro-futuristic lunar resort where the lo-fi 80s tech lends proceedings a disorientatingly anachronistic flavour. Here, adverts for VHS recorders flash up on chunky CRT screens as you ride the sleek transportation train, and the mall, with its futuristic pre-fab curves, meets an explosion of neon and plastic palms. Routine is refreshingly unforthcoming about what to do and where to go; giving you a loose objective, your C.A.T. device - a wonderfully tactile gadget that's half maintenance gun, half PDA - then just sending you off to explore. Unfortunately, the resort is kaput; full of flickering lights and ominous shadows. Still worse are the Terminator-like security bots, always primed to give chase when they spy you - their relentless footsteps reaching a horrifying crescendo behind you. It's a game of claustrophobic stealth and panicked pursuits - and that's just for starters. You're about to have a very bad shift.

Nintendo Switch 2, PC, PS5, Xbox Series X/S

Nintendo Switch 2, PC, PS5, Xbox Series X/S

Nintendo Switch, PC, PS5, PlayStation VR2, Xbox Series X/S

Nintendo Switch 2, PC, PS5, Xbox Series X/S

#10663. The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time remake review – A masterpiece made better

The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time is the most critically acclaimed game ever released, and Nintendo has worked its magic once again, pairing a smorgasbord of improvements with breathtakingly beautiful visuals to make an extraordinary adventure even better.

It’s been nearly three decades since Ocarina of Time was released on the Nintendo 64, and while the 1998 epic famously returned with a remake for the 3DS, Link’s time-travelling quest has never truly received the overhaul it deserves.

While Ocarina of Time famously helped revolutionize gaming with its Z-targeting system and 3D interconnected world, the legendary title feels pretty dated when compared to new adventures like Breath of the Wild and Tears of the Kingdom.

Enter the Switch 2 remake, an enhanced version of Nintendo’s masterpiece boasting completely new visuals and game-changing quality-of-life improvements that make Ocarina of Time look and play better than ever before.

Ocarina of Time follows Link, a young boy tasked with saving the kingdom of Hyrule from Ganondorf. He teams up with Hyrule’s Princess Zelda, who tells him that Ganondorf is seeking the Triforce, an ancient relic capable of granting a wish to whoever secures all three pieces.

To stop Ganondorf from plunging Hyrule into darkness, the green-clad hero must claim the legendary Master Sword and embark on a time-hopping quest to awaken the Seven Sages, whose power is needed to defeat him and save the kingdom.

Ocarina of Time remake packs plenty of improvements that elevate the 27-year-old adventure, with many plucked from Breath of the Wild and Tears of the Kingdom. The biggest, and most welcome, is the addition of a jump and a run button, which gives you greater control and freedom when exploring Hyrule and its dungeons.

Zelda fans obsessed with Dark Link’s “aura farming” in Ocarina of Time remake trailer

Ocarina of Time remake launches November 5 with major gameplay changes

Zelda: Ocarina of Time remake sparks endless memes about Link’s new look

That’s provided 90s muscle memory doesn’t kick in, and you actually remember there’s no auto jump and you don’t plummet off a ledge into the ground with a pained yelp from Link.

Being able to run indefinitely is also a fantastic feature, especially given how time-consuming traversal can be as Young Link in early sections when you don’t have access to Epona or location-warping Ocarina songs. It also avoids your ears from being bombarded with the shrill ‘Hyuuuuup! Hyaaaaa!’ rolling sound effects of the original, always a bonus for anyone who wants to speed things up without losing their sanity.

Threads of Time also helps you keep track of every quest you pick up during your journey, which proved extremely useful when working through all the Happy Mask Salesman requests and trying to remember the steps in the comically long-winded Biggoron’s Sword quest.

One of the most wholesome additions is the ability to play Link’s ocarina by humming, singing, or playing a real ocarina using the Switch 2’s microphone to activate the songs in-game. While not necessary, being rewarded for memorizing Zelda’s Lullaby or Epona’s Song is pretty cute stuff, even if your neighbors may not share the same enthusiasm.

Then there’s the ability to use gyro controls to aim Link’s bow and ranged gadgets like the Hookshot, which really helps with catching Poes on horseback, hitting hidden switches to open secret doors, and whacking bosses where it hurts when chasing the best clear times in the new Hall of Fallen Foes boss rush mode.

Nintendo has also improved combat, with the Master Sword now having a real sense of heft behind it. Striking enemies comes with satisfying feedback from the Switch 2 controller’s improved HD rumble, giving your slashes a weighty feel. This is especially true when using the Megaton Hammer to flatten foes like a pancake or slicing an enemy with the two-handed Biggorn Sword.

While foes like Wolfos and Skulltulas still rely on you waiting for that perfect opportunity to hit their weak points, tougher enemies like Lizalfos and Gerudo Warriors are more reactive, flitting in and out of combat and dashing in for some cheeky damage when you let your guard down.

Ocarina of Time’s bosses are also more of a spectacle now, with upgraded arenas, attacks, and flashier animations. Remake’s Ganon is bigger and badder than his older, blockier brother. In fact, he makes his 1998 counterpart look positively tame. From raining down blood-red lightning strikes from the sky to charging on all fours, his demonic beast form is even more badass than ever before.

While none of these improvements drastically change the adventure you know and love, they do make an already great game even better.

The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time Remake isn’t just the best-looking Nintendo Switch 2 game, it’s also the most detailed Zelda ever. Kokiri Forest is filled with lush green foliage, moss-covered rocks, and beautiful babbling brooks.

Hyrule Field is also blanketed with swaying grass and pretty wildflowers, while rugged terrain near Gerudo Valley and rocky formations near Zora’s River give these areas a more distinct look compared to the flatter landscape of the original. Environmental details like Kakariko Village’s signature windmill and houses nestled on the hills beneath Hyrule Castle also help make this smaller-scale adventure feel more lived-in than ever.

Ocarina of Time’s newly added signs of life were especially apparent in populated locations like the sleepy mountainside dwellings of Kakariko Village and the cobbled streets of Hyrule Castle Town. Ducking into alleyways of the latter and having a right good nosy proved particularly good fun, especially now that you can freely explore and interact with the colorful townsfolk.

Nintendo has carried this same level of detail into the remake’s dungeons, where atmospheric fog carpets the stone floors and reacts to Link’s footsteps and sword swings. Dust particles also now hang in the air and glisten as they catch the light, while deeper shadows and improved lighting heighten iconic areas like Kakariko’s nightmarish well and Ganondorf’s Castle.

Even the Hero of Time and his companions have received a graphical glow-up, with hyper-realistic hair and outfits. The most eye-catching is Link’s iconic tunic, which now features woven green straw fibers, intricately designed belts, and enough tassels to have cosplayers curse Nintendo for the level of intricacy in his new, highly detailed drip.

For veteran Zelda players, exploring these well-trodden environments feels like returning to your favorite childhood haunts as an adult. Although each setting has had a significant upgrade, navigating Hyrule and its dungeons feels familiar but fresh. Nintendo has taken so much care recreating its beloved world that it’s hard not to get pulled back into another 20+ hour adventure, gallivanting around Hyrule atop Epona searching for Heart Containers like it’s 1998 all over again.

Just like Breath of the Wild and Tears of the Kingdom, everyone barring Link and minor NPC characters is now fully voiced. While this may be a controversial addition for purists hoping to go back to Zelda’s silent era, the new cutscenes help add more life to the Seven Sages and provide more details that will have lore lovers happy to revisit Hyrule’s history.

Characters like the Goron patriarch Darunia are now way more expressive, while Nintendo has done more for Princess Ruto and Link’s one-sided romance than even the most die-hard fanfiction writer. There’s also a greater sense of emotion behind certain events, like when Link leaves Kokiri Forest thanks to some heartfelt voiced lines from Saria. Then there’s Ganondorf himself, whose grand entrance and signature organ playing are elevated further by some particularly infectious evil laughter that I may or may not have tried to imitate.

Whether you’re a returning player or a newcomer, Ocarina of Time remake is the definitive way to play Link’s legendary adventure. Nintendo has successfully breathed new life into its decades-old world of Hyrule, giving it a loving lick of paint that not only elevates it over the original, but also makes it the best-looking Switch 2 game.

Just like Link’s time-travelling epic, Ocarina of Time remake honors its past and carefully combines new improvements borrowed from the series’ present to give this timeless classic the treatment it deserves. Ocarina of Time remake isn’t just a masterpiece, it’s a timeless adventure everyone needs to play.

#10662. MIT’s tiny flying robot gets 450% faster with AI

Tiny flying robots may one day help rescuers search for people trapped beneath collapsed buildings after earthquakes. Because of their small size, these robotic insects could potentially move through narrow spaces that larger drones cannot enter while avoiding walls, debris, and falling objects.

Until recently, however, aerial microrobots have been far less nimble than the insects that inspired them. They typically moved slowly and followed relatively simple flight paths.

Researchers at MIT have now demonstrated a new approach that gives an insect-scale flying robot much greater speed and agility. The team developed an AI-based controller that allows the robotic bug to perform demanding aerial maneuvers, including repeated body flips.

Using a two-part control system designed to balance performance with computational efficiency, the researchers increased the robot's speed by about 450 percent and its acceleration by about 250 percent compared with their previous best results.

The robot was agile enough to complete 10 consecutive somersaults in 11 seconds, even while wind disturbances tried to knock it away from its intended path.

"We want to be able to use these robots in scenarios that more traditional quadcopter robots would have trouble flying into, but that insects could navigate. Now, with our bioinspired control framework, the flight performance of our robot is comparable to insects in terms of speed, acceleration, and the pitching angle. This is quite an exciting step toward that future goal," says Kevin Chen, an associate professor in the Department of Electrical Engineering and Computer Science (EECS), head of the Soft and Micro Robotics Laboratory within the Research Laboratory of Electronics (RLE), and co-senior author of a paper on the robot.

Chen is joined on the paper by co-lead authors Yi-Hsuan Hsiao, an EECS MIT graduate student; Andrea Tagliabue PhD '24; and Owen Matteson, a graduate student in the Department of Aeronautics and Astronautics (AeroAstro); as well as EECS graduate student Suhan Kim; Tong Zhao MEng '23; and co-senior author Jonathan P. How, the Ford Professor of Engineering in the Department of Aeronautics and Astronautics and a principal investigator in the Laboratory for Information and Decision Systems (LIDS). The research was published in Science Advances.

AI Gives the Robot a Smarter Flight Controller

Chen's group has spent more than five years developing robotic insects.

The team recently created a more durable version of their tiny robot, which is about the size of a microcassette and weighs less than a paperclip. This newer design has larger flapping wings that support more agile flight. The wings are driven by soft artificial muscles that contract rapidly enough to produce extremely fast wingbeats.

The physical design had improved, but the robot's controller remained a major limitation. This controller acts as the robot's "brain," determining where the robot is and deciding how it should move. In earlier versions, a human had to tune the controller by hand.

For the robot to fly with the speed and aggressiveness of a real insect, the researchers needed a system that could handle uncertainty while rapidly solving complex control problems.

A controller powerful enough to do that would normally demand too much computation to operate in real time, especially because the aerodynamics of such a lightweight flying machine are highly complex.

To solve this problem, Chen's group collaborated with How's team to develop a two-step AI-driven control system. The design combines the robustness needed for difficult, high-speed maneuvers with enough computational efficiency to work in real time.

"The hardware advances pushed the controller so there was more we could do on the software side, but at the same time, as the controller developed, there was more they could do with the hardware. As Kevin's team demonstrates new capabilities, we demonstrate that we can utilize them," How says.

Teaching a Tiny Robot to Plan Difficult Maneuvers

The first part of the system uses what is known as a model-predictive controller. This type of controller relies on a dynamic mathematical model to predict how the robot will behave and determine the best sequence of actions for safely following a desired flight path.

Although this method requires substantial computing power, it can plan difficult movements such as aerial flips, sharp turns, and aggressive changes in body angle. The planner also takes into account limits on the amount of force and torque the robot can produce, helping it avoid movements that could lead to a crash.

Repeated flips are particularly challenging. To perform one somersault after another, the robot must slow down in exactly the right way so that it begins each new flip under the proper conditions.

"If small errors creep in, and you try to repeat that flip 10 times with those small errors, the robot will just crash. We need to have robust flight control," How says.

The researchers then used this expert planner to train a "policy" built on a deep-learning model through a process called imitation learning. The policy serves as the robot's real-time decision-making system, determining how and where it should fly.

In effect, imitation learning allows the team to capture the behavior of the more computationally demanding controller in a faster AI model that can operate quickly enough for real-time flight.

A major part of the challenge was generating enough useful training data to teach the policy how to handle aggressive maneuvers without creating an unnecessarily large data set.

"The robust training method is the secret sauce of this technique," How explains.

During flight, the AI-based policy receives information about the robot's position and converts it into real-time commands controlling factors such as thrust force and torques.

Experiments showed that the two-step control system dramatically improved the robot's performance. The insect-scale machine flew 447 percent faster and achieved a 255 percent increase in acceleration.

It also completed 10 somersaults in only 11 seconds while remaining within about 4 or 5 centimeters of its planned flight path.

"This work demonstrates that soft and microrobots, traditionally limited in speed, can now leverage advanced control algorithms to achieve agility approaching that of natural insects and larger robots, opening up new opportunities for multimodal locomotion," says Hsiao.

The team also demonstrated a movement known as a saccade. In insects, this involves pitching the body sharply, rapidly moving to a new position, and then pitching in the opposite direction to stop. This quick acceleration and braking can help insects determine their position and maintain clear vision.

"This bio-mimicking flight behavior could help us in the future when we start putting cameras and sensors on board the robot," Chen says.

One major goal for future research is to equip the microrobots with onboard cameras and sensors. That could eventually allow them to fly outdoors without depending on a sophisticated external motion capture system.

The team also plans to investigate whether onboard sensing could help groups of the robots avoid crashing into one another and coordinate their movements while navigating.

"For the micro-robotics community, I hope this paper signals a paradigm shift by showing that we can develop a new control architecture that is high-performing and efficient at the same time," says Chen.

This research is funded, in part, by the National Science Foundation (NSF), the Office of Naval Research, Air Force Office of Scientific Research, MathWorks, and the Zakhartchenko Fellowship.

#10660. AI fan wikis riddled with errors are muddying the waters of an already contested part of games culture

"These tools have made it very easy for bad actors to crowd out good information..."

Recently, I was writing a piece about Nighthawks and wanted to remind myself of some basic information about the companion characters. As I have a thousand times before, I put the question into Google and clicked the first result: the Nighthawks Wiki.

Of course, wikis aren't necessarily reliable, especially fan-run projects that might not have enough engaged individuals to immediately spot and fix errors. But the "Nighthawks Wiki" that I landed on immediately and obviously had deeper issues. Firstly, it wasn't a wiki at all. Rather than being editable by anybody, this website only invited "corrections" by email. Secondly, it was full of false information that didn't seem likely to have been added by fans getting their facts slightly wrong, like listing companions and skills that don't exist. It also regularly included unrelated information about the 1981 movie and the Edward Hopper painting, both of the same name, and listed them in its "sources" even when they had no relevance to the page in question.

I don't like to accuse anybody of using generative AI, because its hallmarks are difficult to pin down and are often misattributed to the LLM rather than the human writers it rips off – ask any of us how much we enjoy the em-dash, for example. But, interest piqued by the rate and intensity of apparently made up facts, I managed to trace the domain name to a website building service called Spaceship, which also offers an "AI Website builder." (I emailed the address on the "wiki" for comment, but the message bounced.)

A quick search turned up many more similar sites, some of which were actually editable as wikis and others which only called themselves by that name. They typically targeted indie games without gigantic playerbases – the sort of game that people might seek out help with and not find a thriving community ready to provide it.

It's a problem that Tim Bender, CEO for publishing company Hooded Horse, knows well. Unlike most publishers, Hooded Horse creates wikis for their own games, working with fans to make sure they're well-populated with useful information. But lately the company has had to "play whack-a-mole" with AI-generated sites competing for the search results and providing inaccurate information. One of these, for example, appeared for their game Corsair Cove, but was actually part of a more than 20 game scattershot collection operated by a site called GameDB, which the creator admitted to using generative AI to make. "[Generative AI] enables a run of basically commercial enterprises to mass produce and try to flood the search engine results of games to soak up their traffic," says Bender. Attempts to reach GameDB for comment via their public contact email resulted in an error, stating "the remote server is misconfigured".

And the combination of elbowing out legitimate wikis and being full of incorrect information ends up with a lot of frustrated players. "The whole purpose of putting out a wiki is so when [players] search for help, they end up at a site that helps them. And instead we continually hear from players who stumbled up on these things and got confused and are wondering what's going on," says Bender.

That collection of genAI wikis includes one for the 100-player FPS Wardogs; one of at least five that FlamingMojo, a member of the Independent Game Wiki Network, saw appear in the days after the game's release in September. And he says that the frustration the genAI-created sites causes players also impacts those who are trying to do the legitimate work of creating and maintaining these volunteer-led resources: "It's hurt our reputation, as players have been misinformed but we haven't created or updated the relevant page yet so they bounce off [wikis] entirely."

Fanwikis are already a contested part of games culture. Hosting sites like Fandom dominate the space, and have themselves introduced AI features that had to be rolled back due to their inaccuracies and unpopularity. Many groups have decided to leave these hosts, but those who go independent often struggle to show up in Google's search rankings underneath the competition from the umbrella sites.

Whether on a hosting site or independent, fans rarely make any money from the effort they put into their wikis. Either they sacrifice any ad revenue to the bigger platforms, or they pay for their own hosting, which is rarely covered by ads or donations. FlamingMojo, for example, pays for the Dune: Awakening Community Wiki out of his own pocket and doesn't run any ads on it.

But counterintuitively, the low profitability of these kinds of sites only leads to worse outcomes when generative-AI versions are concerned. "There's not that much ad revenue, but there's going to be a little bit and that's all it takes," says Bender. "Because the barrier is so low, why not just spew out a mountain of it and flood the search results?"

It's also possible the generative-AI sites are sometimes aiming for outcomes beyond a little ad revenue. "Some of them, you don't know what data they're collecting," says Bender. In at least one case, a generative-AI site was pointing users to download additional software that offers a paid subscription. The software had been reviewed poorly online and opening the website caused my malware software to kick in and warn me it had been flagged as "potentially unsafe." (None of the sites I attempted to contact for comment responded to my emails.)

Independent wikis also now shoulder a double burden, as generative AI scrapers send huge amounts of traffic their way, potentially affecting site stability. Publicly available internal data from the Dune: Awakening Community Wiki shows 7m requests in a 30 day period from AI crawlers. (5m of these come from Meta alone. While FlamingMojo is able to tame other scrapers using files that direct them to real pages rather than brute-force trying every link, he's found that Meta simply "hammers every page multiple times per second" with no care for "any sensible ratelimits.") These scraping waves hit smaller wikis particularly hard, as their maintainers aren't necessarily expecting huge amounts of traffic and don't have the compute capacity to handle huge spikes in visitors.

"Independent gaming wikis are in a difficult place," says FlamingMojo. As well as competition for SEO, both from corporate sites and now genAI, he says server costs have increased between three- and ten-fold, again due to the impact of genAI. Building a group of people who want to volunteer their time and energy to keep a resource updated isn't easy, although the Dune: Awakening Community Wiki has found success through gamifying contributions, in affiliation with the developer giving out in-game rewards.

And, Bender points out, all of this is happening within a much wider context of genAI's impact on the availability of information. "In a more fundamental way … these tools have made it very easy for bad actors to flood things and crowd out good information which people put dedicated time into. This is just one example of a much broader struggle that, honestly, whatever frustration I feel for this on my end, I'm sure you [journalists] feel at a thousand times that."

But it's possible that, by being one part of the wider issue, fanwiki culture could also hold a sliver of the bigger solution. "The way we survive the AI pollution is with community," says FlamingMojo. "Many people chipping in a small bit, and feeling a sense of shared ownership."

#10658. UK plans to restrict voice chat in games for under-16s

The UK government has confirmed that it intends to put restrictions on in-game voice chat for under-16s in games in the near future, but not everyone is pleased.

In-game voice chat has long been a way for gamers to get one over on their opponents. Even with a rise in third-party apps like Discord and TeamSpeak, players still flock to game chat to mock their enemies.

However, in the UK, that is likely to be restricted for anyone under the age of 16 as part of the under-16s social media ban that will come into effect in the spring.

“We will then bring forward a host of regulations on features and functions, including stranger communications, which is the biggest problem with Roblox, and some of the other issues that hon. Members have raised,” Ian Murray, the UK Government’s Online Safety Minister, told a debate on November 2.

“That will allow gamers to continue to have the functionality of stranger pairing, but without the ability to talk to strangers in a gaming context. That should resolve some of these issues.”

As per Gamer’s Voice, the new proposal would “require all gamers to prove their age in order to access communication features,” similar to how some nations deal with in-game gambling features.

“Gaming isn’t simply another form of social media. When young people join a gaming guild, work together in a team or make friends through a shared hobby, they are participating in a social activity that can be enormously positive,” Tom Shannon, spokesperson for Gamer’s Voice, said.

Canada could restrict Roblox & Minecraft for under 16s as part of social media ban

Roblox responds to UK social media ban for teenagers with new safety features

UK could ban kids from talking to strangers on Roblox, Fortnite and Minecraft

These plans have been in the offing since May, with games like Roblox already rolling out new safety features ahead of the crackdown.

The UK’s social media ban is similar to the rules implemented by Australia – which was the first country to issue such a ban – with under-16s having restricted access to several high-profile social media platforms.

As noted, age verification systems already exist in different games, as well as on different platforms, but people have found ways around them. That includes scanning Death Stranding’s Sam Porter Bridges’ face instead of their own, seeing as the character looks so realistic.
