#10657. Quantum computing’s “dark horse” just proved it can go universal

A practical quantum computer must eventually be able to handle any type of quantum algorithm, much like a conventional laptop can run many different kinds of software. Researchers have now demonstrated a new way to reach that level of flexibility using unusual quantum objects known as non-Abelian anyons.

Scientists from the University of Chicago Pritzker School of Molecular Engineering (UChicago PME), Harvard, Stony Brook University, and Quantinuum created and tested a full set of operations based on non-Abelian anyons. Their results provide the first experimental demonstration that this approach can support the broad range of operations required for universal quantum computing.

"We demonstrated a so-called universal gate set -- meaning that if you store information in these emergent versions of quarks, and you move them around, you can do any quantum computation you might want to do," said Ruben Verresen, assistant professor of molecular engineering at UChicago PME and a co-author of the new study published in Nature.

A Possible Shortcut Around Costly Quantum Error Correction

The strategy could do more than help create a general-purpose quantum computer. It may also offer a more efficient route toward reliable quantum machines.

Quantum computers are extremely vulnerable to errors, so researchers typically protect information by distributing it across many physical qubits. These error correction methods can preserve data, but they usually do not provide every operation needed to perform universal quantum computation on that protected information.

To fill the gap, engineers often rely on specially prepared resources called "magic states." Producing them usually requires an intensive purification process known as distillation, which can consume a large fraction of a quantum computer's available qubits. The new results suggest that non-Abelian anyons may offer a way around that expensive step.

"Non-Abelian codes are a dark horse in the race to quantum error correction," said Henrik Dreyer, managing director and scientific lead at Quantinuum's Munich office and a co-author of the study. "In this work we show the first universal gate set in a non-Abelian code, which demonstrates that fault-tolerant computations can in principle be done without resorting to magic state distillation or cultivation, which are the most expensive operations in standard quantum error correction codes."

Ordinary qubits encode information using two basic states, along with quantum combinations of those states. Non-Abelian anyons work in a fundamentally different way.

These anyons do not appear as ordinary standalone particles in nature. Instead, scientists create them inside quantum circuits by entangling many conventional qubits into a collective state that behaves as though it were a new type of particle with its own unusual rules.

"The way I think about these codes is they're creating little universes -- alternative universes, but ones that reflect some of the properties of our own," Verresen said.

Each non-Abelian anyon carries an internal state that changes when one anyon is moved around another in a process known as braiding. The sequence of those braiding operations matters (that's what non-Abelian means), allowing information to be encoded and manipulated in ways that are unavailable to ordinary particles.

Because the information is spread across many entangled qubits rather than stored in a single location, it can also be naturally protected from some of the small disturbances that frequently disrupt conventional qubits. Braiding the anyons can also perform computational operations.

In 2024, a research team that included Verresen used a Quantinuum trapped-ion computer to create anyons associated with a symmetry group called D4 -- the rotations and reflections that leave a square unchanged -- demonstrating this form of non-Abelian order on quantum hardware for the first time.

That experiment showed that the unusual particles could be created and manipulated, but braiding them alone was not sufficient to carry out every operation required for universal quantum computing.

"In that work, we didn't demonstrate that those emergent forces were enough to do quantum computation," said Verresen. "That particular universe we created was not powerful enough."

Fusion Unlocks Universal Quantum Operations

For the new study, the researchers switched to another symmetry known as S3 -- the rotations and mirror-image flips that leave an equilateral triangle unchanged -- and created the corresponding anyons on Quantinuum's H2 trapped-ion processor using 54 entangled qubits.

The S3 system had the properties needed for universal quantum computation, but only when braiding was combined with another operation called fusion. During fusion, two anyons are brought together and the resulting state is measured.

The underlying concept was proposed theoretically in 2003 by Carlos Mochon, then a student of John Preskill at Caltech. Turning that theoretical idea into an experiment that could actually run on quantum hardware, however, required extensive additional theoretical and experimental work.

The researchers used pairs of anyons to encode "topological qutrits" which store three possible levels of quantum information instead of the two levels used by ordinary qubits.

By combining different braiding and fusion operations, the team demonstrated three key tools: one entangling gate produced through braiding and two different measurements created through fusion. Together, those operations can in principle produce any quantum operation, including operations that braiding alone could not achieve.

Beyond their possible computing applications, these unusual quantum states could also help researchers investigate fundamental aspects of physics.

"It is gratifying to see ideas we have spent our PhD work thinking about realized in the lab, and it has been made possible by remarkable advances in quantum hardware over the past few years," said Anasuya Lyons and Chiu Fan Bowen Lo, graduate students at Harvard University in the group of Ashvin Vishwanath who helped lead the work.

The researchers also demonstrated that non-Abelian anyons could directly produce a magic state using topological operations -- sidestepping the costly distillation process used in most quantum systems.

The experiment did not yet include active error correction. Instead, the researchers focused on testing the individual building blocks of the method and confirming that they could create a magic state consistent with theoretical expectations.

"So far, we've ignored the question of error correction. Here, it's more like a proof of principle," Verresen said.

The next major step will be combining these operations with active error correction. If that can be achieved, non-Abelian anyons could eventually become a practical basis for large-scale, fault-tolerant quantum computers. Verresen is already working with other PME researchers on new techniques for stabilizing non-Abelian quantum memories.

Materials provided by University of Chicago. Original written by Sarah C.P. Williams. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10656. Best AI Recruiting Agents in 2026: 6 Compared

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Short answer: Pin (pin.com) covers sourcing through scheduling with approvals you control at every step. Pin says it searches 850M+ profiles and assists recruiters with ranking, outreach, follow-up, scheduling and ATS updates. You approve each step (Assist) or delegate the sequence (Autopilot), and Pin logs every action. Closing and offers stay human.

Be skeptical. Gartner estimated in June 2025 that only about 130 of the thousands of vendors marketing "agentic AI" are real. Test any product on a live role before you pay.

An AI recruiting agent is software that takes a hiring goal, such as "three senior backend engineers in Austin," and works through several steps toward it on its own: finding candidates, ranking them, contacting them, following up and booking interviews. A recruiter sets the brief and the limits and makes the final decisions. The agent assists the recruiter; it does not replace one. See Pin's guide to AI recruiting agents.

It differs from three things sold under the same name:

A quick test: if you stop prompting it, does the search keep moving? If not, it is a tool.

Demand is rising: in Korn Ferry's October 2025 survey of 1,674 talent leaders, 52% plan to add autonomous agents to their teams in 2026.

Compliance. The employer carries the legal duty. New York City's Local Law 144 requires a bias audit within one year before an automated employment decision tool is used, a public summary of results, and candidate notice at least 10 business days before use. The EU AI Act's Annex III classes AI that filters applications or evaluates candidates as high-risk, and the Commission's timeline puts those obligations at December 2, 2027. Ask each vendor whether its ranking is covered, whether a bias audit exists and what logs it keeps. Pin feeds no names, gender or protected characteristics to its AI, uses third-party fairness audits, and lists an Algorithmic Fairness Report (August 2026) in its trust center on request. This is not legal advice.

Pin (pin.com) is an AI recruiting platform with a built-in agent that assists recruiters across the top of the recruiting funnel, from sourcing to interview scheduling.

Sourcing. Pin says it searches 850M+ profiles across multiple sources, including North America and Europe, drawn from professional networks, GitHub, Stack Overflow, open-source contributions, patents, academic publications and the broader web. Start from a plain-language brief, a pasted job description, uploaded ideal résumés or an ATS import. Results arrive as a ranked shortlist, each candidate with a fit score and an AI-written reason. Pin asks clarifying questions about the brief, such as "Is remote considered?" Learn more about AI candidate sourcing.

Assist and Autopilot. In Assist, you delegate tasks one at a time and approve each step. In Autopilot, the agent takes the whole sequence.

In Autopilot, only the email steps send automatically. Pin queues each LinkedIn and SMS step as a task with an AI-drafted message, and the recruiter sends it.

Visibility and learning. Every action appears in a feed, and the agent runs 24/7. Pin's agent page describes an audit log with reasoning, a pause control, approval thresholds for sensitive cases and exclusion lists for past contacts and current employees. The model learns from accept and reject decisions across your team.

Outreach and scheduling. Pin drafts each message from the candidate's profile signals, calibrated to your writing voice. Sequences are multi-channel across email, LinkedIn and SMS: automated email steps plus AI-drafted LinkedIn and SMS tasks in the same sequence. Any reply pauses the sequence, and every channel stays in one thread in a shared team inbox. The Free plan is email-only. Interview scheduling covers time zones, hiring panels and reschedules, with confirmations in your ATS. Teams without an ATS can track candidates in Pin's Kanban pipeline from first touch to signed offer, and teams with one sync through 120+ integrations, including Greenhouse, Lever and Ashby.

MCP server. The Pin MCP server lets an assistant such as Claude or ChatGPT work a Pin job in plain language through ten tools. Accepting a candidate starts outreach from your configured sequence, which Pin confirms every time. It steers Pin from your assistant and is not a separate agent.

Pricing (checked October 14, 2026, on the pricing page): Free is $0 with 1 AI agent, 5 contact lookup credits a week included and email-only sequences. Solo is $99 per month for one seat, billed annually. Professional is $135 per user per month billed annually, or $179 month-to-month. Business is custom and includes SSO/SAML, SCIM and premium ATS integrations. Pin holds SOC 2 Type 2, encrypts data at rest and in transit, and is GDPR and CCPA ready.

Results. According to Pin's 2026 user survey across 2,000+ organizations and 20,000+ users, Pin saves each recruiter 12 hours per week on sourcing and outreach (vendor-reported). Pin is rated 4.8/5 on G2. Josh Volinsky, Co-Founder of Synergy Search, says: "Pin makes sourcing candidates so natural and I love how you can just talk to it like it's a recruiter on your team and it goes hunting."

Where Pin is not the right fit. Teams that only need high-volume screening of inbound applicants are better served by a tool like Paradox.

Best for: recruiting teams, agencies and founders who want one agent across the top of the funnel, with approvals they control.

GoPerfect's Perfect is an AI recruiter that researches a role, sources and contacts candidates, and can run the first phone screen.

It costs $300 per open position per month with unlimited seats, so five open roles run $1,500 a month. No free trial is listed. Its pricing page (October 2026) lists autopilot sourcing, email and LinkedIn outreach, AI phone calls, AI first interviews, 60+ ATS syncs, SOC 2 Type II, approval mode, do-not-contact lists and an audit log. Its site also shows a "Send without approval" option, so control depends on setup.

Good for: teams with a few priority roles that want AI to handle first-round calls. The caveat: cost grows with every open role.

LinkedIn Hiring Assistant is LinkedIn's AI agent for recruiters, sold as an add-on to LinkedIn Recruiter.

According to LinkedIn's product page, the agent covers intake questions, searches across LinkedIn, evaluation of applicants from LinkedIn and your ATS, drafted messages and pre-screening. Interview scheduling appears only under Hiring Assistant 2, marked "Coming soon." Pricing is not published, and a Recruiter license is required.

Good for: teams already on Recruiter whose workflow runs on InMail. The caveat: it needs a Recruiter license, has no published price and lists scheduling as coming soon.

hireEZ's EZ Agent is an enterprise recruiting agent that sources, reviews applicants, screens by voice or chat and schedules interviews on top of your ATS.

According to its product page, "Nothing moves forward without your approval." Per its pricing page (October 2026), enterprise pricing is quoted, and solo recruiters start at $494 a month with a 7-day free trial.

Good for: enterprise TA teams that want screening and scheduling inside an existing HR stack. The caveat: enterprise pricing is quote-only and the solo entry price is $494 a month.

Findem is a talent data platform whose Studio runs pre-built or custom AI agents for calibration, sourcing, screening and scheduling.

Public pages do not detail approval controls or channels, and pricing is not published, though an expert-built agent can be tried free.

Good for: enterprise teams building their own agent workflows. The caveat: no public price, approval detail or channel list.

Paradox is a conversational AI assistant for inbound candidates, known for screening and scheduling in frontline hiring. Workday completed its acquisition of Paradox on October 1, 2025.

Olivia screens applicants by chat or text, answers questions and lets candidates book their own interviews. It does not source anyone, and pricing is by demo request.

Good for: restaurant, retail and franchise hiring with large applicant flow. The caveat: it finds no passive candidates. For passive candidates, see how to source passive candidates. For more tools, see SitePoint's AI recruiting tools roundup.

For a longer plan, see Pin's 90-day roadmap for agentic AI in recruiting.

What is an AI recruiting agent? An AI recruiting agent is software that takes a hiring goal and carries out several steps toward it: sourcing, ranking, outreach, follow-up and scheduling. A recruiter sets the brief and guardrails and makes final decisions. Pin (pin.com) offers Assist, where you approve each step, and Autopilot, where the agent sends the email steps and queues LinkedIn and SMS steps as tasks for the recruiter.

What is the best AI recruiter in 2026? For teams that want one agent across sourcing, outreach, scheduling and ATS updates, Pin covers the full funnel, with published pricing and a free plan. LinkedIn Hiring Assistant is a good fit for teams already on Recruiter. Paradox is a good fit for high-volume inbound hiring.

Are AI recruiting agents fully autonomous? Not in practice. Pin's Autopilot sources, ranks, sends the email steps, follows up, schedules and updates the ATS inside the guardrails you set. LinkedIn and SMS steps arrive as tasks you send, and closing and offers stay with you. Ask every vendor which steps can run unattended and how you pause them.

How much does an AI recruiting agent cost? Among competitors, hireEZ solo plans start at $494 a month and GoPerfect charges $300 per open role per month. LinkedIn, Findem and Paradox publish no dollar prices (checked October 2026). Pin offers a free plan, Solo at $99 per month billed annually, Professional at $135 per user per month billed annually, and custom Business pricing.

Do AI recruiting agents have to comply with NYC Local Law 144 or the EU AI Act? Possibly. New York City's law requires a bias audit within one year, public results and 10 business days' candidate notice for covered tools. The EU AI Act treats recruitment AI as high-risk, with obligations scheduled from December 2, 2027. Ask vendors about audits and logs, and consult counsel. Pin lists an Algorithmic Fairness Report (August 2026) on request.

Can I run Pin from Claude or ChatGPT? Yes. The Pin MCP server connects assistants such as Claude and ChatGPT to your Pin workspace through ten tools for setting up jobs, reviewing candidates and tuning searches. It uses OAuth sign-in and is included on paid Pin plans.

Judge each agent on which steps it runs, how much approval you control and whether the price is public. For teams that want sourcing through scheduling with approvals they control, Pin fits that brief: it assists recruiters from sourcing through scheduling, offers Assist and Autopilot, logs every action and publishes its pricing. LinkedIn Hiring Assistant is a good fit if you live in Recruiter, and Paradox if inbound volume is your pain.

#10654. Revisiting Ecstatica - the pioneering survival horror that used its fancy ellipsoid graphics tech to fill its world with dongs

There's a well-known phenomena in video games development known as time-to-penis. It's a somewhat tongue-in-cheek measurement of the time players, when given an exciting suite of new customisation tools, will take to create the first inevitable in-game dong. Ecstatica sort of implies it works the other way round too. That developers - when giddy with a new tech toy - can't always help themselves either. But maybe I'm getting a little ahead of myself.

Ecstatica, developed by Andrew Spencer Studios and published by the long-defunct Psygnosis, originally released back in 1994. It follows a bumbling traveller who unwittingly becomes embroiled in the fate of a demon-cursed medieval village after making a rest stop in search of water. It was heavily inspired by survival horror game granddaddy Alone in the Dark (and arrived a full year before Resident Evil), but while it adopted a similar fixed-camera approach to its adventure, it had some tricks all of its own.

Most immediately notable was the decision to build its pre-rendered backdrops and characters out of ellipsoids; giving the medieval village of Tirih and its surrounding environs a softer, more organic feel compared to the angular lines afforded by the limited polygons of the time. And even today its bold, vibrant, and decidedly spherical aesthetic is quite unlike anything else.

Ecstatica isn't, I should say, new to me. I first played it back on its original release. And it's a game that never quite left my mind; in large part, I'll admit, because of the relative proliferation of dick, boob, and bulge. Faced with the infinite potential ellipsoids could offer, Andrew Spencer Studios' urge to penis was perhaps inevitable, and, lo, the moment our tights-wearing hero dismounts his horses in the opening cinematic, neither his bulbous front bauble or two–giant-eggs-in-a-hanky posterior leave anything to the imagination. And if you opt for the alternative lady hero, well, she's got ample going on there too.

And so it continues: prancing fawns with mega-breasts, muscle gimps with proud chipolatas. Ecstatica really is a singular sort of horror game. But while the cheerfully perky genitalia is undoubtedly what my teenage brain chose to squirrel away in the memory vaults, I also had distant recollections of a game that was intriguing for other, more interesting reasons. So when SNEG - a publisher founded by GOG veterans and focused on reviving lost classics - announced it was re-releasing Ecstatica and its sequel in time for Halloween, I knew I had to go back.

And you know what? After spending a few happy hours revisiting Ecstatica these past few nights, I kind of feel bad I'd mostly condensed it down to its naughty bits (for starters, the pickles, plums, and melons aren't quite as frequent as I remember them). This really is a fascinating game, with a wonderfully, uniquely odd mood - playing something like a bawdy fairytale as it blends daft slapstick silliness with an effectively sinister undertone. This is a game where our hero will stop for an unprovoked pee in a bush or, after being turned into a frog, must seek a kiss from the busty woman who lives down the road. The surprisingly great voice acting, while scant, is pure British whimsy, but throughout the village: demonic destruction, gory evisceration, and crucifixion. And Ecstatica walks a tonal tightrope that somehow works.

It still looks fantastic in its own strange way too; its broad-daylight horrors are vibrantly hued, everything is exuberantly animated, and there's so much characterful incidental detail. As you roam from one jauntily angled screen to the next, searching for objects you'll need to progress - a bible dropped by a hanged priest you can read aloud at the monastery gates, or a flying broomstick you'll need to navigate a boobytrapped dungeon - you'll encounter ale-swigging bears, perpetually annoyed imps with tiny pitchforks, and peeved minotaurs. You'll trip on rocks and inelegantly face-plant; you'll smack your bonce on low ceilings; you'll excitedly put on a suit of armour only to discover it slows you to a comically useless crawl. And you can even kick a priest off the belltower if you're feeling particularly mean.

It's not your usual sort of survival horror, then, but it's got one hell of a trick up its sleeve: recurring enemies that'll readily give chase, notably including an unkillable werewolf that's essentially a precursor to the stalkers - think Resident Evil's Mr. X - beloved by modern-day horror games. You'll catch him peeking around corners as the camera switches to a new angle, he'll launch a surprise attack from the rooftops, or suddenly give chase if you disturb his murdering. And when that happens, he's relentless - the only way you'll lose him is to inflict enough damage he'll give momentary pause, or hide in a cupboard until he moves on. There's even a little stealth. It's rudimentary stuff by today's standards, not helped by the unwieldy combat and tank controls, but it still manages to muster a whiff of tension. And it's remarkable to rediscover something so pioneering in a title I'd mentally reduced to 'the willy game' - particularly when horror's better-known 3D stalker series, like Clock Tower, were yet to come.

Granted, Ecstatica has enough old-school jank that it's not an easy recommendation for modern players; from its wayward combat to its untelegraphed (though pretty amusing) sudden deaths. Or the inventory system, which demands you drop anything your two hands can't hold right there on the floor - so you better remember where you left it. And you're screwed if you accidentally place something in tall grass or just outside the fixed camera frame. SNEG's remaster, though, is decent, and an enjoyable way to experience this cult-classic curio.

And 'curio' is perhaps the best word to describe Ecstatica, with its pioneering survival horror spirit, its ellipsoids, and its unashamed love of slapstick, boobs, and dong. It's been an unexpected pleasure revisiting it some 30 years later, and I'm excited I'll soon have my very first brush with Ecstatica 2. All I know about the 1997 sequel is its hero isn't blessed with a prominent bauble from the off, so its time-to-penis is already looking like a longer one.

#10653. This light-powered AI can spot deepfakes with nearly 98% accuracy

Researchers at the University of California, Los Angeles (UCLA) have created a new optical-neural processor that uses light to help identify deepfake videos quickly and accurately. Unlike conventional systems that typically examine videos one after another using digital hardware, the UCLA technology can analyze 15 or more video streams at the same time.

The key difference is that part of the detection process takes place through the physical propagation of light. This allows many videos to be evaluated simultaneously during a single optical pass rather than requiring each one to move separately through a conventional digital processing pipeline.

The technology is detailed in the study "Scalable, Energy-Efficient Optical-Neural Architecture for Multiplexed Deepfake Video Detection," published in eLight. The researchers designed the optical AI system to serve as a high-throughput, attack-resilient first layer of defense for screening large amounts of manipulated and AI-generated video.

The Growing Challenge of Deepfake Detection

Rapid improvements in generative AI have made synthetic videos increasingly realistic, increasing the need for detection systems that are both accurate and capable of operating at large scale.

Many advanced deepfake detectors depend on enormous amounts of digital computation. A single analysis can require hundreds of billions of floating-point operations, and videos are often processed sequentially. As more content must be checked, both the processing time and energy requirements can rise proportionally.

Digital detection systems face another problem. Attackers can deliberately alter fake videos in subtle ways designed to confuse a detector and make manipulated footage appear authentic.

Professor Aydogan Ozcan and his UCLA team developed a hybrid digital-optical system intended to address both challenges.

A lightweight digital encoder first collects compact information about each video, including spatial, spectral, and temporal features. That information is transformed into a phase pattern and displayed on a programmable spatial light modulator.

The resulting optical wavefront then travels through a free-space-based, passive optical decoder. At the other end, paired optical detectors directly produce an authenticity score for each video.

In effect, the system replaces a computationally demanding digital decoding network with a physical process that can handle many streams in parallel.

Nearly 98% Accuracy Across 15 Videos at Once

In experiments using visible light, the processor examined 15 Celeb-DF videos simultaneously during each optical pass.

It achieved an average detection accuracy of 97.79%, along with a sensitivity of 99.86% and a specificity of 95.72%. Sensitivity measures how successfully the system identifies manipulated videos, making the particularly high sensitivity important for a screening tool designed to keep fake content from slipping through.

The 99.86% sensitivity translated to an average false-negative rate of ~0.14%. In other words, only a very small fraction of manipulated videos were incorrectly classified as authentic.

The researchers also pushed the system further by increasing its capacity to 18 videos in a single optical pass. Even at that level, average detection accuracy remained at 96.13%.

The team found that the processor could also become more capable by increasing the physical depth of its passive optical decoder without substantially increasing energy use or inference latency.

When researchers added two optimized passive diffractive layers while testing more difficult deepfake manipulations, detection accuracy improved by ~6.8%.

These phase-only diffractive layers can be manufactured as passive, static optical structures/surfaces. They perform additional calculations through the diffraction of light, meaning they do not require additional electrical power while the system is performing an inference.

That approach could allow more sophisticated processing without the same energy costs that typically accompany larger digital neural networks.

Testing the System Against Google VEO-3 Videos

The researchers did not limit their experiments to conventional face swapping deepfakes. They also challenged the processor with videos produced using Google's VEO-3 model.

Newer generative AI systems can create footage that lacks many of the obvious artifacts associated with earlier deepfake technology, making them a more difficult target for detectors trained primarily on older forms of manipulation.

With only minimal fine-tuning, the optical processor achieved 94.80% accuracy and 97.61% sensitivity on previously unseen VEO-3 videos during experiments.

The results suggest that the approach could potentially adapt as generative AI technology continues to evolve.

A Deepfake Detector Designed to Be Harder to Fool

The optical system also showed resistance to black-box adversarial attacks and provides inherent protection against white-box attacks.

Part of that security comes from the way the detector physically performs its calculations. Because some of the inference process takes place through diffraction, important parameters of the optical model are effectively embedded within the hardware.

Those parameters can be difficult for an attacker to measure, reproduce, or reverse engineer. That makes reconstructing the detector and designing carefully tailored adversarial changes that can evade it considerably more difficult.

The processor also continued working reliably when videos were affected by image noise, blur, JPEG compression, and experimental misalignments. According to the researchers, those results illustrate how optical computation could provide a more secure and dependable foundation for some artificial intelligence systems.

A First Line of Defense Against Deepfakes

Rather than replacing sophisticated digital detectors entirely, the UCLA processor is designed to work as a highly sensitive first stage of a larger detection system.

Massive volumes of video could initially pass through the parallel optical processor. Content identified as suspicious could then be sent to more computationally demanding digital models for a more detailed final assessment.

Such a system could combine the strengths of both approaches. Optical processing could provide parallel operation, low decoder energy requirements, high sensitivity, resistance to adversarial attacks, and the ability to adapt to newer AI video generators, while conventional digital systems could provide deeper analysis when necessary.

The researchers say this combination could eventually be useful for large-scale content moderation, media authentication, surveillance, and other security-critical AI applications.

The authors of this work are Parnian Ghapandar Kashani and Dr. Shiqi Chen, who contributed equally, and Professor Aydogan Ozcan. The researchers are affiliated with the UCLA Electrical and Computer Engineering Department, the UCLA Bioengineering Department, and the California NanoSystems Institute.

Materials provided by Light Publishing Center, Changchun Institute of Optics, Fine Mechanics And Physics, CAS. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10651. Pastor goes viral after giving sermon in $4,000 massage chair

A pastor has gone viral after delivering part of his Sunday sermon from a $4,000 massage chair, reclining and enjoying a massage while preaching to his congregation.

Michael Todd, the lead pastor of Transformation Church in Tulsa, Oklahoma, is known for his unconventional approach to sermons, often using unusual props to illustrate his messages.

But his latest service on November 1 has attracted attention online after he wheeled out a luxury massage chair and spent several minutes relaxing in it.

During his sermon, Todd climbed into an Osaki Champ II massage chair, which sells for around $3,999. But it wasn’t just for his own comfort, it was used to make a point.

“I would tell you that righteousness is right standing with God. But most believers are in what I call right sitting with God,” he told the congregation as he sat down. “You are living for Christ in a La-Z-Boy.”

He then reclined and activated the chair’s massage functions before continuing to preach about those who are only faithful when it’s easy.

“I’m only doing it if it’s comfortable,” he said. “I’m only doing it if it does not require me to move out of my spot.”

He then used the chair to explain how prioritizing comfort could prevent people from helping others.

“How good could I fight from this position?” he asked. “How good could I protect myself? How good could I actually help somebody who was in need right there?”

“Right standing with God means that I have to stop letting my life of righteousness be reclined.”

TikToker creates viral touring event where tall people can finally feel “normal”

TikToker who claimed to have “magical powers” jailed for scamming victims out of $10K

The pastor eventually left the chair, using the demonstration to encourage his congregation to become more active in their faith. Clips of the unusual sermon have since circulated on social media, attracting millions of views.

This isn’t the first time a pastor has thought outside the box. Earlier this year, another went viral for promising to make someone a “multi-millionaire” if they donate $10,000.

#10650. Inside Buckshot Roulette developer Mike Klubnika's terrifically nasty horror universe of post-Soviet malaise

In Mike Klubnika's growing oeuvre of brilliantly deplorable games, the same elements tend to recur. First, smoking: deep, long drags to make the lungs burn and eyes water. Second, club music: hard and fast, accented with just a faint sense of melancholy. Third, guns: big and loud, usually aimed at you from point-blank range, ready to blow your brains out.

Across three excellent games in almost as many years - 2023's Buckshot Roulette, 2025's S.p.l.i.t, and 2026's Machine Party - Klubnika has remixed each of these elements with design verve and stylistic aplomb. Buckshot Roulette is a gruesome game of chance delivered at the business end of a pump-action shotgun. S.p.l.i.t. is a Matrix-esque hacking game with the gnarliest, most harrowing ending I've played in recent years. Machine Party, meanwhile, delivers a seedy, hyper-violent take on Mario Party: funny, yes; brutal, too.

But each of these games also contains further tell-tale signs of the expanding Klubnikaverse: grubby low-poly graphics whose every surface seems to be coated in some kind of unpleasant residue; bleak settings that hint at a shared near-future authoritarian setting. These games are short, too, lasting just an hour apiece. But that means they land even harder - thwack - like a pistol whip to the face.

There is a "shared vibe," Klubnika tells me over a video call. "It's like, if you're someone living in this type of universe then you probably don't have what you want most of the time."

Klubnika, a 24-year old university drop-out from the tucked-away enclave of Tallinn, Estonia, describes more specific emotions that he intends his games to arouse: "isolation", "desperation", "risk", "worry." It's hardly crowd-pleasing stuff, but that hasn't stopped his three most recent titles enjoying massive commercial success. Buckshot Roulette has sold 10 million copies. S.p.l.i.t has shifted 200,000. Machine Party currently sits at 1.5 million.

Like other young creatives working in and around horror - notably Backrooms director Kane Parsons and Obsession director Curry Barker - Klubnika was a child of the computer. He was part of a "tech-savvy" friendship group in Tallinn: some made YouTube videos, others were self-taught programmers. Early on, Klubnika made 3D art in Blender while messing about with sound design and video editing. All of them played video games.

Klubnika's desktop PC, then, was both a creative instrument and portal into exotic worlds and taboo-feeling media: Klubnika remembers pirating the edgy top-down shooter Hotline Miami when he was 10 or 11 years old before discovering the music of Aphex Twin in one particularly strange level of the Half-Life multiplayer mod Sven Co-op.

When Klubnika wasn't glued to his screen, he was hitting house parties in Tallinn. But then it was back to the computer with a view to pushing his 3D art skills towards architectural visualisations. However, Klubnika grew bored because "archviz is very technical," he says. "I started making art instead." Klubnika applied to the world-renowned Estonian Academy of Arts, though his application was unsuccessful (clearly, the admissions staff didn't appreciate the young artist's eye for the macabre). Thereafter, he began studying English Language, though he dropped out after a single term because Buckshot Roulette was blowing up.

Ever since, Klubnika seems to have possessed a knack for unlocking Steam's inscrutable algorithm - of winning the wishlist war and riding high on the recommendations tab. This success did not occur overnight: Klubnika self-published 19 small games prior to this moment, five of which are collected on 2023's Unsorted Horror. "I got into game development with the idea it's probably not going to work out as a career," he says. "I did it for the love of the game."

All games are born from specific circumstances: a forest that naturally invites a game of hide and seek; the military-funded MIT computer labs that birthed nascent video games in the 60s. Klubnika's games are no different: first and foremost, their greyscale atmospheres are inspired by the huge, concrete buildings of Tallinn's Soviet era (which offer a stark counterpoint to the city's more straightforwardly picturesque medieval architecture). More specifically, Klubnika, like all young men in Estonia, was required to complete mandatory military service.

He trained in communications, which meant he spent a lot of time fiddling with radios. "I had a pretty easy time," he says, comparing the more technical requirements of his role to the physicality demanded of a machine gunner. "But it was still very demanding," he stresses. Especially challenging was spending large amounts of time away from home: he was stationed three hours away from Tallinn and needed a permit to return.

"There's something a little bit weird about this type of bureaucracy which doesn't feel human to me," says Klubnika. Rather than freely cavorting about Tallinn, he found himself incorporated into a stringently controlled military system. "My games take those ideas to the extreme," he says. "The people in them are very small small cogs in the machine."

It's easy to imagine the protagonist of S.p.l.i.t. toiling away in front of a large, old-fashioned computer as something of a mirror to the radio-operating Klubnika during his military service. But the events of S.p.l.i.t. are no mere training exercise. One mistake and everyone dies, including the player. The game's ending, which involves a devilish triptych of life-ending options - a noose, power drill, and screwdriver - makes this point in awful, hilarious fashion, like a cross between Pi and Eraserhead. It took my breath away and also left me feeling queasy - a testament to Klubnika's commitment to stacking one grim punchline on top of another.

But the Estonian game maker's military service days also yielded moments of levity. He enjoyed smoking with his fellow trainees; a "great ritual," he says, and a means of "decompression" (it's also a pastime Klubnika loves seeing in other media - video games like Arctic Eggs and TV shows such as True Detective). That explains one of his major motifs, then.

Klubnika's eyes also light up at the mention of firearms in the military. "Yeah, we had firearms and shooting drills and stuff," he says, casually noting the "super cool" feeling of firing an AR-15 assault rifle and the "sheer destruction" of a hand grenade. That, then, seems to explain Klubnika's fondness for including ballistic, brain-splattering weaponry in his games. He recalls one explosive training exercise, in particular: "You start off in a bunker and then you go out and you do the exercise. Then you go into a different bunker, and the entire unit goes through. So you're sitting there with a bunch of people that are, like, 20-years-old, and they're all holding high-explosive grenades. It's like, ‘This is crazy bro. Surely nothing will go wrong.'"

The anecdote seems to speak to a central sensibility of Klubnika's gritty games: a foregrounding of violent power that finds no contradiction in the powerlessness of those who wield it. Discussing Machine Party, Klubnika describes the contrapuntal nature of his work in simpler terms. "The idea was to have this fun party game, but it's industrial horror - and it's also kind of sad," he says. At one memorable moment, players get to enjoy respite from the otherwise murderous experience, running across a field of golden wheat, as if fleetingly inhabiting a scene from Terence Malick's 1978 movie, Days of Heaven. Klubnika's right: it is sad - a better life is dangled in front of these people, and then cruelly snatched away.

Barring any kind of major geopolitical rupture involving Estonia, neighbouring Russia, and Europe, Klubnika plans to keep making precisely the type of games that has brought him such success. He has toyed with the idea of scaling up but decided against it for fear of becoming an "administrator" - a suit who delegates the creative process rather than actually does it. "If I end up doing that then a large part of the reason why I make games is gone," he says.

The next game, reveals Klubnika, should harmonise two aspects of his work: the replayable arcade mechanics of Machine Party and Buckshot Roulette, and the single-player story focus of S.p.l.i.t. "I want to find a balance between the two styles," he says.

Regardless of its ultimate form, Klubnika will make it in a staunchly DIY manner: he will laboriously hand-paint each grotty surface, stretch every texture into a warped shadow of itself, and smash together 3D models of varying fidelity into a single scene. In the Klubnikaverse, a nightmarish fun-house distortion of the real world, these kinds of ostensible visual flaws deepen immersiveness rather than interrupt it. This is a fast, dirty way of making video games - and it suits Klubnika's nasty fiction down to its trash-strewn ground.

Nintendo Switch, PC, PS4, PS5, Xbox Series X/S

#10648. Dawn of War 4 may be the most generous RTS I've played

Maybe the best way to understand the kind of game Dawn of War 4 wants to be is through a single button. If you're playing a campaign mission in DoW 4 and you're not really feeling it, or you made a silly mistake right at the end and got summarily wiped by the AI, or maybe you're in a hurry and just really curious about what happens next in the story, you have a quietly revolutionary option to hand: you can just hit the 'skip mission' button, and move on.

Dawn of War 4's game director, Jan Theysen explained the thinking to me like this: "In the end, we feel it's not that we want the player to play, necessarily, the game we envision for them. It's more like: here is the thing you can play with, and have fun."

That line of thought seems to permeate Dawn of War 4 in all kinds of places, and in turn makes it come across as incredibly generous. My most recent time with the game, for instance, focused on the campaign and the skirmish mode. The campaign alone is massive. It has four sub campaigns within it, one for each faction, their stories linking to form one grand narrative surrounding the planet of Kronus - returning here from the original Dawn of War's cult favourite expansion, Dark Crusade. Any one of those four campaigns is, according to developer King Art Games, longer than the entire original Dawn of War campaign. And within each there's at least one fork in the road, where you make a choice leading you down one of two mission paths. In other words, each campaign is really almost two campaigns in one.

The two I've played - right the way to completion, making that about half the total game's campaign already - are those of the time-honoured kunnin' Orks, and the shiny new techno-Catholics of the Adeptus Mechanicus (Admech, to the 40k nerds). To pick one example of how these can split, the Orks' campaign initially focuses on Guzkutta - a local Kronus upstart with suitably big ambitions - and you're given the choice of sticking with him or switching to the glorious, returning warboss Gorgutz, who may or may not need knocking off his perch. The campaign that follows mixes wonderfully chaotic infighting between the two Ork factions, with a fair bit of Admech-stomping and human-squashing on the side.

The Admech campaign is similar. Without spoiling much, you begin playing as a deeply inquisitive Potentia-Delta-9, who goes digging around in Kronus' deserts without a sufficient remit. All that's laced with an array of delightful 40k-isms: Mysterious voices! Betrayal! Computer spirits! Outraged officials citing revered and highly verbose bureaucratic procedure! If you've read any of the 40k novels (or indeed the Horus Heresy; those mysterious voices will jump out to anyone familiar with the first couple of books) then you will be well at home here. Dawn of War 4 has nabbed John French, one of the Black Library's Horus Heresy authors, so perhaps none of this should come as a surprise.

These splits then play out smartly in the structure of missions themselves. Some might be a race, for instance, where you and your rival need to fight your way through to a final point beyond a single, powerful enemy's base. I found one version this format especially enjoyable in the Admech campaign, when those two separate, essentially linear paths converged at one point, creating a supremely 40k type of bottleneck as three warring factions all met at once. I found success by letting my rival battle the stronger Ork faction, then darting in to pick off their main army for a major setback, then brute force my way through the remaining, slightly softened Ork horde. Then I sould send my my newer reinforcements from my base back into that chokepoint for more stalling while what was left of my vanguard made a big push to the finish.

Back to the sense of generosity, meanwhile, and the other part of this comes in the skirmishes, where there's an extraordinary amount of customisability available. You can tinker with the game's underlying numbers to make units squishier or tankier by default, or to make research or point-capturing faster or slower. You can change income rates, reinforcement costs, cooldown times. By my count, there are nine 'general' settings, eight 'economy' ones, and eight 'combat' ones to play around with. You can choose from multiple commander units (or disable them or tinker with how they spawn), as well as multiple game modes. There's the classic Annihilation and Domination (wipe out the enemy or take and hold capture points) alongside new modes called Maelstrom and Defence. These have you racing to claim one-off capturable points that drop in periodically all around the map, and, I'm going to assume… defending something (only the first two modes were playable in this preview). Then there's the Last Stand mode, which returns here revamped from Dawn of War 2 - again unavailable in this build - and the army painter; a classic feature which I can't talk about for now. Plus, reams of post-launch stuff to do, from free maps to new modes such as my personal beloved: the Conquest mode popularised in Dark Crusade.

Basically, by RTS launch standards it's pretty massive. The only limiting factor is that by 40k standards, having only four factions is a little thin, given how many there are in the game's universe. Four is perfectly standard for launch-version RTS games, but a few big 40k ones are missing here, most notably Chaos, the Astra Militarum, and the Eldar - who all featured prominently in the previous games. (The Astra Militarum are present and briefly playable in DoW 4's prologue, and pop up here and there in other missions as blobs of unfortunate cannon fodder, so you could reasonably hope they might arrive after launch. More factions have already been promised by King Art for 2027.)That scope is mirrored, in a way, by King Art's approach to Dawn of War 4's mechanical side. DoW has a little bit of every Dawn of War in it - yes, even Dawn of War 3, with its huge scale, spectacle, many active abilities, and Imperial Knight-class super units, for starters - and it's seemingly all the better for it. The sense when you play is that you're playing Dawn of War in its ultimate form: lavish, spectacular, dark, authentic, deep, and complex - but also a little campy, playful, and inviting to newer RTS players too.

Some more of that generosity, then: there are times, particularly when Orks and Admech are involved, where the screen is quite literally full of units. An ingenious simplifying factor in these cases is the new right-click-and-drag function, whereby you can select a group of enemies to attack in the same way you'd click-and-drag to choose your own units to command. Doing so sees your selected units automatically prioritise targets amongst the opposing group you've chosen.

You might think that sounds like it's oversimplifying the approach, removing the hard-earned skill of RTS veterans to micromanage, a bit like the combo shortcuts of modern fighting games. But that's not quite right. Instead, in practice I found it means you can efficiently move huge numbers of units into battle knowing they won't make truly irrational decisions (for the most part), and then get into micromanaging after the fact - which is where the great players will undoubtedly be separated from the good. The same goes for active abilities, of which there are quite a few in DoW 4: you can right-click on one to have that unit automatically use it when it comes off cooldown, so you know you're not leaving anything forgotten amongst the carnage.

"We basically said: let's give the player the option to automate a lot of things," Theysen explained to me. "It might not be 100 percent perfect - there is still something you can do if you really want to be more optimal - but you know, it's pretty good, right? And it's definitely better than not using something because you're overwhelmed." To Theysen, "the philosophy is that as a normal player, an average player, this really helps you out. If you're into your esports, and you're a really high-end RTS player, then you can probably still optimise things and you [will] want to do it yourself. But you know, not everybody is about 20 clicks-per-second."

I was curious about this - so many games with a PvP element find themselves falling into the trap of appealing to the competitive hardcore, leaving the often larger audience behind. "Basically, what we're saying is: we do not decide if this is, you know, the next big esports title. There are a lot of people with a lot more money who tried that," Theysen explained. The team is instead simply trying to "make sure there is no good reason why it couldn't be … but also, this is not our goal. We are not designing the game for the top one percent. It's more like, okay, we want a lot of people having fun with it, and we want to make sure that if people want to play it competitively, it's possible."The approach is the same when it comes to elements of balance - some of which, such as the reinforce function, have already become discussion points on certain subreddits. "If you balance everything for the top one percent then the game might feel wrong for the 99 percent. Because for them it's like: why are these units so weak? Well, they are so weak because there is some sort of thing that some pro player can maybe pull off."

On the topic of reinforcement, players can hit the reinforce button and have a squad produce a new unit after a short moment (or right-click it to have it automatically done as soon as resources are available). Theysen told me that, initially, the game didn't have on-field reinforcements at all. It's a returning feature from the first Dawn of War game, and quite a unique one. Later games from original developer Relic, such as Company of Heroes, saw reinforcement shift to be something only done on recalling your units to their main base. But at first it "felt a little bit weird - it's like lore-wise, it doesn't really make sense. And it's also not a super typical RTS feature."Then the team tested it just in case it was fun, "and it turned out to be really good because it's a way to bring the metagame, or the economy, into the battles, right?" Suddenly the player has a choice between spending lots of money to reinforce mid-battle, prolonging and maybe turning the battle in your favour, or they can micromanage more closely for a more efficient result. In practice I found myself having it set to 'on' almost by default - but in a way that's a conscious decision too, and a kind of crutch that I may have to ween myself off if I want to improve as a player over time. (Or, equally, something I can just leave on, so I can get on with having fun smashing my sea of Orks into Admech turrets.)

Even Dawn of War 4's headline visual innovation is a consideration here. The combat director feature - where hordes of individual units lock onto each other in melee with bespoke fighting animations - works in such a way as to take exactly the same amount of time as if your units were fighting each other the old-fashioned RTS way, Theysen assured me. "The things that are going on below the hood and the visualisation are completely separate from each other," he explained. "You could deactivate the whole thing and the game would still play exactly the same." Including it is essentially a flourish: something done, as I wrote when first testing it out, simply because one of King Art's animation specialists loved how it worked in the original and wanted to try something cool with it here.

This, and so much of Dawn of War 4's apparent munificence, brought another question to mind. How exactly can the team - not a particularly large one, by modern development standards - manage this approach? Amongst all the talk of ballooning budgets and sustainability in games these days, here's a fairly modest studio stacking feature on feature, promising lengthy post-release support - and, crucially, a lot of these little things are what you could fairly describe as luxuries.

Theysen put it down to a few things. The first is time: "We were very lucky," he said, with the team having worked on the game for around five years now. "That's a very long time." But that was made possible by the relatively small team size, "so the burn rate - the overall budget - is not higher than for other RTS games." The other factor is that they've been working with their current tech and fellow teammates for a long time, making that development more efficient. "Since we had the technology, had a lot of experience already, and had the time, we could basically say: okay, let's try to get it right - and everybody was very supportive," he added, citing publisher Plaion and Games Workshop's patience.

"If you look at some RTS games in the past, and you hear they had like an 18-month development time… I would say it's not possible to really make a great RTS game in 18 months," he continued. "Because it's just too complex - even if you have the technology, you discover stuff while you're working on the game, and then you can't follow up because whatever, you have to hit the next deadline. For us it was more like: we like the long development times. We also like long pre-productions where we can really figure out what we [want to] try and do here? And really, really experiment."

There's one more factor Theysen put the most emphasis on, however. "We really try hard to keep the team together. So here there are a lot of people who worked at King Art for like 15 years, or even longer, and everybody knows everybody in the team. It's like you have a sort of shorthand, but also you really understand, well, who is good at what." He gave an example: "Someone might not be the right person for something, and maybe if we put these two together, that's going to be great because you know this is really something they want to work on."

He cites the combat director again. "That was something that came from our head of animation, who was like a huge Dawn of War 1 fan, and he basically said: yeah, we can do the same thing, but we could also make it cooler; give me a couple weeks and I'll try to figure it out. And [our] first impression, everybody was like: I don't know how this is supposed to work. Because we have literally tens of thousands of permutations of who can fight who, right - because not only do we have almost 100 units, we also have all the different weapons and all of that stuff. But he basically said: 'No, no, I have a plan, let me try it out.'"

The result is a feature I've genuinely never seen before in the genre, amongst a sea of other neat little ideas that have no doubt come from someone wanting to try something cool, and insisting they have a plan.

"It basically comes down to having the time first, but also having the confidence," Theysen said. "And knowing the guy [so] that you say: okay, if he believes that works, it probably will, so let him try it. And I think this is really, really valuable. Because you cannot just buy a team. Or cannot just say, you know, we're doing the next great RTS or shooter or whatever it is, and now we buy everybody that we need for that and two years later we have a great game. That's not how it works."

All this has to be hedged by the fact I've only played a chunk of Dawn of War 4 so far, of course. But what a glorious, grim, bewitchingly good chunk it's been. The scale of some of my campaign battles has been stupendous, with huge numbers of units quite literally filling the screen and a level of visual spectacle rarely seen in this genre. The commitment to doing 40k right is eveident throughout, from the obvious narrative inspirations to the relentless, almost comical bleakness (especially in the level of violence imposed on those poor Astra Militarum). And the mix of older elements - requisition points, reinforcement, base-building - with more modern RTS intricacies feels considered and deliberate.

There'll be tinkering to do, no doubt - melee units look to be a bit too strong for now, and some better artillery-style disruptor units or abilities wouldn't go amiss - but there's time for that, and no doubt more to follow it after launch. And even as it is, this is one of the most impressive previews of a strategy game I have ever played.

#10646. This new qubit could be 100 times less error-prone in superfluid quantum computer breakthrough

Researchers at the University of Surrey have proposed a new kind of qubit that could help address one of the biggest obstacles in quantum computing: keeping error rates low as systems become larger.

The concept uses superfluid helium, a form of helium with unusual quantum properties, to create a qubit that may be less sensitive to some of the disturbances that affect today's leading quantum computers.

Why Quantum Computers Struggle With Errors

Quantum computers rely on qubits to store and process information in ways that conventional computers cannot. Many current systems use superconducting circuits, but these are highly sensitive to electromagnetic noise and stray electrical charges - similar to the static electricity that can make hair cling to a balloon.

Even very small disturbances can disrupt the fragile quantum information stored in a qubit. As more qubits are added, controlling those errors becomes increasingly difficult, making scalability one of the central challenges in quantum computing.

In a study published in npj Quantum Information, researchers from Surrey's Quantum Sciences Group describe a different approach built around superfluid helium-3 - an unusual form of liquid helium that can flow without friction.

Their proposed device, called the Superfluid Helium Oscillator Quantum (SHOQ) device, would use charge-neutral superfluid helium. Because the helium carries no electric charge, the design could be naturally protected from certain types of electromagnetic noise.

A Qubit With Much Lower Predicted Error Rates

According to the researchers, this is the first reported design for a qubit based on a superfluid. Their calculations suggest the SHOQ device could have error rates about 100 times lower than conventional superconducting qubits.

Dr. Priya Sharma, Daphne Jackson Fellow in Hybrid Quantum Systems at the University of Surrey's School of Mathematics and Physics, and lead author of the study, said:

"We are not the first to think about the individual components behind this idea, but what we have done for the first time is bring them together in a microfluidic device and work out the specific details that could enable the device to function as a qubit.

"The maths tells us that it should work. We have taken what we already know about superfluid helium and quantum technologies and turned that into an educated design, with the parameters and specifications needed to build one. The next step is to make a prototype and put those predictions to the test."

Rather than replacing existing quantum computing hardware, the researchers say the SHOQ device could potentially be integrated with superconducting quantum technology.

That could allow different types of qubits to perform different jobs within the same system, depending on their strengths.

One longer-term possibility is to use the SHOQ device as a form of quantum memory. In that setup, a superfluid based qubit could store quantum information while other hardware carries out calculations.

Dr. Eran Ginossar, Associate Professor at the University of Surrey's Department of Physics and Advanced Technology Institute, and co-author of the study, said:

"We don't necessarily need one type of qubit to do everything. Combining different quantum technologies could allow us to take advantage of the strengths of each.

"Superfluid helium gives us a fundamentally different type of quantum hardware to explore. If the predicted performance can be demonstrated experimentally, it could eventually work alongside existing superconducting technologies as part of a larger quantum system."

The team now plans to build a prototype to find out whether the predicted performance can be reproduced in a real device. That effort is being supported by an IAA Commercialisation Fellowship awarded to Dr. Priya Sharma.

The SHOQ device would need to operate at extremely low temperatures, but researchers have already reached the necessary conditions in previous experiments involving superfluid helium-3.

The project was led by the University of Surrey in collaboration with Professor Jens Koch at Northwestern University in the United States. Koch was one of the researchers involved in developing the transmon - a superconducting qubit design that is now widely used in quantum computing.

Materials provided by University of Surrey. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10645. Viral 97-year-old Vito dies after dancing to Stayin’ Alive for his birthday

A 97-year-old man has gone viral after dying during his own birthday party, where he had just danced to Stayin’ Alive by the Bee Gees.

On November 2, baker Eden Malinoff took to her TikTok account ‘BakedSoCal’ to reveal a cake that she had made for a neighbor’s 97th birthday party. According to Malinoff, Vito, the man at the centre of the party, wanted a “Halloween-birthday rager” in “true Vito fashion.”

However, as per the baker’s video, the 97-year-old “made a dramatic exit” as the night “did not end as anticipated” by everyone in attendance.

“If there is any way that Vito could have chosen to go out, it would have been like this. He was surrounded by people he loved, we were celebrating his long and full life, and I am just so grateful that I got to be a part of it,” Malinoff added, showing off quick snippets of Vito dancing at his party.

Viewers quickly noted that the 97-year-old appeared to be dancing to ‘Stayin’ Alive’ by The Bee Gees in one clip, with questions piling up in the TikToker’s comments.

She uploaded another video on November 3, showing off a longer version which confirmed that he had, in fact, danced to that song before passing.

“I’m sorry how are you not TRAUMATIZED,” one person commented. “I would choose this over rotting away in a hospital bed/retirement home 100% of the time,” another said. “I’m sorry he DIED? at the PARTY?” another added.

Malinoff added that Vito’s friends “believe that he was ready and honestly there’s not a better way to go.”

Another friend of the 97-year-old confirmed his passing on Instagram too. “He had some medical issues that we didn’t know the severity of, and he passed away respectfully with his favorite music going. Rest in peace, you legend.”

The baker’s videos have gotten well over 10 million views since November 3, with viewers asking for even more details about Vito.

The clips have also been reshared to other social media platforms as well by accounts that aren’t run by Malinoff, introducing even more people to Vito.
