#10644. With a cracking new tactical feature and dedicated UI "strike team", Football Manager 27 is a huge step back towards FM's best form

Welcome back, Football Manager. I've only played half a day or so of it, whistling through my inductions, board meetings and opening transfer window, but already the positive signs are there. Football Manager 27 is a big, much-appreciated step in the right direction. In a way it's a step back - this is essentially the game I wish FM26 had been - but sometimes that's necessary. One back, two forward. At least it's genuine progress. This is a Football Manager game again.

If that "game I wish FM26 had been" line has raised an Ancelotti-eqsue eyebrow, here's what I mean by it: FM27 has got rid of most - if not quite all - of the cards-and-tiles UI that gave FM26 its notoriety. It's dramatically easier, faster, and more sensible to navigate, though I still think there are places to improve. But it's also maintained the best part of last year's entry, in the majorly overhauled in- and out-of-possession tactics systems. And it's built on that system smartly: elements are tinkered with and made clearer; other parts are expanded, and a new system added entirely, in something called "tactical effectiveness". This sounds almost comically dry but, in true Football Manager fashion, that's the good stuff. This is a really smart system, one that brings with it a huge boost to the role-playing side of the game. Combined with the rolling-back of various UI elements to more traditional FM fare, it makes me feel about as optimistic as I have been for this series for a little while.

It's worth focusing on last year's game for a moment. "We had a lot of technical challenges to solve," explains Rémy Boicherot, FM27's head of design, to me in an interview at Sports Interactive's head office in East London. Part of that was porting the interface to the new engine itself, with last year's game moving to Unity for the first time, and requiring that unprecedented year's delay as a result. The other was with "new graphical data," as Boicherot puts it. "It's a huge undertaking to transfer technology, and this is what we had to focus on last year to release the game." The studio's feedback rounds with players have since brought up two big points of improvement they hoped to make this year. The first, unsurprisingly, was the UI. The second, a little more interestingly, was the question of immersion. "The sense of feeling lost in that world."

With FM27, and those technical challenges seemingly now overcome, "we just focused on gameplay and making sure that the game resonated with our community," Boicherot continues. "We focused all our efforts into this. It was really a consolidation year. We still used a lot of these new philosophies to empower the interface, but with more of a familiar feel, and with just more efficiencies all throughout."

Ant Farley, senior features manager at Sports Interactive, put it similarly. "We were building from scratch," he said, which meant "everything needing to be built from the ground up, and just the volume of work that that entails and the technical complexity of that mammoth task. You can zero in on [the fact] that didn't give us much room to iterate."

He says the dev team, as a result, "realised that FM26 is a game that's good if you're very focused on your own team, and becomes [one with] quite an amount of friction when you're trying to be part of that wider world, starting to browse around." That realisation came "very early on," he says: "We really need to work on this, and the tiles and the remit of the tiles, I think is the really important significant change there… I think on paper the notion of a tile as being both navigation and information is really quite powerful. But what I think we did was, we relied on it for navigation, rather than it being a kind of secondary purpose for it. It became their primary purpose in a lot of places, and what that means then is, you're constructing screens in a way that imbalances the information to some extent."

The improvements are immediately evident. The navigation bar is more sensibly organised, the "portal" now more closely resembles your old inbox, with a bit of colour-coding thrown in. In the simplest terms, I found myself running down far fewer navigational cul-de-sacs than with FM26.

There are still snags, of course. Personally, I think the entire cards-and-tiles system should be left behind now, rather than just most of it. There are just very few circumstances under which a pop-up window is more useful or efficient than navigating to a full page. Boicherot's view here is that certain systems, such as messages from in-game people, suit the pop-up windows better - he may be right, though for me the two are essentially competing UIs overlaid on one another. The classic FM experience - not a spreadsheet, as people like to call it, but really a kind of web browser, where you can seamlessly move from your email inbox-cum-to-do-list to a series of interlinked wiki pages via hyperlinks and a clearly defined navigation bar - remains unbeaten. It's a single, intuitive and widely recognised visual language: underlined text takes you to a page on that topic, while dropdown menus and the nested ones within them take you to the most essential hub pages you'll visit most often. The tiles system only muddies the waters.

Thankfully the team, such as Boicherot, still seems to largely agree. "As a player, you don't want to think about the UI," he says. "Every time you watch a show, you don't want to learn how to use a [remote] control again. So with FM, we just wanted to get that familiar feel again." The studio had a "whole dedicated strike team" to resolving the issue, while the wider team, he says, has coupled that rework with the new drive for immersion, and in turn essentially adapted most of the compositions of the game's various screens to focus on "dynamic elements, on player stats, on the latest matches, who's hot, who's not, who's performing, underperforming. We have quite a lot of news stories as well. We wanted to make sure that we had better journalists in-game, reporting on more interesting stories, and basically giving you a better window to the world."

It's all good progress, ultimately, even if I still have my foibles with the card system's leftovers. And those new additions are really the things I want to talk about - and which I can tell Sports Interactive's developers are keen to talk about as well. Don your anoraks, then. It's time to talk about tactical effectiveness.

The system works like this: replacing the old and somewhat ignorable gauge for your team's tactical familiarity, there's now a gauge called tactical effectiveness. That gauge is influenced by four sub-categories: tactical understanding; player ability; player buy-in; and next match info. Those are then influenced by another layer of influence beneath them. Tactical understanding, for instance, depends on a combination of your player's understanding of their in-possession shape, passing style, build up, chance creation, out-of-possession shape, line of engagement, pressing intensity, and the pressing trap. You'll recognise most of those as tactical settings you can tinker with, which we'll come back to shortly. And even that layer, at times, has things that influence it again. Chance creation, for instance, is dictated by the training contribution, match exposure, players' tactical ability (which I understand from Sports Interactive developers to be influenced by their adaptability, teamwork and other attributes), and the tactical complexity of what you're asking them to do.

Sports Interactive says this is a result of direct conversations they've had with their connections in the Premier League and wider parts of the professional game. Fundamentally, Boicherot says, the previous tactical familiarity system was "quite simplistic" in comparison to what real managers were thinking about. "We also looked at Pep Guardiola's approach at City. [It] was very much, spend quite a lot of money on players, and then you don't see them play for quite a while until he knows that they're ready. And when he puts them on the pitch, they are effective. They just understand how to implement his vision directly on the pitch, and that was something that we felt we couldn't capture with the old system."

All of this is presented in a slightly underwhelming screen of several plain columns with a list of the above factors on them, which is a bit of a shame, but the impact it already had on my thinking in just a few hours with the game was huge. This is, ultimately, a role-playing mechanic, acting as a kind of bridge over the gap between how you think about tactics in Football Manager, and how a real manager might think about them in the real world. The biggest impact happens in how you think about the game - which, in a way, is where a lot of the playing of Football Manager really happens.

Beyond Boicherot's case of Pep's new players, an example given in a brief introduction was a good one: the "new manager bounce" - the notion that a team, after a previous, underperforming manager gets the sack, suddenly has a short-term improvement in results. Technically this is a bit of a myth; the data apparently suggests changing gaffers doesn't actually lead to a noticeable short-term uptick in results on average. But when it does happen, the theory is that these new managers tend to go "back to basics" with their tactics, simplifying instructions for bogged-down, low-morale players and letting them play their game with a bit more freedom.

Take Michael Carrick, for instance, from my trusty old management basket case Manchester United. Previous manager Ruben Amorim's system was complex and demanding, requiring both immense fitness and a large amount of tactical understanding and adherence from his players. Michael Carrick, then an interim, came in and seemingly reverted the team to something far simpler, and the short term impact was an immediate uptick in results. He put Bruno Fernandes back in the number 10 role, reverted to a familiar 4-2-3-1 formation, gave a relatively large amount of creative freedom to more creative players, and set them up in more of a fluid counterattacking mid-block as opposed to Amorim's structured, finely-tuned high press. And initially they did well! And now this season, after opponents had all summer to work through their opposition analysis and realise the tactical plan was actually quite simple, they've been "found out", as the football cliché goes - with results flipping into disaster territory.

FM27's tactical effectiveness system is designed to do something similar (although perhaps not quite as drastically as the Carrick disaster). Simple tactics, such as going long to a target forward, staying compact and hitting teams on the counter, are more easily grasped and implemented by players, leading to better short-term results. Complex pressing, inverting fullbacks, and intricate tiki-taka football meanwhile will likely take far longer to master, potentially leading to early-season struggles as the team learns your fancy, armchair-tactico system. And so in comes the role-playing: do I gamble on tactical purism for a higher long-term ceiling? Or do I secure my short-term future and attempt to gradually transform the team over time? Will fans accept my route one football, even if it gets me immediate results? Will players feel comfortable playing it?

This is where other, interlinking systems come in, and now my anorak is positively bristling. Training has also had quite a major rework, for instance. Training sessions have new categories: tactical understanding, match preparation, footballing development, and physical and recovery. Many of the sessions are familiar, but their recategorisation is important. Previously, we're told, sessions were primarily seen as a means of improving players' attributes - "Defending: Engaged" improved things like tackling and heading, for instance - while a few others helped with tactical familiarity or match fitness, without a clear gauge of their effectiveness.

Now, all of the sessions that come under "tactical understanding" will, you guessed it, help with the "tactical understanding" aspect of overall tactical effectiveness. And knowing that the gauge for, say, your team's understanding of your preferred passing style is in the orange will immediately indicate to you that you need to schedule a few more "Passing Style" training sessions. That then needs balancing with developmental sessions, match preparation sessions such as set pieces - now broken down into individual ones such as corners, free kicks, and so on - and recovery. It's another aspect influenced by actual conversations with those in the real game. Farley highlights the modern emphasis on "tactical periodisation," the approach of making training sessions resemble matches in various ways, down the certain "game state" such as being 1-0 down, consolidating a lead, and so on.

"It's going back a few years now," Farley continues, offering one example of how the team learned from the real game, "but we were very fortunate - I won't name the manager - we had a manager do a 'foot talk' for us, where they were talking about that tactical periodisation, and that kind of, almost playbook, American football-style of: now execute this plan. And that being something they would communicate from the touchline. And it was during covid, behind closed doors games, right? So then when I next watched this team play and you have the benefit of there being no crowd noise and everything, you really actually see it in action with that insight."

Beyond that, there's now an impact on your squad-building, too. Recruiting effectively now means considering your scout's take on a player's tactical suitability - are they likely to fit a high-pressing, high-technicality style such as Guardiola-like tiki-taka? Would James Tarkowski suit a high defensive line? Does Rayan Cherki want to play park-the-bus football? Bruno Fernandes, clearly sensing the importance of the topic with FM27's impending release, made his thoughts quite clear on what he thinks about not pressing opponents after Manchester United's humbling defeat to Como just the other week. This is the tactical effectiveness system personified.

"Take Thierry Henry moving from Arsenal to Barcelona," Boicherot suggests. "There was a big, big adoption time, right? But others are a bit more efficient with different types of player roles. It depends on their attributes, their stats, on their adaptability." It's essentially a melding of soft skill man-management with the modern, analytic side of the game - the type of blend that makes the very best coaches in the world so effective.

If you hadn't guessed, I'm a fan. It's a wonderful way to bring the type of gap-filling I'd already been doing with my imagined narratives into the reality of Football Manager itself. Beyond it, there are more tweaks that get the nod, too. Touchline shouts return, having been patched in apologetically to FM26, but now they're accompanied by a nifty feature that runs in tandem called Tweaks. These are basically one-touch tactical shortcuts you can instigate without leaving the in-match screen, shouts-style, to get your team to push up, or step off the press, or, much to my enjoyment, send the goalkeeper forward for set pieces. Notably Sports Interactive opted to make these tweaks stay active after you make them, until you consciously turn them back off again, as opposed to operating on a timer. The theory there being that the decision to turn them off is often as interesting as the decision to implement them in the first place.

"We wanted to capture a bit more of a 'duel' type of experience against the opposing team manager," Boicherot explains. "How do you want to disrupt your opposition in a quick way, really trying to capture the kind of quick instructions you tell your captain on the sideline and let them go and implement."

International management also gets a welcome bit of attention, with training now properly implemented for that, as does the 2D engine, which has had a lovely visual revamp that still maintains a pleasingly retro tinge. There are reworks to how players' running speed works, which interlinks with fatigue and even the types of runs they might attempt. Tied to that is a new instruction for how a forward should attack the box, all this together theoretically giving slower forwards greater influence on the game. And the onboarding experience has been tinkered with once again, with more manager customisation options and tweaks to how your traits and attributes are set up.

The headline changes, though, are where FM27 is at its best so far. It is easier to play and navigate, "faster to the fun," as well-trained UX developers often like to say, and has genuine potential to be the most tactically interesting and nuanced game in the series to date. Shuffle away the last of those remaining cards, and this could be a seriously good year. Thank goodness for that.

#10643. Building Location-Aware Interfaces Without Mixing Up Language, Currency, and Region

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A visitor opens an online store while traveling. Their browser uses English, their account is registered in Canada, their network appears to be in Germany, and their delivery address is in France.

Which currency should the store show? Which products are available? What language should the checkout use?

There is no single answer hidden inside a country code. These decisions depend on different signals and different product rules.

For developers and designers, the challenge is to represent that context explicitly. Otherwise, a convenient shortcut—such as deriving everything from an IP address—can produce confusing prices, unexpected redirects, and incorrect availability.

This article walks through a practical approach to building a regional product interface, with examples that also apply to travel sites, service directories, and applications that retrieve current web information.

Avoid a single variable named country that silently controls every aspect of the interface.

These values can disagree without any of them being wrong.

For an anonymous visitor, network region might help suggest an initial market. Once the user selects a destination, the application should not silently replace it because a later request arrives from a different network.

Write precedence rules for each decision. Language selection and delivery eligibility need separate policies.

SitePoint’s introduction to JavaScript internationalization provides useful background on separating software capabilities from locale-specific adaptation.

This is an illustrative application contract. It keeps the observation's market, currency, and timestamp attached to the value.

Without that context, another component might reuse the amount for a different market or label it as current long after availability changes.

At the API boundary, validate the required fields and their allowed values. A missing amount must not become zero, and a missing availability value must not become “in stock.”

Also define the timestamp precisely. Does it mean the source was fetched, the source published an update, or your database last processed the record? Those are different events.

For the interface, use wording consistent with the evidence: “Checked at 09:00” is more precise than “Live availability” when you only have a timestamped observation.

Browser preferences and network location operate at different layers.

Changing the browser locale can affect language preferences and formatting. It does not relocate the connection seen by the server. Similarly, supplying browser geolocation coordinates does not change the network's public IP address.

This distinction also matters in live-web retrieval. Bixberry describes an opt-in network that routes requests through participants' internet connections. It illustrates how retrieval infrastructure can involve connections outside a conventional data-center environment.

When comparing two responses, record the relevant conditions:

Keep diagnostic records minimal and avoid retaining unnecessary personal information.

For authorized regional testing of your own application, SitePoint’s guide to testing geo-dependent web apps offers related background on separating browser settings from network behavior.

JavaScript's Intl.NumberFormat formats numeric values according to locale and currency settings:

Both calls represent the same euro amount. Formatting it for a different locale does not convert it to another currency.

Do not turn a euro price into a dollar price by changing only the currency argument. A conversion requires a defined exchange-rate source, timestamp, rounding policy, and explanation of whether the result is an estimate or the actual charged amount.

For calculations, choose a representation appropriate to your pricing model rather than relying on floating-point arithmetic throughout. If using integer minor units, remember that currencies do not all use two decimal places.

SitePoint’s overview of the JavaScript Internationalization API explains related formatting capabilities.

Practical check: switch interface language while leaving the selected market unchanged. The number format may change, but the underlying price and currency should remain consistent with the product rules.

A country flag is a weak substitute for a language label. Countries can have multiple languages, and the same language can be used across many countries.

Use clear, separate controls when the product needs them:

Do not expose a control unless its effect is meaningful and implemented consistently. A currency selector that updates a price label but leaves checkout charging behavior unexplained creates confusion.

Before a market change, explain consequential effects. For example:

Changing your delivery country may update availability and delivery options.

Preserve the user's task where possible. If a product is unavailable in the newly selected market, retain its page and show an explanation instead of sending the user to an unrelated homepage.

For designers, test longer translations, currency codes, and expanded validation messages. Use logical CSS properties where appropriate so layouts can adapt to right-to-left content:

These rules are a starting point, not a complete localization solution. Review reading order, icons, numeric content, and translated copy with people familiar with the target language.

A regional interface can behave correctly at the origin and still show incorrect content when a shared cache serves the wrong variant.

If a response varies by market or language, that variation must be reflected in your cache design. One option for public content is an explicit route structure:

These routes are illustrative. They make market and language differences visible, but the rest of the cache configuration must still match the application's behavior.

Personalized prices and account-specific responses require their own cache policy. A public page cache must not expose one account's content to another.

Test the deployed caching layer, not only a local development server:

Keep freshness requirements separate from variant separation. A response can belong to the correct market and still be too old.

Playwright's emulation settings let tests configure locale, time zone, geolocation, and permissions.

This assumes a configured base URL and an application implementing the illustrated route and selectors. It tests the application's handling of explicit market selection under a different locale; it does not prove that the request originated in Germany.

Use controlled API fixtures for predictable frontend tests, then test real routing and caching separately. Browser emulation is one layer of verification, not a substitute for every regional check.

A location-aware application should be able to explain why it chose a language, price, destination, or availability result.

Keep those decisions separate, preserve the context of retrieved data, and make explicit user choices easy to change. Then test the situations where signals disagree.

The result is an interface that works for travelers, multilingual households, remote workers, and anyone whose preferences cannot be reduced to the location of their current internet connection.

#10642. Mouthwashing is finally on consoles and Xbox Game Pass, and it's a Halloween must for horror fans

A mutilated man stares at you, his single piercing eye following as you move around the room. His filthy bandages hide a ruined face, raw lips circling disturbingly perfect teeth, and his moans echo through the halls. But that eye: it knows something you don't. At least not yet.

Developed by Wrong Organ, Mouthwashing rightly earned a lot of praise when it was released in 2024; it's an extraordinary first-person horror adventure game placing you among the five-strong crew of the Tulpar, a commercial space freighter part-way through its year-long mission to carry its unknown cargo across the stars. Moments into its dramatic opening scene - "0 days before the crash", we're told - things go dramatically awry, establishing a dire tone and a baseline of dread that only builds from there.

Mouthwashing isn't a game laden with jumpscares; instead it is a masterfully crafted discomfort simulator, conjuring unease through its claustrophobic environmental design, strong audio work, disorientating structure and pacing, and a lo-fi visual style that creates spaces for your imagination to fill.

Slowly, for instance, the Tulpar's already gloomy corridors constrict, shrunk by the crew's increasingly desperate use of repair foam; this continuous degradation of the space around you mirroring the minds of its characters, as circumstances grow increasingly dire. For as enjoyable as Mouthwashing's light puzzling is, you're really here for its unexpectedly impactful story.

This is an intelligent game that trusts its audience, and that's most obvious in its breathless, non-chronological narrative. It's a story that doesn't stay still, that offers no moment to breathe. Scenes transition into each other with a digital datamoshing effect, almost as if you're watching system files recovered long after the fact. And each transition often signals a different, disorientating kind of switch, as it's not always clear when - or even who - you are now.

I don't want to spoil much, but there's a surprising amount of subtlety to the way characters are portrayed and gradually evolve. One crew member, for instance, sleeps near a statue of the shipping company's cartoon mascot, which emits voice lines whenever someone draws near. The reason why isn't spelled out to you, but on a second playthrough, this small act of self-preservation becomes clear. And Mouthwashing only grows more rewarding with each subsequent return as you start to notice this attention to detail more and more.

Speaking of characters, one is particularly deplorable; a truly detestable creature up there with Shepherd from the original Modern Warfare trilogy and perhaps even Eric Sparrow if you're a Tony Hawk's Underground fan. Their narcissist journey, culminating in a spiral of delusion that really made my skin crawl, exemplifies what makes Mouthwashing work so well.

Its horror stems from the psychological disintegration of the Tulpar's crew, and the way the resulting violence unfolds. Violence is sparse and unglorified here, but you're often an active participant, much of the nastiest actions occurring when you're in direct control. It absolutely made me wince a few times.

And then comes the closer; one of the most moving monologues I've heard in a video game. It's a final message - not only to a member of the crew, but to you the player - on taking responsibility. By this point, what's done is done, leaving one - perhaps futile - final decision to make. One last chance for a kind of salvation. For me, it's a message that hit home.

Mouthwashing is powerful stuff, then; a different sort of horror this Halloween. It's an easy recommendation, and has been wowing PC players for a while. As of today, though, it's finally available for consoles - PS4, PS5, Switch, and Xbox Series X/S to be exact - and is out on Game Pass too. Additionally, there's currently 40 percent off on Steam.

Nintendo Switch, PC, PS4, PS5, Xbox One, Xbox Series X/S

#10641. KFC reveals Avengers: Doomsday menu with black burgers, green mayo and ‘Doom Dust’

KFC has announced a collaboration with Marvel Studios ahead of Avengers: Doomsday, introducing a limited-time menu inspired by Doctor Doom.

The fast-food chain is teaming up with Marvel for the first time, bringing a range of themed burgers, wings, fries, drinks, and collectibles to restaurants from November 9.

Although it will be available in the UK, through January 2027, the United States will miss out.

The new menu features several items inspired by the Marvel villain, including the Doom Burger, which combines KFC’s Original Recipe chicken with lettuce, pickles, cheese, Doom Cola sauce, and Smoky Green Mayo inside a black sesame bun.

The Doom Cola sauce is described as a sweet, sticky cola glaze with smoky BBQ notes, while the green mayo and Doom Dust seasoning add more unusual flavors to the lineup.

Other menu items include Doom Dripped Wings coated in the cola sauce, Doom Loaded Fries topped with both sauces, and a Doom Bucket featuring chicken, dipping sauces, and Doom Dust.

There’s also a Doom Supreme Fizz, featuring sparkling lemonade, green apple or kiwi syrup, and boba pearls, alongside Masked Mozza Melts served with the themed sauces.

However, some items won’t be available in some countries, with KFC saying it will vary between markets.

The collaboration also introduces five collectible Colonel Sanders figurines, each dressed in costumes inspired by Avengers: Doomsday characters. These will be available with qualifying menu purchases in select markets while supplies last.

#10640. Scientists recreate a bizarre quantum effect that stops a particle cold

A particle does not have to pass through a magnetic field to be influenced by it. That surprising feature of quantum physics has now been recreated by researchers at the University of Oxford, who used a hybrid quantum computer to demonstrate how an invisible magnetic influence can bring a simulated particle's movement to a complete stop.

The experiment reproduced a phenomenon known as the Aharonov-Bohm effect, in which a charged particle traveling around a region containing magnetic flux experiences a measurable change in its quantum state, even though its path never takes it through the magnetic field itself.

The Oxford team achieved this using a quantum system that combines qubits, the basic units of quantum information, with quantum oscillators, which represent vibrating motion. Their findings, published in Nature Physics, demonstrate how this combination could help scientists investigate fundamental interactions between matter and fields that are increasingly difficult to simulate using conventional computers.

A Strange Quantum Effect Predicted in 1959

In everyday physics, a charged particle responds to the electric and magnetic fields it encounters. Quantum mechanics, however, reveals that particles can behave in ways that have no straightforward counterpart in our ordinary experience.

In 1959, physicists Yakir Aharonov and David Bohm predicted that a charged particle could be affected by magnetic flux even if it traveled entirely outside the region containing the magnetic field. Rather than experiencing a conventional magnetic force along its path, the particle would undergo a change in its quantum phase.

Quantum phase describes an aspect of a particle's wave behavior that influences how different possible paths interfere with one another. Although the phase itself is not directly visible, differences in phase can produce measurable effects.

The prediction became known as the Aharonov-Bohm effect and was eventually verified in experiments involving real electrons. It remains one of the clearest demonstrations that the quantum world operates according to rules that go beyond the familiar laws of classical physics.

Researchers are now exploring how this phenomenon behaves in more complex theoretical systems known as lattice gauge theories.

These theories describe how matter interacts with fields associated with fundamental forces. They represent space as a grid, with matter located at individual points and fields associated with the connections between them. Such models are particularly important in particle physics and high-energy physics, where scientists study the basic particles and interactions that make up the universe.

The challenge is that the mathematics becomes extraordinarily demanding as these systems grow more complicated. Even powerful conventional computers can struggle to calculate how all the interacting components evolve.

Quantum computers offer a different approach. Instead of attempting to calculate every aspect of a quantum system using classical computing methods, scientists can construct another quantum system that follows the same underlying rules. They can then observe its behavior directly, an approach known as quantum simulation.

How Scientists Built the Quantum Experiment

The Oxford project began in 2022, when lead author Dr. Sebastian Saner, Dr. Oana Bazavan, and their colleagues started developing a way to simulate lattice gauge theories using a hybrid quantum system. They collaborated with Dr. Alejandro Bermudez of the Instituto de Física Teórica in Madrid.

Their experimental design relied on two different quantum components that performed separate functions.

The first consisted of qubits encoded in the internal states of trapped ions, which are electrically charged atoms held in place using electromagnetic fields. These qubits represented the gauge fields in the theoretical model.

The second component used quantum oscillators, created by the vibrations of those same trapped ions. These oscillators represented matter.

By combining the two, the researchers constructed a simple loop that served as a basic building block of a lattice gauge theory. Two oscillators represented matter at two different locations, while two qubits represented the fields connecting those locations.

Although the arrangement was small, it was sufficient to reproduce an important interaction between matter and magnetic flux.

Quantum Entanglement Creates a Surprising Solution

The researchers next placed the two qubits into an entangled quantum state. Entanglement is a phenomenon in which quantum systems become linked in ways that cannot be fully explained by classical physics.

Within the team's simulated lattice gauge theory, this entangled state represented magnetic flux passing through the center of the loop.

Initially, the researchers had planned to introduce the magnetic flux as a fixed background condition. However, creating the necessary interaction would have required capabilities that their quantum hardware did not possess.

Encoding the flux directly into the qubits provided a practical alternative. More importantly, it allowed the magnetic flux to become part of the evolving quantum system rather than remaining an unchanging external influence.

"For us, the exciting step was to encode the magnetic flux in a gauge field that was itself dynamical," says Dr. Saner. "Rather than having matter evolve in a fixed background, the matter and gauge field become part of the same quantum dynamics."

What began as a technical workaround ultimately enabled the researchers to explore a more interesting version of the phenomenon, one in which matter and the field could participate together in the quantum dynamics.

A Quantum Effect That Stops Particle Motion

With their simulated loop in place, the scientists examined how a matter particle moved between locations through a process known as quantum tunneling.

Quantum tunneling allows particles to move between states in ways that would not be possible under ordinary classical rules.

When the researchers ran their simulation without magnetic flux, the particle could tunnel freely around the loop.

Introducing the simulated flux produced a strikingly different result.

The particle had two possible paths around the loop, but the magnetic flux changed their relative quantum phases. As a result, the two paths interfered destructively, meaning their quantum contributions canceled each other out.

This interference completely suppressed the tunneling, leaving the simulated particle frozen in its original state.

The outcome demonstrated the Aharonov-Bohm effect within a lattice gauge theory containing a dynamical field. Rather than simply reproducing the familiar behavior of a particle moving around a fixed magnetic influence, the experiment showed how the phenomenon can emerge when matter and the field are both represented by interacting quantum components.

A New Tool for Exploring Fundamental Physics

The Oxford findings were published alongside a related study by researchers at the University of Maryland, led by Professor Norbert Linke.

The Maryland team also used a hybrid quantum system, but their experiment focused on simulating the Yukawa potential, a mathematical description of an interaction relevant to nuclear and particle physics.

Although the two research groups developed their methods independently, they later coordinated the submission of their studies to Nature Physics.

Together, the experiments demonstrate how hybrid quantum computers could become valuable tools for investigating fundamental interactions that are difficult to reproduce using classical simulations.

By combining different types of quantum components, researchers can build systems in which matter and fields are represented in ways that more closely reflect the physics they want to study.

The Oxford experiment provides an example of that potential. A phenomenon first predicted in 1959 and later observed with electrons has now been recreated in a quantum simulator where the field itself participates in the dynamics, offering scientists another way to explore the unusual rules governing the quantum world.

Materials provided by University of Oxford. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10637. Call of Duty Modern Warfare 4 review

It's easy to dislike Call of Duty. Aside from the annual cycle of development, marketing, and controversy that has defined the series identity for the past 22 years, the series has become an integral part of how video games prop up the military-industrial complex. Whilst some of the stories - especially 2025's Black Ops 7 - have deteriorated into the realms of science-fiction absurdity, many of the narratives you'll find in the Modern Warfare splinter of the series are more grounded in reality. When you're playing as semi-fictional UK and US troopers, taking part in illegal operations in foreign territory, wantonly executing people over differences in ideology, Call of Duty can feel gross: paper-thin propaganda designed to glorify war and reinforce lines between 'them' and 'us'.

But Modern Warfare 4 shies away from this. A little. The bar is low, granted, but there is a slightly more humanitarian slant to the game this year - aping 2009's No Russian, there are a few missions that plant you into the shoes of the 'enemy', and ask you to make moral choices that, I won't lie, I was rather shocked to see manifest in a CoD game. In 2019, developer Infinity Ward asked what would happen if there was a terrorist attack on the streets of London. In 2022, the studio ponders what happens when a co-ordinated international military operation goes awry on a Mexican oil rig. In 2023, we go to Lower Manhattan as the Russian army stages an attack on the New York Stock Exchange. So, in moving our lens and making us play as a North Korean militiaman, I was genuinely surprised to learn that - if you refuse your orders - you are summarily shot, leaving the rest of the mission to play out in front of your lifeless corpse. There is no 'game over' state for not complying: the horrors carried out by the ruthless regime carry on even in the face of your dereliction of duty. It might be using baby speak, by literary standards, but here I think Call of Duty is trying to make a point.

And it doesn't fall on ears unwilling to listen, either. Just this week, a South Korean politician urged the country's president to play Modern Warfare 4, so striking is its depiction of a theorised North Korean assault on the nation (which makes the dev's inclusion of real disaster footage an even more regrettable gaffe. Whilst it's important to remember that a country's population is not its leadership, the news has been full of North Korean threats and bluster for many years now. And in Modern Warfare 4, Infinity Ward wanted to tell a story "ripped from the headlines". The result is a slightly more 'reactive' setup, where a militarised nation responds to an invasion rather than instigates one, which in turn gives a sense of urgency and speculation that is more agreeable than, say, another shadowy skirmish in the Middle East, or an insurgency into some proxy state secretly controlled by Russia (don't hold your breath).

As we flip back and forth between ex-SAS hard bastard, John Price (who we've been following since 2007) and a ragtag bunch of conscribed South Korean soldiers and US Marine allies that find themselves in the middle of a brutal, casualty-heavy North Korean invasion, we get to see Call of Duty campaign design at its finest. There's a rousing John Wick-inspired shootout in a club that evolves into a parkour dash across Manhattan rooftops. There's a cinematic and genuinely impressive shooting gallery-like encounter in a trainyard that evokes classic arcade lightgun design. There's an expansive open world area that toys with stealth and sniping, there's a car chase through the streets of Paris, and there's a beachhead assault that ties it all together nicely.

Beyond even those, the standout mission is Stranded, which sees the South Korean team deep in enemy territory, fighting for their lives. This introduces crafting for a few missions - something that could feel tacked-on and rote, but is actually deployed like a lever by Infinity Ward to wedge open just how important each of the various tools at your disposal are. Want a thermal scope? Then you're going to prioritise that over more munitions. Want explosives? Then your basic arms will suffer. It serves to emphasise just how well every aspect of Modern Warfare's toolkit complements every other piece, resulting in this rich, deep, interlocking marvel that makes older Call of Duty games look rudimentary in comparison.

This is complemented by setpieces like Traffic, where you engage on a breakneck chase through the streets of Paris in a romp sure to cost the Parisian treasury millions in collateral damage. Here, you get to see the breadth and depth of the enemy AI that Infinity Ward has crafted - which no doubt went hand-in-hand with the new DMZ mode, more on that later - as we witness just how complex the studio's engine can get: destruction, cover mechanics, and so many different kinds of visual effect all rush on-screen at once. It's overwhelming, it's intimidating, it's electrifying. It's peak Call of Duty.

Between these standout missions, there are moments where you're asked to consider what it's like for the pawns on the board - the other soldiers, the unnamed, those whose strings are being pulled by forces they don't understand. It's a bit Fisher-Price, a bit A-Level, a 'War is Bad' pin badge worn on a jacket from an Army Surplus store, but hey: I'd rather this than a completely unobserved 'ooh-rah!' story about the US army coming to the rescue again.

The downside of the campaign comes more from its setup and pacing than its content, then. Price's story wraps up without applause, really - I won't ruin the specifics here, but his actions and what he does to quash the machinations of a very oddly-implemented Mads Mikkelsen are sort of left in the dark to rot, as the desperate resistance of our South Korean recruits takes the spotlight. Again, I think Call of Duty might be trying to say something here: that the morally ambiguous and collateral damage-accumulating actions of one desperate operative that actually saved the world don't get to breathe in the daylight; instead, the 'courageous' defense of privates on the frontline should be lauded. War is messy, no-one is in the right, everyone loses except for the multinationals putting the hardware in the hands of the humans. Is that what I'm supposed to come out of this thinking?

Whatever the emotional impact is, you can't argue with the physical one: this is a tight, eight-hour campaign with moments up there with Modern Warfare's best. It stops before it ever risks getting overlong, it's got plenty of new ideas to throw at you and - played on the hardest mode - it's a tight puzzle box of encounter design that's just as obsessed with detail as it is with Michael Bay-esque spectacle. It's a far cry from whatever the hell was going on in Black Ops 7 last year.

Reeling from the fact I think I nearly felt something during a Call of Duty campaign, I needed to boot up the multiplayer - a mode which has by now been refined into its near-perfect form, an ultra-fast, turbo-injected mosh pit of quick scopes, slide cancels, and kill corridors. Map design is great - of course it is, it's Infinity Ward - and the gunplay is good as it ever has been in the Modern Warfare games. Since 2019, when Infinity Ward went to great expense to rebuild its engine, the Call of Duty experience has been luxurious. The sound design alone is worthy of applause, every clack of a bolt-action lever, every shell casing falling to the floor, every fizz of thermite boring through your cover… it's immediately identifiable and extremely important. To play Modern Warfare 4 without a decent set of headphones or a good surround sound setup is to intentionally handicap yourself.

There are a few qualms, as ever: some respawn mechanics are vexing (especially on quicker, gnarlier maps - this week's addition of Shipment is a hilariously bad example of this), and some of the weapon balancing has yet to be totally calmed down, in part owing to a last-minute change to weapon damage that extends the time-to-kill beyond Infinity Ward's usual fare. Whilst this means there's a bit of chaos for early adopters like myself, it actually speaks to the level of nuance Infinity Ward has to play with: I'd love to see the backend of Modern Warfare 4, with all its knobs and dials, seeing how the studio tweaks and balances guns to keep up with what players are doing. The slightly lower time-to-kill is actually a fascinating experiment in figuring out what Modern Warfare is beyond its selection of guns - and I'd happily take a bit of weird balancing a deeper, more intricate damage system.

Speaking of, the customisation system also works nicely here. With the right attachments, some guns are elevated from 'useful' to 'essential'. The ISO Nightshade SMG seems a bit busted right now, and I keep getting chewed up by suspiciously powerful one-two taps of the Type 73 LMG. I'm a born sharpshooter, and the Oris 8.6 is an early favourite for popping heads across the map, especially dressed up with a 2.1x scope with thermal imagine. The overall economy of guns and range-versus-power feels a bit off as it stands - and is quite literally being addressed as I type - but I'm playing multiplayer in launch week, so there's always going to be some friction.

Then there's Kill Block: an experimental and dynamic rotating multiplayer map and mode concept. It's absurd in its pitch: by using a selection of 'slabs', it can form 500+ unique combinations of map, meaning no match plays out quite like the last. Maps in Call of Duty are (in)famously defined by their sight lines; if you stand here, you can shoot someone there. If you camp this corner, you'll rack up kills. If you snipe on this lane, you'll collect greenhorn scalps a-plenty. Kill Block turns all that on its head, forcing players to be more reactive, and engage more with the systems on offer in Modern Warfare 4, rather than rely on instincts baked in from two-plus decades of CoD. With the promise of more modular elements to come - from fan-favourite maps like Crash, for instance - I can see this mode and setup becoming yet another staple in the ever-expanding Call of Duty canon. This is how you breathe life into the more competitive-focused modes that often fail to shine alongside respawn-focused experiences like Team Deathmatch.

I have to praise the complexity and wizardry Infinity Ward has displayed with how the guns handle; each attachment is meaningful, most guns feel unique, and the feedback you get from firing any armament is exquisite. It has all the production value of a dedicated military sim, but in action it's readable and almost arcade-like in its simplicity. No one does it like Infinity Ward, and even though this feels like an iteration on the last three Modern Warfare games, I won't knock the developers down for staying in their lane and developing a formula that works.

And that brings us to DMZ. Initially, I was cynical. 'This is Call of Duty chasing the bag', I thought. 'This is trend-chasing gone awry'. But Infinity Ward is not afraid of change, and I shouldn't be either. The extraction shooter trend shows no signs of abating, and with even Call of Duty managing to make it work within the specific arcade-cum-sim setup of its engine, I need to accept that. I don't think it's a stretch to say DMZ could be a standalone game - packed with enough to go toe-to-toe with the likes of Marathon or Arc Raiders.

Simplified controls, motion sensor functionality, deadzone/trigger customisation, vibration/trigger effects, reduce tinnitus sound, audio focus (PS5 only), mono audio options, HUD customisation, motion blur/DoF customisation, colour filters, camera movement reduction options, aim assist, aiming sensitivity, movement/sprint assists, FoV sliders.

The Hajin city map, smartly repurposed from the campaign, is expansive and packed with nuance, and the tasks you're given within the city limits are quick and painless - more than I can say of Marathon. You can easily and somewhat permanently get cash that persists even if you die, you can sacrifice max health to 'revive' from a down-but-not-out state, and you get XP for doing just about anything. The result is an extraction shooter experience that feels more casual and agreeable to the average FPS enjoyer; this isn't a 'lifestyle game' like other extraction shooters. Even if it does have its own pseudo-stealth system, for some reason.

In many ways, DMZ emulates what Activision did with Warzone: a wrangling of a complicated formula that makes sense within the uber-specific Call of Duty ecosystem. It's a great interpretation of the PvPvE formula that's been slowly but surely evolving over the past decade or so, and I think this is where DMZ finds its niche: Arc Raiders has become more about the PvE side of things (at least in my experience) and Marathon and Tarkov go hard on the PvP. DMZ somehow treads the line between both and, as time goes on and Infinity Ward monitors the mode and how people engage with it, I imagine its niche will only become more distinct compared to its genre stable-mates. We're already seeing some changes bleed into the game, and it's not even been out a week - it bodes well for the future.

Modern Warfare 4 represents the highest tide for Call of Duty in seven years. Infinity Ward needed to pull off a balancing act I don't envy; injecting just enough nostalgia into a series propped up with so many conventions and expectations there's barely room for anything new, alongside enough innovation and intrigue to keep people interested. Beyond that, after several weaker years in a row, Black Ops 7 in particular caused enough reputation damage that even the most loyal CoD fanatics started to see through the series' aging veneer. And so, even if the campaign occasionally struggles under the weight of legacy, and the multiplayer is happy to keep rolling on the same wheels Modern Warfare has been using for four games now, Infinity Ward has succeeded - after four years away from the helm - in saving Call of Duty. Modern Warfare 4 is a course correction sorely needed, and deftly deployed.

A copy of the game was provided for this review by Activision Blizzard.

#10636. Delivery driver goes viral throwing coffee at customer after delivering it to her

A delivery driver has been put on blast in a viral TikTok after being confronted by a customer as he was seemingly trying to steal a coffee that he had just delivered.

When delivery drivers drop off parcels, food, or anything else you’ve ordered online, they typically have to snap a photo to prove that it has been delivered. However, there have been cases where orders are stolen from people’s porches too.

In the case of Karmen Karma, the TikToker put a delivery driver called Darian on blast after he appeared to be trying to steal her coffee order on November 3. “Your coffee is right here, I don’t want your $4 coffee,” the driver said before throwing the cup at the TikToker.

“He delivered coffee to my front door, took a photo, and then went running back to his car with my coffee,” Karmen explained. “Tried to drive off fast but got stuck in a cul-de-sac, so on his way back down my street he decided to do this with my two small children present next to me. Also, tell Darian my coffee was $19.70.”

A number of commenters urged Karmen to provide proof to the delivery service as a way to get the driver punished in some way.

She did just that a few hours later, showing footage from her doorbell of Darian dropping the coffee off and snapping a photo of the delivery.

In that video, the driver can be seen speeding off too with the TikToker also in view, recording him doing so. Karmen added that she had tipped 33% of the cost of the order too.

Some viewers argued that there is “more to the story,” while others are completely on the TikToker’s side.

#10634. 8-bit horror Tenebris Somnia mixes weird live-action horror with a retro 2D adventure to impressive and unsettling effect

I've been captivated by the idea of wrong-footing an audience ever since playing Faith: The Unholy Trinity. That game presents like an 8-bit horror, then proceeds to bend the rules of what an 8-bit game can do - or perhaps what you remember they could do - in order to surprise and scare you. Tenebris Somnia is very similar. Indeed, it's executive produced by the developer of Faith, Airdorf Games, so the connection runs even deeper. Tenebris Somnia utilises the same retro aesthetic with its grinding chiptune music, its pixelated graphics, and old-school presentation. But the difference is it takes the rule-bending a step further, essentially welding two very different experiences together: a retro-styled 2D adventure and a full-on live-action film. And it's horrific, in all the right ways.

To begin with, Tenebris Somnia is a slow, plodding, fairly unremarkable affair that plays a bit like a point-and-click adventure. You're a young woman on her way to her former boyfriend's apartment, checking it out because you suspect something has happened there. You move from screen to screen, examining points of interest as you pass them, collecting odds and ends - an empty bottle, a mysterious book - and storing them in your inventory for later. Eventually, there's a locked door, and you'll have to piece together the scattered clues in order to unlock it. And despite the apparent urgency of the situation - the carnage you find in your ex-boyfriend's apartment - the pace is slow; deliberately old-school in its approach. Walk back and forth, examine everything, try "combining" items in your inventory, "use" them on the environment. Did it work? No? Try something else.

But this is where Tenebris Somnia begins to distinguish itself: your busywork is often rewarded with a glossy live-action sequence - clips of real actors in impressive costume and make-up enacting key moments from the unfolding adventure. A person with what appears to be two faces, one inverted, rushes out of the darkness toward you. A strange woman suddenly appears in a lift, offering you a gift. Two waxen figures embrace each other in bed. A giant lady with a hole in her face stretches towards the ceiling. It's captivatingly weird and very well-produced, finished with a level of polish that's impressive in an otherwise lo-fi indie experience such as this. And there's a reason for this: the live-action scenes are made by filmmaker Andrés Borghi, who's worked on a number of films including acclaimed Argentinian horror When Evil Lurks. Borghi has also made games of his own, so there's a natural overlap and understanding there.

It's these live-action sequences that stand Tenebris Somnia apart, that bring another, unusual dimension to it. That pixelated creature rushing toward you in the hallway: it has more substance in your mind now you've seen it up close. That enigmatic lady in a cocktail dress: you know the contours of her face and the strange demonic symbols branded there. You know the world better, more intimately now, and that gives it more impact. These live-action moments also create a kind of propulsive anticipation, knowing that when you do solve a puzzle or unlock that mysterious door, lavish reward and revelation awaits you there.

But I have my doubts about the game overall. Granted, I'm only early on, so perhaps I'm yet to really settle in. But I find its plodding stop-start rhythm works against the suspense and horror. Laboriousness deflates the tension. Frequently revisiting areas robs them of their surprises. And I haven't found much inherent interest in exploring new areas of the game; the writing feels functional to me rather than intriguing, and that's a problem, because in the adventure game sequences it has to do so much of the heavy lifting.

But I remain hopeful. I've watched a little more of Tenebris Somnia than I've managed to play, and the movie-making story at its heart - one focusing on a horror short of your ex's creation - appears to deepen and circle back on itself in interesting ways. Everything you do somehow seems tied to that movie - around deals with devilish entities and the grotesque consequences they bring. That filmic motif repeats again and again. And who knows? Perhaps the live-action sequences represent more than the things our character is experiencing. So there's intrigue there, depth, thought, layers, and, by the looks of it, plenty of imagination to support it.

#10633. Physicists discover “astonishing” property of light that defies expectations

Scientists have discovered a surprising ability of light that allows it to develop spinning patterns as it moves through empty space, without relying on special lenses, mirrors, or materials. The finding reveals that light possesses a hidden geometric structure that can influence its behavior in ways researchers previously thought were difficult to achieve.

The discovery comes from an international team led by researchers at the University of East Anglia (UEA) in the United Kingdom, working with scientists in South Africa. Their experiments demonstrate that carefully prepared light can be controlled through its own internal geometry, offering a new approach to manipulating how light spins and twists.

The findings challenge long-standing assumptions about how different properties of light interact. They could eventually lead to more sensitive medical tests, faster and more secure communications, and advanced quantum technologies that use light to transmit and protect information.

Why the Direction of Light's Spin Matters

One of the most intriguing aspects of the discovery involves chirality, a scientific term describing the difference between left-handed and right-handed structures.

Human hands provide a familiar example. Although they are mirror images of each other, a left hand cannot be perfectly superimposed on a right hand. Many molecules have a similar property, including compounds used in medicines. Two versions of the same molecule can have nearly identical chemical structures yet produce very different effects in the body.

Scientists can distinguish these molecular forms by studying how they interact with light that has a particular rotational behavior. Traditionally, producing and controlling such light has often involved specialized materials, carefully designed surfaces, or powerful lenses that focus beams extremely tightly.

The research demonstrates that appropriately prepared light can develop this behavior naturally as it travels, without requiring those additional optical components.

"Our work shows that light can naturally develop this handed behavior all on its own," said Dr. Kayn Forbes from UEA's School of Chemistry, Pharmacy and Pharmacology.

"You just have to prepare it in the right way.

"Most people think of light as traveling in straight lines. But scientists can also create structured light -- light whose brightness, shape, and direction are carefully arranged.

"One extreme example is light that twists as it travels, forming a corkscrew shape known as an optical vortex. Each twist can carry information, making this kind of light valuable for high-speed internet, secure communications, and advanced sensors.

"Light can also spin as it travels, depending on how it is polarized. This spin can be left-handed or right-handed -- another form of chirality."

Scientists Discover How Light Develops a Hidden Spin

Light can exhibit different kinds of rotational behavior. An optical vortex, for example, has a wave pattern that twists around its direction of travel, much like a corkscrew. Light also has a property known as polarization, which describes how its electric field oscillates. Certain polarization patterns produce a form of rotation called spin.

Physicists have long studied the relationship between these two properties. However, the interactions responsible for the newly observed effect were generally considered extremely weak and difficult to detect without specialized experimental conditions.

The researchers found that the situation changes when a beam of light is prepared in a particular balanced configuration.

Under those conditions, the beam can initially have no spin, yet distinct regions with spinning behavior emerge as it travels through free space. The effect does not require the light to pass through engineered materials or additional optical devices.

"It starts off with no spin at all," explained MSc student Light Mkhumbuza, who carried out key experiments. "But as the beam travels forward, spinning regions appear and separate out -- almost as if the spin was hiding and then revealed itself."

Rather than creating spin through an external optical component, the researchers demonstrated how the beam's existing internal structure can determine where spinning regions develop during propagation.

To explain this unexpected behavior, the researchers turned to topology, a mathematical field concerned with properties that remain unchanged when objects are continuously stretched, bent, or reshaped.

Dr. Isaac Nape, from the University of the Witwatersrand in Johannesburg, South Africa, offered a simple way to understand the concept.

"To explain it, imagine a mug and a doughnut," he said. "You can morph one into the other without tearing it, because they both have one hole. That hole is a topological feature."

The researchers found that structured light possesses its own topological characteristics, encoded in the arrangement of its polarization.

This underlying pattern acts like a mathematical fingerprint. Even as the light beam changes shape while traveling, its topological structure remains, influencing how different regions of the beam evolve.

As a result, the arrangement of the light itself can determine where its left-handed and right-handed spinning patterns become visible.

The discovery gives scientists a way to guide this behavior by designing the beam's initial structure, rather than depending on specialized materials to produce the desired effect.

"This gives us a completely new tuning knob for light. By adjusting its topology, we can decide how and where chirality appears," said Dr. Nape.

Potential Breakthroughs in Medicine and Communications

The ability to control light through its geometry could eventually make several important optical technologies simpler, more affordable, and more versatile.

One particularly promising application involves medical diagnostics and pharmaceutical research. Because many biologically important molecules have left-handed and right-handed forms, carefully structured light could help scientists identify these differences with greater sensitivity. Such tools could be valuable for evaluating drug safety and detecting biological markers associated with disease.

The same principles could support smaller optical sensors capable of identifying chemicals or biological substances without requiring large, sophisticated laboratory instruments.

Communications technology could also benefit. By encoding information into different twisting and spinning patterns, researchers may be able to increase the amount of data carried by individual light beams. This could contribute to faster optical communications, improved information security, and future quantum networks.

Other potential uses include manipulating microscopic particles, cells, and molecules with light, as well as developing more resilient quantum systems. Topological properties may help protect delicate quantum information against certain forms of noise and interference.

"The implications are wide-ranging," said Dr. Forbes. "This work could lead to simpler and more sensitive medical tests, especially in drug development.

"It could also be used to pack more information into laser beams -- boosting data capacity for communications, including future quantum networks.

"And because the effect doesn't rely on fragile materials or precision-engineered surfaces, it could be easier and cheaper to use in real-world technologies.

"This research could lay the foundations for a new generation of light-based technologies, by showing that light's behavior can be controlled using its own internal geometry," he added.

These applications remain possibilities for future development, but the underlying discovery provides researchers with another way to design and control the behavior of light.

A Hidden Property of Light Finally Revealed

For decades, scientists have relied on increasingly sophisticated optical equipment to produce and manipulate unusual forms of light. The findings suggest that some of these behaviors can instead arise from carefully designed structures already encoded within a light beam.

By showing how topology governs the emergence of spinning regions during propagation, the team has revealed a previously underappreciated connection between light's geometry and its physical behavior.

The discovery also illustrates how much remains to be learned about something as familiar and extensively studied as light.

"For something so familiar, light is proving to be far richer, stranger and more powerful than anyone imagined," said Dr. Forbes.

"And astonishingly, this new behavior has been there all along -- just waiting to be seen."

The research, 'Topological Control of Chirality and Spin with Structured Light', was published in the journal Light: Science & Applications.

Materials provided by University of East Anglia. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10632. A Practical Workflow for Testing JavaScript Forms Before Deployment

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Web forms are an essential part of many applications. They allow users to register accounts, submit contact requests, update profiles, and complete purchases. However, a form that appears to work correctly during development can still fail when users enter unexpected values, lose their connection, or submit information multiple times.

Testing forms before deployment helps developers identify these problems early. Instead of relying only on a successful submission, it is useful to test validation, browser behavior, accessibility, and error handling as separate parts of the workflow.

Before writing tests, define what the form should do from the user's perspective. A registration form, for example, might require a valid email address, a password that meets specific rules, and confirmation that the user has accepted the terms.

List the expected outcomes for each field. Consider valid input, empty values, incorrect formats, and unusually long entries. This gives you a clear checklist for testing the form without depending on assumptions about how users will interact with it.

It is also worth considering what happens after submission. Does the form display a confirmation message? Are users informed when something goes wrong? Can they correct their input without entering everything again?

These details help establish the expected behavior before testing begins.

HTML provides several built-in attributes that handle common validation requirements. The required attribute prevents submission when a mandatory field is empty, while type="email" checks whether an email address follows an accepted format.

This approach reduces the amount of JavaScript needed for basic validation. It also provides a useful starting point for testing browser behavior.

However, client-side validation should not be treated as a security measure. Users can bypass browser checks, so the server must validate submitted data independently before processing or storing it.

Developers can also explore SitePoint's HTML and responsive web design guide to understand how form elements fit into layouts across different screen sizes.

JavaScript becomes useful when a form requires custom rules or more detailed feedback. For example, a password field might need to meet a minimum length, or two fields might need to contain matching values.

When testing this logic, check both successful and unsuccessful cases. A common mistake is to test only valid submissions, leaving error conditions unexamined.

Make sure that error messages explain what users need to change. A message such as "Please enter a valid email address" is more useful than a generic notification stating that the submission failed.

Also check that validation does not unexpectedly clear fields or prevent users from correcting mistakes. Error messages should be associated with the relevant inputs so that keyboard and assistive technology users can identify the problem.

Browser developer tools help identify problems that are not immediately visible in the interface. The Console panel can reveal JavaScript errors, while the Network panel helps developers inspect requests sent when a form is submitted.

For example, a form might display a loading indicator but never show a result because its API request returns an error. Inspecting the request status, response, and timing can help distinguish a frontend problem from a backend failure.

The MDN guide to browser developer tools explains how to inspect page elements, debug JavaScript, and investigate browser behavior.

Test situations such as an unavailable server, a delayed response, and invalid server-side data. The interface should communicate the problem clearly and provide a reasonable next step.

A form should remain usable across different devices and input methods. Test it with a keyboard to confirm that users can move between fields, activate buttons, and identify validation errors without a mouse.

Every input should have a clear label. Placeholder text alone is not a reliable replacement because it disappears when users begin typing.

Next, test the form at several viewport widths. Check that labels remain readable, buttons are accessible, and fields do not extend beyond their containers. Long error messages should not overlap other elements or break the page layout.

Automated accessibility checks can identify some issues, but manual testing is still important for understanding the actual user experience.

A simple checklist makes form testing more consistent across projects:

Check server errors and slow network responses.

Confirm that repeated submissions are handled appropriately.

Test keyboard navigation and accessible error messages.

Verify the layout on mobile and desktop screens.

For larger applications, these checks can be supplemented with automated unit and integration tests. Automation is particularly useful when the same validation rules must work consistently across multiple forms.

Reliable form testing involves more than confirming that a submit button works. Developers need to consider validation, server responses, accessibility, responsive layouts, and unexpected user behavior.

By combining HTML validation, targeted JavaScript tests, browser developer tools, and a repeatable checklist, teams can identify problems before deployment. The result is a more dependable interface that helps users complete tasks without unnecessary confusion or frustration.

#10631. What we've been playing - "I've been intrigued by horrible images of live-action sequences for weeks"

A few of the things that have us hooked this week.

Happy Halloween! Are you scared? I am: I'm in a theatre play that opens next week and we're not ready yet. I'm also off to a ghost stories evening at the theatre tonight; I've had a few people there tell me, with faces as straight as a ruler, of their eerie encounters. Actually, one encounter involved me.

I was in a two-hander there, a play with only two people in it, and I got to the changing room one evening to see a concerned look on the face of the other person in the show. "Have you only just got here?" he asked. "Yes," I said in response. And at this, he blanched. He's a gruff sort of fellow with a streetwise sensibility: not a person I would associate with being easily scared. But he was. "I was just talking to you," he said. "You made the curtain move as you walked past." I remember the hairs on my arms and neck standing up, and I haven't been as confident sitting in that dressing room alone since.

But back to games! This, as you know, is our regular feature where we talk about what we've been playing this week. This week, Marie cooks with Death; Connor revisits a game that terrified him as a teenager; and Bertie is a little bit disappointed by a new indie horror.

But enough about us, what about you? What have you been playing this week? Do tell all! And don't forget we have an entire archive of previous What We've Been Playings for your leisurely perusal, just in case you forgot what you were playing a year or a month or a week ago (it happens). And now onward!

My supernatural farm is coming together very slowly, and I do mean slowly. I'm not in a rush this time - my many restarts of Stardew Valley have taught me to be patient while building or I'll want to wipe everything and start afresh. Also, I do not do scary games in the slightest, so this is my version of a halloween-themed entry, and I am fine with that.

So: my current farm contains a small livestock barn, a vegetable patch, a rather fetching vineyard next to some kegs, and I've just started to renovate the lower level by the beach. What that will be is yet to be decided, but the slow progress isn't bothering me as much as I thought it would. In fact, being unable to race through it is making it more enjoyable.

Also, watching Death learning to cook was not on my 2026 bingo card, but I am adamant a friendship will form from the literal ashes of his creations.

Given we're in the Halloween season and the site has been diving into all things scary, I wanted to pop back and play the game that kicked off a horror revival of sorts when I was almost a teenager: Amnesia!

Like many my age, my first exposure to Amnesia was through YouTube playthroughs back in the Let's Play early years. I didn't actually touch it myself until years later, dipping my toes in the game and swiftly pulling them back out, frightened.

Now I'm older and hardened by adult life, I wanted to give it another go and see how horror games have evolved over time. While they certainly have come a way since 2010, Amnesia holds up! It's well worth playing if you want a blast from the past.

I'm a bit disappointed. I've been intrigued by horrible images of live-action sequences from Tenebris Somnia for weeks, anticipating what I believed would be a terrifying experience. But a couple of hours into the game, I haven't had that. I've written about this at more length in a Tenebris Somnia preview, but suffice to say the adventure game half of Tenebris Somnia lets it down a bit, I think. However, I'm still impressed; I'm impressed by people approaching horror - and games - in these interesting ways.

It also reminds me strongly of Faith: The Unholy Trilogy, which is another, closely related 8-bit horror game that really is terrifying. It's tighter and meaner, and its crazed, hand-drawn MS Paint cinematics are worth seeing up close for yourself.

What a spooky week it's been! After Monday's trawl into some of our favourite scary games, I delved - some 30 years after my original encounter - back into pioneering survival horror Ecstatica, dongs and all. Since then, we've had post-Soviet grimness, minty fresh breath and misery, plus Bertie straddling the line between retro pixel art and retro FMV.

In amongst all that (and a bit of Silver Pines), I've squeaked in some time with The Killing Stone. It's very early days, but I'm already intrigued by its distinctive mix of roguelike deck-builder and first-person narrative horror, especially given the folkloric aspects of it all as you, a Scandinavian witch's apprentice, reckon with previous dealings with the devil.

It's fascinating so far, with an unusual card-based combat system that plays out across a single line, meaning placement matters as you attempt to snuff out your opponent's orb at the far end by battling through - or around - its guards. Each of your units can be augmented with spells and abilities placed strategically, and adjacently, outside of the battlefield, introducing even more possibilities. And I especially love the fact you can play the whole thing in modern or ye olde English, the strong voice cast making fine work of both.

I'm excited to explore more, then, but with Halloween here I've other engagements to see through first. Two annual movie traditions: Trick 'r Treat, and then - as someone old enough to have been scarred by it the first time around - controversial BBC classic Ghostwatch, with Michael Parkinson, Craig Charles, and Sarah Greene. Over three decades later it remains remarkably effective, and that bedroom Pipes reveal still haunts me.
