#10629. Silver Pines is a great survival horror throwback with an unexpected Metroidvania twist, but it makes me glad the genre's moved on

It's those bloody typewriters all over again. Developer Wych Elm Games' Silver Pines is fantastic in a whole bunch of ways, from its woozy small-town ambience to its mix of taut survival horror and exploratory Metroidvania. But from the very first moment I saw its save system, its please-insert-coin phone box demands, I knew our relationship was going to be a fractious one.

Silver Pines begins, however, in the gloom of a 1950s diner; lights off, windows boarded, as the titular town awaits an oncoming storm. You are private investigator Red Walker, but your jittery demeanour and Valium drawl suggests you're equally oblivious to the whos, whats, and whys of it all. Out in the parking lot, a phone box rings; there's cryptic talk of a missing musician and a motel room. Then, the scene set, it's onward into the heart of the town.

Silver Pines dabbles in neo-noir and psychological horror, but its debt to David Lynch - Twin Peaks in particular - is unmistakable. If the 50s diner opener wasn't enough of a tip-off, it's not long before the town's cast of beautifully rotoscoped oddballs begin making themselves known; enigmatic conversations unfurling in awkward non-sequiters. Then later: as Red passes through a suspicious door, reality seems to bend entirely, taking him to a dingy nightclub bar somewhere out of time. There's no talking giant, but we do get a song.

Twin Peaks' iconography has been borrowed and repackaged so much in the 36 years since its landmark arrival, it's hard to pay it homage without ringing the creative death knell. So it's testament to Silver Pines that, for all its hat-tips, it still manages to find a mood that feels like its own. Granted, its one that exists at the intersection David Lynch and Silent Hill (itself indebted to Twin Peaks) but the town's ambience - a kind of dishevelled melancholy - is an affecting one. Its world, built from layers upon layers of strikingly painterly 2D artwork, is drab and desolate; perpetually dank and lost in artfully controlled shadow. Its sorrowful monstrosities shimmer like a suggestion. And beneath the wind and the rain and the wailing of lost souls lies an ambient synthline so slight the soundscape skirts close to ASMR.

That, though, is entirely fitting for an adventure whose pace is such a languid one; as woozily unfocused as Red seems to be when he first picks up that phone. That's not a criticism though. Silver Pines isn't afraid to create space for its insidious ambience to slither in. There are pockets of staccato violence, absolutely, but between the deliberately stilted gunplay and awkwardly stumbling melee its primary rhythms are exploratory ones.

Which is where the Metroidvania stylings come in. Silver Pines, the town, is a vast, interconnected space: drizzly exteriors lead to gloomy interiors that branch out to new pathways or double back to previously impassable ones (you'll be hearing Red muttering "The door opens from the other side!" in your dreams). Sometimes you'll stumble across a perfectory puzzle, but progress most often requires identifying which part of the map, perhaps a location first seen much earlier, might succumb to new tools: a boxcutter that opens up passageways sealed with plastic; a pistol that can shatter rusty padlocks. And a shout-out to one of my favourite underused Metroidvania mechanics - the ability to photograph points of interest and pin them to your map, serving as reminders for future you.

It all makes for a haltingly ambulatory flow that suits and serves the quieter mood. But truthfully, having hit the five-hour mark, I'm finding the whole thing a bit of a struggle as I grapple with its intentional throwback design. Beneath Silver Pines' sumptuously bleak presentation lies an ancient heart. And while I'm certainly not criticising a deliberately old-school adventure for playing it old-school, it is making me thankful the genre's moved on.

Silver Pines, like most survival horror, builds tension through scarcity and impermanence, offering thrilling glimpses of power only to take them all away again: bullets are limited, melee weapons break, and the resources needed to restore them are equally few. Without all that, your only hope of survival is a stumbling duck-and-weave. But Silver Pines spins a particularly punishing web, tying everything to the finite number of coins left out in the world. Ammo, duct tape, health items, and so on are rare finds on the map, but comparatively plentiful in vending machines. However, coins are also the currency you'll need for phone box saves, meaning every choice you make feels even more perilous; the perpetual threat of a death spiral.

And the truth is, as much as I love survival horror, I've always hated limited saves; not just the push-your-luck stresses they invoke as I'm forced to decide if I dare risk - for instance - my last Resident Evil typewriter ribbon, but the steadily deflating atmosphere when my luck runs out and I've got to do the same section all over again. It's a problem modern survival horror still grapples with, of course, but, by jettisoning the arbitrary restrictions of limited save currency, they're able to manage a more sustained momentum.

And by binding its survival systems so tightly - the threat of weapons failure, the threat of death - under the guise of coins, Silver Pines has made me even more obsessive about my saves, even more cautious about frivolous spending. And it's turned mood into mechanisms. I'm min-max route plotting, enemy tracking, bullet counting, and limiting my box cutter swings - so much energy spent on mental spreadsheet-building I'm no longer immersed in the mystery of the thing. And the open-ended structure does rather exacerbate the issue: its long, slow stretches of nothing followed by unexpected deaths make redoing bits particularly wearying.

Of course, it's not Silver Pines' fault it's igniting my old-school survival horror PTSD. And I'm fully aware there are sickos out there who love this kind of thing; the queasily unshakeable threat of spiralling disaster and the harsh punishment when calculations are off and disaster finally comes. But you know what? I certainly respect Silver Pines, even if we don't always get along. It's a gorgeously woozy exercise in mood, with a fishing line mystery that - for all my grumbles - just keeps pulling me on. Not for nothing is its enigmatic bar known as The Rabbit Hole, and as the search for Eddie Velvet continues, here I am; lost in the warrens again.

Nintendo Switch, Nintendo Switch 2, PC, PS5, Xbox Series X/S

#10628. AI can act exactly like a human, but we still don't think it's conscious

Even when artificial intelligence behaves exactly like a human, people are reluctant to consider it conscious, according to a new study from Ludwig Maximilian University of Munich (LMU).

As artificial intelligence becomes more advanced, reports of AI systems misleading users, working together, or appearing to pursue independent goals are becoming increasingly common. These behaviors often lead people to describe AI as though it can think, feel, or make decisions for itself. But does behaving like a human actually convince people that a machine has consciousness?

New research from LMU suggests that people make a clear distinction between displaying intelligent behavior and possessing a conscious mind, even when humans and AI act in precisely the same way.

How Scientists Tested Perceptions of AI Consciousness

Published in the journal Cognition, the study was led by Dr. Louis Longin of LMU's Chair of Philosophy of Mind, working alongside Dr. Bahador Bahrami, Professor Ophelia Deroy, and other researchers.

"Whereas previous studies have typically asked general questions about whether AI actually has mental states, our study is the first to directly compare how people attribute the same mental states to AI and humans behaving in exactly the same way, under identical circumstances," says Longin.

"We may be using mental terms to refer to AI, but this may only be because we lack better words," says Ophelia Deroy, philosopher and a senior author of the study. "After all, AI is trained to act and speak like a human, so these descriptions seem natural."

To investigate how people judge consciousness, the researchers conducted experiments involving nearly 1,100 participants. One group read brief descriptions of artificial agents that showed different levels of responsiveness to their environments. For example, some agents reacted to sounds or recognized the emotional states of others.

Another group encountered the same situations, except the characters were described as humans rather than AI systems. Participants then evaluated how conscious or aware each character seemed to be of its surroundings.

A Surprising Gap Between Human and AI Consciousness

The experiments revealed that responsiveness influenced how participants judged both humans and artificial intelligence. Characters that reacted more actively to their surroundings were generally considered more conscious and more aware.

However, a striking difference emerged when participants evaluated consciousness specifically. Even the most responsive AI was rated as less conscious than the least responsive human. In other words, identical behavior did not lead people to assign the same level of consciousness to humans and machines.

The results changed considerably when participants were asked about awareness instead. For this characteristic, judgments of humans and AI followed largely similar patterns, suggesting that people interpret awareness and consciousness in fundamentally different ways.

"There is a growing worry in public discussion that people will see AI behaving in a human-like way and start treating these systems as if they had human-like mental states," says Longin, lead author of the study. "We found something much more nuanced: People are quite willing to say that an AI notices things and is aware of its surroundings. But they clearly draw a line when it comes to the term 'consciousness'. People consistently attribute less consciousness to AI than humans -- even when their behavior is identical."

Although people sometimes describe AI as conscious when discussing its actions, the findings indicate that they still distinguish between artificial systems and human minds.

The researchers believe the difference between consciousness and awareness offers an important insight into how people understand artificial intelligence.

Awareness is a relatively neutral term that can describe an agent's ability to detect and respond to information in its environment. Consciousness, however, carries deeper implications about subjective experience and what it means to have an inner mental life.

Because awareness focuses more directly on observable behavior, people appear comfortable applying it to both humans and AI. Consciousness carries a different meaning, leading people to judge the two much less similarly.

The findings also highlight why the language used to discuss artificial intelligence matters, particularly as AI systems become increasingly capable of producing convincing human behavior.

"Our study teaches us lessons for how we communicate about AI," says Bahador Bahrami, who is also a senior author of the study and an expert in social neuroscience. "Companies and journalists often reach for hyped descriptions of AI models, and credit them with intentions, plans, or even moral conscience and hesitation. What our study shows is that everyday judgments are much more discriminating: People do not simply put AI and humans on the same mental scale. But the boundary also depends on which terms we use. For more neutral descriptors such as 'awareness', the distinction can largely disappear. That makes our choice of language important."

#10626. Winnie-the-Pooh turned into anime girl for new psychological horror game

A new psychological horror Steam game has transformed Winnie-the-Pooh and his companions into anime girls, putting a unique spin on Christopher Robin’s adventures through the Hundred Acre Wood.

When you think of Winnie-the-Pooh, your mind probably conjures up images of the cute, cuddly bear with a crippling addiction to honey. However, in a move not too dissimilar to Umamusume turning real-life racehorses into anime girl idols, a horror visual novel has transformed Pooh and his pals into waifus (words I’m still struggling to come to terms with).

Yes, Piglet, Eeyore, Rabbit, and co. have all been given an anime girl makeover, and according to the Steam Store page, the visual novel has been tagged as having psychological horror and dating sim elements.

Winnie-the-Pooh: Honey Distortion is an upcoming point-and-click mystery visual novel that has been inspired by A. A. Milne’s stories. Players take on the role of Christopher Robin as they explore the Hundred Acre Wood, unravel mysteries, and make decisions that can lead to different endings.

As shown in the screenshots, Honey Distortion has transformed every character into an anime girl. Winnie-the-Pooh retains the character’s signature red top, but now comes with some added short shorts, while Rabbit is shown wearing a bunny girl outfit.

Meanwhile, Piglet is shown sporting a textured black hairstyle with pink streaks and a matching pink sweater. She’s also shown holding a handgun in one scene, threatening to blow Christopher Robin’s head off (I guess this is where the horror elements come in?).

Ironmouse designs official Hatsune Miku outfit for Halloween

Winnie the Pooh slasher movie gets first wild trailer

Peter Pan horror movie to feature “obese” drug addict Tinkerbell

It’s certainly one of the more unique ways to celebrate 100 years of Winnie-the-Pooh, and there’s currently a free demo available if you happen to be curious. Winnie-the-Pooh: Honey Distortion is currently planned for release in early 2027 on PC.

#10625. The Mortuary Assistant dev on laying his cancelled Paranormal Activity game to rest, resurrecting it as a Halloween freebie, and a potential return to his biggest game

"[It] makes me feel like it was not all done in vain."

Halloween feels like an especially appropriate time for Playable Archive's arrival. It's a genuinely spooky found-footage affair for one - a cleverly orchestrated 30-or-so minutes of scares from the developer of horror hit The Mortuary Assistant, released this week as a Halloween freebie. But more than that, the unusual tale of its creation is also one of death and resurrection. As its slightly cheeky title implies, Playable Archive (a riff on the legendary Playable Teaser created for Hideo Kojima's cancelled Silent Hill game) is a small slice of developer Brian Clarke's much larger cancelled Paranormal Activity game, making it a fascinating window into 'what could have been'. When I heard the news of its release, my curiosity was piqued, so I spoke with Clarke about this unexpected turn of events, of laying his project to rest, and how its legacy might live on - perhaps even as a new Mortuary Assistant game.

Playable Archive's story begins sometime after the launch of The Mortuary Assistant in 2022, when Clarke was approached by publisher DreadXP to gauge his interest in developing a game set in the universe of the Paranormal Activity movies. "I was like, sure, I'd be down to do that," Clarke tells me over video call, "because I love Paranormal Activity. It's one of my favourite horror films." One thing led to another and Clarke found himself submitting a design and pitch deck to Paramount. And the company clearly liked it; amid a major management shakeup at DreadXP, the "slow process of contracts" began, and the game would eventually be announced as Paranormal Activity: Threshold in November last year.

For Clarke, Paranormal Activity was a good fit for his preferred story-first approach to horror: one less about cinematic scares that seize control of the camera, and more about "passive" chills; moments where something happens which might not be obvious, and which many players might even miss, giving them slightly different experiences they'll have fun sharing. "I think the flavour I like to do," he explains, "is something that feels like you're driving the experience as opposed to me just putting you on rails and showing you."

And the project felt like an opportunity to add depth to a series that so far hasn't delved much further than spooky houses and demonic possession, in a way Clarke particularly enjoys. "I love lore - demonic lore was a big thing in The Mortuary Assistant - [and so] I built a whole demonology for Paranormal Activity, and built out this whole new concept that still tied in with everything from the films," he recalls. "I was really excited to do this deeper thing ... that maybe could be used for other things in the future, other games, films, you know."

The vestiges of Clarke's original pitch and his broader ambitions can still be seen in Playable Archive. His take on Paranormal Activity would follow a couple as they moved into a new home - presented using the movie series' familiar found-footage style - with events gradually taking a supernatural turn. Except, at a key point, the perspective would shift, and we'd learn we're watching this old camcorder footage through the eyes of a detective, investigating the house following the couple's unexplained demise. In the planned full-length version of the game, the action would jump between the two timelines as more tapes are found, and information learned in one time period would help players progress in the other. However, a third perspective would reveal "something has been set in motion outside of these two".

Clarke, who works as a solo developer under his DarkStone Digital label, had already spent around two years developing the Paranormal Activity project before it made its PAX East debut earlier this year. And the feedback to his PAX demo - more or less the exact same slice that's now been tidied up for public release as Playable Archive - was extremely positive. But things were about to take a turn. With Paramount's deadline approaching, it was clear there was still work to do, and Clarke requested a "small extension". Not just to finish the game, but to complete QA, localisation, and porting to get Paranormal Activity ready for a simultaneous release. But Paramount, perhaps eager for a Halloween release this year, said no.

"It was really a stressful time because I had put a lot of pressure on myself for the project and I was pouring a lot of myself into it," Clarke recalls, noting that, for him, quality is everything - "I feel it shows when people play any of my projects". With the likes of additional mo-cap, voice acting, and editing work still to be done in order to complement Paranormal Activity's completed mechanics, he was concerned rushing to meet Paramount's inflexible deadline would leave the second half lacking after investing so much time in the earlier part of the game. "That sort of thing … feels much more damaging to an individual [developer's reputation] than a big old company," he says, and so the idea of putting "something out faster, and very much compromising what the game is going to be" wasn't something Clarke wanted to entertain: "I talked to Dread … and we all kind of agreed that, yeah, if we can't [get Paramount to] budge then we'll just have to kind of end it.

"To Paramount's credit, when … we floated [the idea of ending things] they were okay with that." But notably, Clarke was permitted to keep everything he'd created for the project, which is why it's been possible to trim out the Paranormal Activity branding and release Playable Archive this Halloween. It was current DreadXP director Hunter Bond who first suggested giving the project a public send-off of sorts, according to Clarke, so it could "sort of get its flowers [and] be laid to rest". For Clarke, who was in triple-A games development for 13 years before going independent, game cancellations were far from unfamiliar. And while "it's harder when it's just my work" he felt he'd "accepted that, okay, this one's not done." But it wasn't just him: "It seemed like [the project's cancellation] was probably sitting with various people at Dread" and so the decision was made to let it live on in some small way. "And now that it's out there," says Clarke, "it feels really good to let people actually play it."

Playable Archive packs a surprising amount of tension and some killer scares into its scant 30-45 minutes of playtime, but there wasn't really ever any thought of charging for it. "Just knowing it's not the full game [it was intended to be], "explains Clarke, "I can't bring myself to be like, 'Hey, give me money for that.' It's been four years since The Mortuary Assistant came out [and] I would love to put something out that could bring a little something in, but it's just not the piece for it. And Dread felt the same way."

Playable Archive isn't quite the same experience PAX East attendees had with the Paranormal Activity demo earlier this year, though. As well as scrubbing the branding, Clarke took the opportunity to make a number of adjustments, even adding some new lore. At one point, he considered expanding it still further, incorporating found-footage segments from the larger game. In the end, though, he decided there was "no elegant way" to do it.

"I'd want to be like, no, you need to access this to use the demonic connection, and to open this," he elaborates. "And then if you access this, well, this room's art isn't finished so I'd have to finish that. So it turned into a big old can of worms. Like, if I wanted to do more - which I would have loved to have done - it just would have been a bigger time investment, at which point I might as well be trying to finish the game … So ultimately [we decided] let's just polish up and make this demo the experience so people can at least see where I was going with it and get something out of it. It's better than letting it collect dust in the dark."

But there's that elephant in the room: why not finish what he started and release the full game? The answer, Clark suggests, is a little complicated. There are the technical practicalities for one - "What I created was very much meant to tie into Paranormal Activity and so to go back and adjust all of that would be a tonne of work," Clarke explains - but there's the personal aspect too; of resurrecting a game he'd already put to rest after two years of development, and of potentially being drawn back into rhythm - "inviting an unnatural process to my normal process" - he'd already struggled with the first time around. "There're a lot of factors," says Clarke, "beyond just 'Brian doesn't want to do it because he's sad', right?"

And in the end, Clarke found himself asking a simple question: "Is it better to take the concepts that I really like, that are mine and that I got to keep, and make new stuff with it?" That, ultimately, is what felt "more valuable" to him, and since reaching that decision, he's "come up with like five different game ideas, so I'd much rather chase those."

Clarke admits, though, that he's kept a curious eye on Playable Archive's reception, and says the hugely positive response has been "beautiful" to see. "When this much time passes," he says, "I think it's really common for artists and creatives to have impostor syndrome and so it's nice to be like, 'Okay, I still know what I'm doing to some degree, right?'" But with the positive feedback comes the inevitable calls to finish the game. "And I've got to fight that urge," says Clarke, "[but] I see people play through the whole thing again and I go, 'Man, that was a good story.' Like, I know where I was going with it. I know what I wanted to do with it, and people were kind of starting to understand what I was doing."

But despite that lingering temptation, Clarke seems eager to move on, and is already juggling a number of ideas that draw on both The Mortuary Assistant and Paranormal Activity. "I've realised I've got so much here," he says. "What could I build with these? I don't want to build Playable Archive again because that exists … but can I take elements from this?" According to Clarke, there are "two games in particular" he wants to do, that he thinks will strike a chord with players, and that feature concepts recognisable from Playable Archive. "I'm going to execute them again, but with more focus on those things," he explains, pointing to Playable Archive's Ouija board and different timelines. "Those things will live on. So this whole thing, at this point, makes me feel like it was not all done in vain."

For Clarke, the positive response to Playable Archive is "bittersweet", but it's also "really been motivating. It's really made me be like, alright, it's time to roll up and do this again. After everything happened … I'm out on the other side and I want to make something." As for what that something might be, Clarke says he's "already started" work on a new Mortuary Assistant game, and is also tinkering around with a "little prototype" incorporating some of Playable Archive's mechanics. Both, he says, are proving exciting. "This is the problem with the early part of game development: everything is fun. Everything's grey-box. Nothing's anything. It's just ideas. It's really cool … So I think what I need is to get a few ideas out [and decide] do I want to pursue this or do I want to pursue this other idea? You know, whether it's Mortuary Assistant, or this other prototype, or this other [thing]."

Clarke is, though, acuately aware it's been four years since he last put out a full game. "I desperately want to make something full, get it out there, and have people playing it," he says. "So, I'm certainly motivated to have something out soon."

#10622. The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time (2026) review

I have a friend who first played The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time back in the day, when the game was only just released and he was home from university for Christmas. This is objectively the best of all times to play any video game: not Christmas specifically, but dropped out of the regular world and faced with a few weeks filled with nothing pressing to do whatsoever. Years later, though, my friend told me that the magic of the game didn't just come down to the playing of it, but the way that the playing of it unfolded.

Here's what he meant. He'd play all day, curtains drawn, family ignored, working through dungeons and pelting across Hyrule Field on Epona. Then in the evening he'd call his girlfriend, who was back with her own family for Christmas. She always wanted to know what he'd got up to that day. And not in East Sussex; she wanted to know what he'd got up to in Hyrule.

So as he played he took regular breaks to tell someone else about what he had encountered, and what he had discovered, and what he had yet to overcome. It is my bright delight today to be able to tell you that, around thirty years later, having just revisited the game in Nintendo's new remake, Ocarina of Time is still worth telling people about. It's still an adventure so surprising, so satisfying, so moving that I need to talk about it.

Here's the thing, though. Despite the game being a cast-iron masterpiece, this reaction was something of a surprise to me. Or rather, it was a surprise precisely because the game was a cast-iron masterpiece. In truth, a couple of weeks ago I wasn't really aching to head back to Ocarina of Time after all these years. I kind of dreaded having to slog through an echoing, once-great game and summon a thousand words of measured reverence afterwards.

Thankfully I had forgotten two things. The first is that I am an idiot, which means that I had failed to realise that any chance to replay a Zelda should be leapt at. And secondly, it turns out that I'd forgotten exactly what kind of masterpiece Ocarina of Time is. And along with that last point - and we'll get to this properly in a bit - I hadn't realised that there's a secret ingredient to this specific remake that has proved absolutely essential to its greatness, and that has nothing at all to do with the code running on the Switch 2.

Let's deal with remake duties here. This is more or less the game that people played back in 1998. The beats of the adventure, with Link travelling between different eras of Hyrule in order to defeat Ganon, remain the same. You still start in Kokiri Forest and you still ride Epona over the fence at Lon Lon Ranch in one of the great heart-racing, skin-prickling moments in all of art. If you're stuck in a dungeon, you can almost always use an existing guide from the N64 game to work your way through it. The game world is still broken up into little chunks that fade out and in as you move between them. And each game still begins with that unskippably evocative intro that has Link and Epona dashing over a misty hill with the moon behind them, Zelda meets Susan Cooper.

Changes though? For one thing the whole game's been updated with modern graphics, and has ditched any and all fixed cameras for a modern system of movement on one thumbstick and camera on the other. One example: Hyrule Castle Town, in my memory at least, always had a central camera and you would pivot about its streets as if you were a horse on a circus merry-go-round.

Now it's pure Frozen territory, cobbled paths racing off in every direction and you can explore them with the camera rushing behind you. The game's spaces feel less like elaborate machines that you were a mechanical part of, and more like video game spaces. Yes, from the way I've phrased that I now see that something of a trade-off has taken place here. I loved that sense you got in early 3D games that the whole thing was a clever practical trick that was forever almost about to collapse in on itself, but anyway! The new Ocarina is a game you can show kids who weren't alive in 1998 and they will feel at home more quickly.

Sometimes the graphical update is really wonderful. The route to Zora's Domain, which I barely remember in the original Ocarina, is now a lovely scramble over cliffs and arcing crags. A bold smattering of 1990s Nickelodeon colours and glossy textures, meanwhile, makes Jabu Jabu's Belly a cross between a pre-millennium rave and a modern soft play.

My favourite locations of all, however, are two of the mid-game dungeons. The Water Temple is now a drowned Art Nouveau palace that you could easily imagine Captain Nemo knocking around in. And the Forest Temple, which is my absolute favourite Zelda dungeon of all time, is a parquet-floored murder mansion that's been overtaken by trees. Dark wood-panelled corridors and weird bits of church sticking out of the foliage. It's the kind of place you'd hear a weird crime podcast about.

Trade-offs, however. My wife, a huge fan of the original game, would wander in and out as I played, and often felt the game she remembered was a little more characterful in its visuals. The moment you fight Dark Link, for example, has lost a little of its glitchy liminality, although the staging, with an old tree in the middle of a vast smoky room, is still wonderfully strange. Towards the end of the game, with the trip into Gerudo territory, you could also occasionally be forgiven for wondering if you'd stepped into one of a dozen third-person Unreal games in development at the moment. It's pleasant to look at, but it sometimes doesn't look specifically like Zelda anymore.

All that, and some strange character models that inevitably I learned to love, don't really matter very much, however. Not least because a handful of clever tweaks elsewhere mean that Ocarina now races along at an astonishing clip. And all this with a careful paring back of intrusions from Navi the fairy and the owl, who were both there in the original game to help move you along swiftly in the first place.

These tweaks are all pretty great. Link now has a jump button, which removes the original game's contextual jumps from the equation, and makes the thundering jump attack even more pleasing to pull off. Even better, in a loan from the 3DS remake of Ocarina, you can swap out usable items using a trigger mini-menu, and you can change boots and whatever you had on your head with some of the directional face buttons.

This doesn't sound like much, but it's quietly transformative in the way that the best updates are. Take the Water Temple. This is the stuff of memes in terms of difficulty and the patience required in navigating an environment that can be flooded up to three different levels. But now, as on the 3DS, you can enjoy it without having to slip into the menu every few steps to add your iron boots or remove them.

And this in turn means that a dungeon I had been really dreading turned out to be a dungeon that revealed itself via experimentation. I would change the water level and see what options I now had, think about the relationship between spaces I could reach and spaces I could not, all without needing to muddle about in menus and interrupt the flow of things. Flow is the word in fact: a simple UI and item-management tweak has given a legendary Zelda dungeon its perfect pace and sense of revelation.

Alongside tweaks like that which I noticed - oh, it's easier to equip the Hookshot - are no doubt hundreds of little things I didn't notice. I suspect there's a lot more gentle direction prompting built into places like the Water Temple, while elsewhere, in the Forest Temple, for example, I suspected that some of the backtracking had been removed. Individual item placements have been changed around perhaps, and there are generous tweaks like a wallet that holds more rupees from the off. For me at least, all of these tweaks had several things in common: they improved the game without removing or altering anything that was particularly meaningful about the original, and, also, I struggled to confidently spot them.

I did try to spot them for a while. And I measured up what I was playing against my memory, which is always wonky in the first place. Hyrule Field feels smaller now, but it's still thrilling to race across. I remember Epona entering the game sooner, or maybe I just remember the idea of a horse in a Zelda game being a huge, attention-holding deal. I thought a favourite boss involving oil paintings came much later, but again, I had just shifted it to the third act because of its original impact on me.

But this gives way over time to the most important thing about Ocarina of Time, I think, and the reason why this remake has such an emotional impact on people like me. This isn't just another Zelda with an ingenious overworld. It's a Zelda game that's even more preoccupied with time than ever before. And this is because time unfolds within the game, via a central mechanic that takes Hyrule seven years into the future, transforming Link from a child to an adult - and then allows you to go back again.

To play a game like this, a game that is about this stuff, after a period of almost thirty years away, gives your imagination and memory a kind of IMAX treatment. I realised that not only is this a game about the passage of time and the way that the world looks different when you're young to when you're a bit older - I had never really realised that adult Link goes through variations of the same dungeons that young Link went through, but now they're all just a bit harder and more consequential - it's also the kind of game that a lot of players have knowingly or unknowingly measured parts of their lives by.

This time, when I reached the first Fairy Fountain, for example, I remembered that my wife had walked down the aisle to this music - about half the registry office recognised the tune with a shiver of laughter, incidentally. Then there's the Water Temple, which has become cultural shorthand for a challenge that is really not screwing around. How many awful things had I faced in life by first conjuring the spirit of Link about to enter this place.

Players can change camera rotation speed and toggle motion-control aiming. Targeting can be controlled by holding a button or toggling a button, Ocarina can be controlled by the mic or by buttons, controller vibration can be switched on or off.

There's that first arrival in Hyrule Field, which is both a promise of everything that games were about to become in 1998 and that fleeting moment in childhood where the boundaries suddenly change. This game doesn't just send me back into my memories, then. It puts me back in conversation with all the people I have known who have ever loved it. I play it now and think I'm talking to my friend who played it at university, or my wife who loved it long before we met.

Was it always this good? Were the dungeons always so complex and challenging, but also so playful, so coherent? Were the bosses always driven by interesting ideas and drama and humour rather than difficulty? Was the world always so rich with minor characters who linger in the mind - the accordion player in the windmill, the gravedigger who has his own tour he'd really like you to try out? It's impossible for me to say at this point. This is Ocarina as it's always been, as timeless and intricate, but it also has a vibrancy and clarity that makes it feel like it was just created last Wednesday. If you're new to Zelda and you're coming to this game fresh, you're going to love it. Someone, somewhere, is about to play a game so special for the first time that they need to call up their friend and tell them all about it. But if you're returning to this game? Well, somehow you'll find that you're still coming to it fresh.

My dad always said that you should read Don Quixote three times in your life, and I only recently realised that he meant that the book changes because you change. So. When I got to the Deku Tree back at the start of Ocarina a few weeks ago, and I completed the dungeon and watched the impossible vision of the Deku Tree dying of weariness? I remembered that the first time I'd been here, almost thirty years ago, I couldn't help but identify with Link, freshly gifted a sword and shield, one dungeon down, ready for whatever came next. This time, I think, I was closer to identifying with the Deku Tree.

And here's the important part: this wasn't a depressing thought. It just meant that I had taken the long route to reach this point, and this astonishing game had taken the long route too. And yet we arrived at this point together, and together we were transformed. And that's really why any of this video game stuff matters.

A copy of The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time was provided for this review by Nintendo.

#10621. People are taking an unapproved weight-loss drug. AI found something surprising

An experimental weight-loss drug that has not yet received regulatory approval is already attracting users willing to obtain unapproved versions online. Now, scientists are using artificial intelligence to investigate their experiences, uncovering unexpected symptom reports that differ from those documented in clinical trials.

The drug, retatrutide, belongs to a new generation of GLP-1 medications designed to help regulate appetite and body weight. Although it remains under investigation and is not approved for use, people are purchasing unapproved formulations that are not intended for human consumption and discussing their experiences on internet forums.

Researchers at the University of Pennsylvania analyzed nearly 150,000 Reddit posts and comments from approximately 40,000 users to investigate these accounts. Their study, published in JAMA Network Open, provides the first systematic examination of potential side effects reported by people claiming to use unapproved retatrutide.

One finding stood out. Among users whose reported symptoms could be classified, increased appetite was the most common complaint. That was particularly unexpected because retatrutide is designed to suppress hunger, and its Phase 2 clinical trials primarily identified digestive problems as the most frequent side effects.

AI Uncovers Unexpected Weight-Loss Drug Reports

"Our goal is to identify hypotheses in online conversations that we could test. AI helps us find recurring patterns in people's self-reports, including experiences that may be missed by conventional drug-safety monitoring," says Sharath Chandra Guntuku, Research Associate Professor in Computer and Information Science (CIS), Director of Penn's Computational Social Listening Lab and senior author of the study.

However, the researchers emphasize that the findings should not be interpreted as proof of previously unknown side effects. Reddit users represent a very different population from clinical trial participants, and their accounts cannot establish whether the drug caused the symptoms they described.

There is also considerable uncertainty about what these individuals are actually taking. Products sold outside regulated medical channels may differ from the genuine experimental medication in their ingredients, purity, and dosage.

"We can't say that retatrutide is causing these symptoms, or even that Reddit users are actually taking genuine retatrutide, since the drug is not legally available, and gray market formulations vary," says Lyle Ungar, Professor in CIS and a co-author of the study.

Rather than confirming new risks, the research offers scientists an opportunity to identify questions that warrant more rigorous investigation.

"This is the first glimpse into the experiences of a population that is otherwise outside conventional drug safety monitoring because they are taking unapproved versions of a drug that has yet to finish clinical trials," says Neil Sehgal, the study's first author and a doctoral student in CIS.

How Researchers Analyzed 150,000 Reddit Posts

The project builds on previous research by the University of Pennsylvania team. In that earlier investigation, scientists used large language models (LLMs), the type of artificial intelligence that powers many modern chatbots, to examine more than 400,000 Reddit posts discussing semaglutide and tirzepatide.

That analysis revealed reports of possible side effects, including menstrual irregularities, that may not have been fully documented in clinical trials or official drug safety records.

"We wanted to apply the same methodology to gray-market retatrutide," says Sehgal.

Retatrutide is particularly interesting because it works differently from existing GLP-1 medications. Semaglutide acts on one hormone receptor involved in blood sugar control and appetite regulation, while tirzepatide targets two. Retatrutide activates three receptors, potentially producing a different combination of effects throughout the body.

To investigate what people were experiencing, the researchers used AI to examine nearly 150,000 Reddit posts and comments. They identified approximately 14,000 users who claimed to have personally taken an unapproved version of retatrutide.

The AI systems then extracted descriptions of symptoms that users associated with the medication and translated those descriptions into standardized medical terminology. This allowed researchers to compare reports even when different people used different words to describe similar experiences.

After excluding expected effects such as weight loss and reduced appetite, the team identified 7,823 users who reported at least one symptom that could be medically classified.

"A researcher might be able to examine a few thousand Reddit posts by hand, but looking at nearly 150,000 would be an enormous undertaking," says Sehgal. "AI makes it possible to analyze these conversations at scale, identifying patterns in what people are sharing that would otherwise be difficult to recognize."

The most striking difference involved hunger.

In retatrutide's Phase 2 clinical trials (the drug is currently in Phase 3 trials, the final step before FDA approval), participants most commonly experienced gastrointestinal complaints, including nausea. These effects are familiar concerns with medications that influence appetite and digestion.

But among Reddit users who reported taking unapproved retatrutide, the pattern looked different. Increased appetite emerged as the most frequently described classifiable symptom, despite the substantial weight-loss effects demonstrated in clinical testing.

The researchers also compared the new findings with their earlier analysis of semaglutide and tirzepatide.

Compared with those earlier Reddit reports, discussions involving unapproved retatrutide contained proportionally more mentions of appetite-related changes, sleep disturbances, increased heart rate, and muscle-related symptoms. Reports of nausea and other digestive complaints were proportionally less common.

These differences could reflect how retatrutide interacts with the body. However, they could also result from differences between the groups of people discussing the medications, their other drug use, or the contents of the unapproved products.

"Retatrutide works differently from existing GLP-1 drugs, so it's possible that its effects on the body are different, too," says Jena Shaw Tronieri, Senior Research Investigator at Penn's Center for Weight and Eating Disorders and a co-author of the study.

"But the people discussing it on Reddit also may be different," she notes. "Users willing to obtain an unapproved medication on the gray market likely differ from other clinical populations. Some may be taking multiple experimental peptides, and we can't verify exactly what they're taking. We need more research to understand what, if anything, these differences in self-reported symptoms mean."

Can AI Detect Drug Safety Concerns Earlier?

Beyond the findings about retatrutide, the study demonstrates how AI could potentially improve the early detection of drug safety concerns.

Traditional monitoring systems depend heavily on clinical trials, reports submitted to regulators, and information collected by healthcare professionals. People obtaining experimental medications through unapproved channels may not be represented in those systems.

Social media offers another source of information, allowing researchers to identify recurring experiences that might otherwise escape attention. However, these reports must be independently investigated before they can be considered reliable evidence of a drug-related problem.

The Penn team has already seen an example of how this approach can help guide further research.

In their previous investigation of semaglutide and tirzepatide, researchers identified online reports of menstrual irregularities. Those findings subsequently encouraged another scientific team to examine similar symptoms using the FDA's database of reported adverse drug events.

The follow-up study, published in Obstetrics & Gynecology, also detected disproportionate reports of certain menstrual changes. Although those findings did not establish causation, they provided additional reasons to investigate the potential connection.

The researchers hope their retatrutide findings will lead to similar independent studies, particularly as the drug advances through clinical development.

"That's what we hope will happen with this study," says Sehgal. "Other researchers can take what we find on Reddit and look for the same patterns in other datasets, using entirely different methods. Whether those signals hold up or not, the goal is to build a better understanding of what people are actually experiencing."

The research was conducted through the University of Pennsylvania's School of Engineering and Applied Science and Perelman School of Medicine. Benjamin Rader of Boston Children's Hospital also contributed as a co-author. Funding was provided in part by the University of Pennsylvania's Penn Medical Communication Research Institute. Tronieri disclosed receiving an investigator-initiated grant from Novo Nordisk on behalf of the University of Pennsylvania, along with consulting fees from Currax Pharmaceuticals, LLC. No conflicts of interest were reported by the other authors.

Materials provided by University of Pennsylvania School of Engineering and Applied Science. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10618. Faraway’s Unprecedented 16-Year Development Has Resulted in an Instant Classic

Annapurna's high-score chasing constellation game is a little triumph

You can’t rush perfection. So many artists have created their life’s work after decades of toiling in the lab like a mad scientist. But when it comes to video games, a laborious life cycle rarely results in a masterpiece. Due to rapidly changing tech, a game that’s in development for a decade can often wind up dropping as a Frankenstein monster built from the bones of shifting design trends and hardware changes. So it’s a miracle that Faraway, a game that’s been in development for closer to twenty years than ten, lands as a little triumph.

Faraway is the latest game from Steph Thirion, the solo developer behind 2009’s critically acclaimed iOS puzzler Eliss. (It’s published by Annapurna Interactive, the company arguably best-known for publishing offbeat games for wider audiences.) At a distance, Faraway looks like a small project: a high-score chasing arcade game about shooting stars. In reality, it’s a 16-year passion project that has finally come to fruition. Even in its one-button simplicity, Faraway feels like the kind of small-scale masterwork that so often gets overlooked in an industry that increasingly values Hollywood production over expressive gameplay.

Like a classic arcade game, Faraway doesn’t need much explanation. You control a shooting star floating through space in a straight line. When you press and hold the game’s single button while passing by a star, you’ll snag onto it and orbit around it until you let go. That allows you to change directions and build speed as you hurtle through space. You’ll need to master those fundamentals before you get to Faraway’s delightful puzzle conceit: Your goal is to create as many constellations in the sky as you can before time runs out.

As you fly around, you’ll bump into round zones that contain a bunch of stars. Using your orbiting powers, you can connect lines from one star to the next. A constellation is made by linking together as many stars as possible without intersecting with an existing line. You’ll get points for each line segment placed, but you’ll get an added score multiplier each time you create a closed loop of stars. The more complex your constellations, the more points you’ll get. Plus, you’ll add more time to your star’s short lifespan and unlock larger zones that let you make even bigger constellations as a round goes on.

It’s so easy to pick up and play, yet contains so much tricky nuance that begs for mastery. You need to learn how to launch out of an orbit to make sure you don’t bump into a line, or how to make criss-crossing shapes without bumping into any lines. A had a breakthrough early on when I realized that I could create bigger combos if I started my constellations at the center of a zone, giving me room to work outwards and loop shapes together as I worked outwards.

It can be frustrating to learn the ropes, especially as you get used to the fact that you can’t choose which star you stick onto if you’re in a cluster of them, but Thirion does a great job at easing you into the complexity. As you gain higher scores in the main mode, you’ll unlock puzzle missions that ask you to trace increasingly complicated constellations. Those missions will help you practice some tricky maneuvers and learn different shapes you can make with careful thinking. And since they’re doled out to you as your skill increases in the high-score chase mode, it always feels like you’re learning new ideas when you’re ready to graduate and take your game to the next level.

A weekly cheat sheet to what you’ll eventually love to play. No buying, no commitment — just saving for later.

Faraway is the kind of unassuming design feat that I wish was more valued in an industry increasingly enamored with narrative spectacle. It has the creative spirit of the game designer Jeff Minter’s work, minimalist games like Tempest 2000 that express so much through a focused gameplay loop and immaculate vibes. Like an abstract painting or an experimental film, Faraway has a trance-inducing quality to it that’s so easy to get lost in. It has circled my mind much longer than some of this year’s most thought-provoking narrative games.

Though Annapurna Interactive has a reputation for publishing interactive movies these days, it’s arguably at its best when it's using its sharp eye to scope out games like this. Faraway joins a pantheon of minimalist triumphs under the label, a class that includes the poetic Gorogoa and unforgettably strange Cocoon. It isn’t likely to get as much awards buzz as a story-forward game like Mixtape, but the soul-nourishing constellation game deserves its flowers for being every bit as expressive by barely saying a word.

Faraway will be released Nov. 5 on Nintendo Switch, Nintendo Switch 2, PlayStation 5, Windows PC, and Xbox Series X. The game was reviewed on Windows PC using a prerelease download code provided by Annapurna Interactive. You can find additional information about Polygon’s ethics policy here.

Forever Ago is an overly sentimental tear-jerker, but it's still sincere

#10616. MIT scientists build a tiny robot that swims using living muscle cells

A swimming robot does not necessarily need a bulky motor or powerful mechanical parts to move through water. MIT researchers have demonstrated that a single, carefully engineered layer of living muscle cells can generate enough force to propel a tiny robot forward.

The engineers developed an extremely thin swimming robot that uses living muscle tissue to move its fins. Described in a new study published in Advanced Functional Materials, the device has a flexible gel framework roughly the length and width of a stick of gum. The gel forms two fins, each coated with a layer of muscle cells far thinner than a human hair. These cells have been genetically modified to contract when exposed to light.

By directing light onto either fin, the researchers can trigger muscle contractions that make the fin flap and push the robot through water. Adjusting which fin receives light and how frequently it is illuminated allows the team to control both its speed and direction.

In experiments, the remarkably thin robot successfully swam and turned through a simple underwater maze. At maximum speed, it covered approximately four times its body length per minute. Although that is much slower than an Olympic swimmer, who can travel up to 65 body lengths per minute, it is comparable to the swimming pace of a cow shark moving leisurely through the ocean.

"It takes a lot of force to move through water versus air," says study author Ritu Raman, associate professor of mechanical engineering at MIT. "The robot's quite strong, given its size. "

A New Generation of Robots Powered by Living Muscle

The device represents the first demonstration of a very thin, two-dimensional robot that can move from place to place using living muscle tissue.

Most existing biohybrid robots rely on relatively large, three-dimensional structures made from laboratory-grown skeletal muscle. Producing these structures typically requires millions of cells. Raman explains that thinner designs could reduce manufacturing costs while potentially improving movement efficiency.

"Currently, biohybrid robots from our group and others' are built from bulky, 3D chunks of lab-grown skeletal muscle that require millions of cells to fabricate," says Raman, who notes that thinner, less bulky designs such as the team's new bot could be cheaper to build and could move more efficiently. "We believe that biohybrid robots powered by living muscle could one day perform delicate jobs like exploring environments too fragile or unpredictable for conventional hardware, because living tissue is soft, responsive to its surroundings, and can heal itself."

The MIT research team also included first author Maheera Bawa, along with Arielle Berman, Laura Schwendeman, Ferdows Afghah, and Seanbiron Johnson.

From Artificial Muscle to a Swimming Robot

The new swimming robot builds on an earlier achievement by Raman's laboratory. Last year, the researchers created an artificial muscle disk inspired by the human iris, demonstrating how a thin layer of living cells could produce precisely controlled movements.

For that project, the engineers patterned a gel disk with grooves extending outward from its center and forming concentric circles. They then placed living muscle cells on the surface, where the cells grew into a thin layer following the patterned channels.

When illuminated, the muscle tissue contracted in different directions, causing the disk to stretch and compress. Its movements resembled the way the iris of the human eye changes shape as the pupil expands or narrows.

The experiment established that researchers could grow extremely thin muscle layers in intricate arrangements and use light to control their movements along multiple directions.

"People hadn't seen this muscle architecture engineered from scratch before," Raman says. "And the cells were moving in multiple directions. But they only moved about 100 microns. From a robotics perspective, their movements were tiny."

For the latest project, the researchers wanted to dramatically increase the amount of movement and force generated by the muscle cells. Their goal was to produce contractions powerful enough to propel a robot through water.

They determined that achieving this would require a better foundation for the living muscle tissue.

The earlier artificial iris had used fibrin, an exceptionally soft gel, as its underlying structure. However, the team found that fibrin could quickly shrink under the forces exerted by contracting muscle cells, limiting the effectiveness of the resulting movement.

To overcome this problem, the engineers investigated three characteristics of the gel foundation: its chemical composition, its stiffness, and the dimensions and geometry of the grooves used to organize muscle growth.

"For engineering any type of tissue, it's known that these are knobs you can tune," Raman says. "And we wanted to optimize all these parameters to support live muscle cells."

Engineering a Stronger Muscle-Powered Robot

Finding the right supporting material required testing gels with different formulations, stiffness levels, and surface patterns.

The researchers compared several groove shapes, including narrow channels with square profiles and longer channels with curved bottoms. They discovered that muscle cells aligned more effectively inside square-shaped grooves.

This alignment was important because muscle cells that grow in the same direction are more likely to merge into fibers. Those fibers can then form stronger muscle tissue that contracts in a coordinated way. The square grooves therefore provided a more effective structure for producing force.

The team also replaced fibrin with gelatin methacrylate (GelMA), a material commonly used to grow and engineer biological tissue.

By adjusting the formulation of GelMA, the researchers produced supporting gels with varying degrees of stiffness. They then examined how muscle cells developed on each surface and measured their ability to generate force.

Stiffer gels produced the best results. On these firmer surfaces, muscle cells grew in better alignment and generated stronger contractions.

The engineers next investigated how thick the gel should be. They determined that a film measuring approximately half a millimeter offered an effective balance. It was sturdy enough to support a single layer of muscle cells while remaining sufficiently lightweight to prevent the contracting cells from detaching from the surface.

The team further improved muscle performance through a form of exercise. Repeated pulses of light stimulated the cells to contract, strengthening the muscle tissue before it was incorporated into the swimming robot.

With the gel structure optimized and the muscle cells strengthened, the researchers assembled their swimming device.

The finished robot consisted of a thin gel framework divided into two fins. Both sides of the gel were patterned with square-bottomed grooves and covered with living muscle cells. As the cells developed, they fused into organized muscle fibers capable of contracting together.

Each fin could be activated independently using light, giving the researchers a simple way to control the robot's movements.

"You can think of the robot as having two independent muscles," Raman says. "If we shine a light on just one, only that muscle moves. If shining on both, they both flap."

To test the device, the researchers placed it inside a large petri dish filled with water. They then moved a light source by hand, illuminating the fins to guide the robot through a maze positioned inside the dish.

As the muscles contracted, the fins flapped and propelled the robot through the water. By changing the light stimulation, the engineers successfully controlled its movement and helped it navigate the obstacles.

Although the robot demonstrated the ability to swim and steer, its current shape remains relatively simple. The primary objective of the experiment was to establish that an exceptionally thin layer of engineered muscle tissue could generate enough force for swimming.

The researchers now plan to refine the robot's body to improve its speed. Beyond demonstrating a new approach to biohybrid robotics, the technology could eventually be useful for monitoring aquatic environments or operating in locations where conventional rigid machines might cause damage.

"Our next goal is to optimize the body design to enable faster swimming," Raman says. "But even at slow swim speeds, one could imagine a muscle-powered swimmer being used for purposes like environmental monitoring in aquatic environments."

The Office of Naval Research provided partial funding for this research.

#10615. 7 Ways to Keep API Changes Safe as More Teams Join a Project

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A backend team renames a response field from name to displayName. Its tests pass, the deployment succeeds, and the API continues returning HTTP 200. Minutes later, a dashboard starts showing blank user labels.

The failure is easy to explain: the server changed a contract that another application depended on. Preventing it requires more than asking teams to communicate better. The dependency needs to become visible in documentation, tests, and deployment decisions.

This article follows that field rename through seven practical safeguards. The examples apply to frontend and backend teams working in the same office, across departments, or in different countries.

Start by documenting the existing response and the intended replacement.

The two objects may look equivalent to a person, but software reads specific keys. A client using user.name will not automatically discover displayName.

An OpenAPI description can document request and response shapes alongside status codes and other interface details. Keep it in version control and update it in the same change as the implementation.

Document meaning as well as structure. Does displayName contain user-supplied text? Can it be empty? Is it intended for display only? Must consumers avoid using it as an identifier?

SitePoint’s REST API best practices provides background on interface design. For an existing API, the immediate task is to identify the promises clients already rely on.

An endpoint can serve more than the application shown in the product demo. Consumers may include an administrative dashboard, a mobile app, scheduled exports, internal scripts, and external integrations.

The point is to discover different upgrade timelines. A web application may update quickly, while an installed mobile client remains active for months.

Inspect request telemetry where available, but do not treat a short period of inactivity as proof that a consumer is unused. A quarterly export will not appear in a one-week observation window.

Also record whether consumers reject unknown fields. Adding a response property is often easier to migrate than removing one, but strict decoders can make even additive changes incompatible.

For this example, a transitional response can expose both fields:

Generate both values from the same source so they cannot drift during the migration.

The fourth step needs evidence. The server usually cannot observe which property a client reads after receiving a response. Use consumer version information, published contracts, maintained integration records, and explicit confirmations where appropriate.

For external APIs, removal may require a new version or a documented deprecation process. Do not remove a field simply because the internal dashboard has migrated.

Consider rollback before deployment. If the consumer now requires the new field, rolling the server back to an older response can break it. Either preserve compatibility during rollback or give the consumer a deliberate transition strategy.

A temporary adapter can let a consumer accept both the old and transitional response formats. Keep it narrow and document when it can be removed.

Save the following runnable example as user-label.test.mjs:

Run it with node --test user-label.test.mjs.

The fallback policy is a product decision. For a navigation label, “Unnamed user” may be acceptable. For an operation that requires a verified identity, missing required data may need to stop the workflow.

These tests exercise the adapter only. They do not prove that the live API returns the expected payload or that the application calls the adapter. Add integration coverage at the boundary where the response enters the client.

For UI rendering, insert the returned label as text or use your framework’s normal escaping. The adapter is not an HTML sanitizer.

Several tools can contribute to the review, but their responsibilities differ.

A useful pipeline can lint the API description, compare it against the released baseline, run consumer tests, and verify provider behavior.

Store the baseline deliberately. Comparing two files from the same proposed change can miss a regression against the deployed contract.

SitePoint’s Postman workflow guide explains collections and request organization. Keep shared examples free of production credentials, and include failure responses alongside successful ones.

Mocks should include old, transitional, and new payloads during a migration. This gives frontend developers and designers a way to inspect loading states, missing data, and error handling before the provider rollout finishes.

As more contributors join a project, architectural decisions need to remain understandable outside the meeting where they were made.

Poland has also become a popular choice for companies looking to expand their development teams without limiting their search to local talent.

For a distributed project, the engineering requirement is concrete: a new contributor should be able to identify who owns an interface, which consumers depend on it, and how a change reaches production.

Use actual team names and version identifiers in a real repository. Link the note from the pull request and make unresolved decisions explicit.

A reviewer should not need to infer whether a field is already deprecated or whether the mobile team has been consulted. That context should remain available when colleagues work different schedules.

Define what success looks like before shipping. For this rename, useful checks might include schema-validation failures, client exceptions around user rendering, and unexpected increases in missing-name fallbacks.

Avoid putting names or entire response bodies into diagnostic logs. A template version, consumer version, error category, and correlation identifier may provide sufficient evidence.

Test combinations that can exist during deployment:

Do not assume every combination is supported forever. Define the supported window and its rollback implications.

Once the removal condition is satisfied, retire the old field and temporary adapter through reviewed changes. Update fixtures and documentation so they describe the current contract.

A safe API migration gives teams an explicit sequence, tests the compatibility assumptions, and preserves enough evidence to decide when the transition is complete. That is what allows contributors to work independently without surprising the applications that depend on them.
