#10612. Hate hearing your own voice? Blame your skull

In the 20 years I’ve been a TV producer, media executive, and speaking coach, I cannot count the number of times I’ve seen people literally cringe upon hearing their own voice. Sometimes it’s a slight scrunch of the nose and sometimes it’s an audible sigh. Why do we hate our own voice? And what can we do about it?

It’s not you, it’s biology

You’re not that special. I don’t mean that rudely, but you’re actually a member of a very large club. The reason you hate the sound of your own voice is grounded in biology. In evolutionary terms, we have only been hearing our voice played back for a short amount of time. While humans have been around for hundreds of thousands of years, our recordings have not. Thomas Edison first recorded and played back his own voice just 150 years ago. (Fun fact: he sang “Mary Had a Little Lamb.”) Our body has not caught up yet to technology.

It’s your skull’s fault

Why do your friends not cringe at your voice, but you do? They hear your voice differently than you do. When we hear our own voice, there are vibrating sound waves hitting the ear drum and vibrations inside our skull due to our vocal cords. As they travel, the lower tones come through stronger. That makes us think our voice is deeper and richer than it is. When we hear our voice as it actually sounds, we’re not used to it.  

How to get over it

When you’re just starting to improve your speaking skills, it’s very hard to listen to recordings of your own voice. However, if you’re committed to improving, it’s essential. Every CEO I media train watches their TV segments back. Every coach I work with listens to the podcast they were a guest on. Every speaker I support watches their speech. We listen to it afterwards together. 

Familiarity breeds like

Familiarity often breeds liking because of a psychological concept known as the “mere exposure” effect. Listen to your voice back. I recommend starting with audio or video where you sound unrehearsed—on a zoom call or a friend asking you about something you love, like a hobby or a vacation. That way you’ll be hearing yourself as you actually sound.

Analyze it

Separate two aspects: your message and your delivery. Ask yourself: Do I like what I said in this video/segment/podcast? Then analyze it just for delivery. If this is too hard, read a transcript instead of listening to it. Now, listen to the recording. Listen for specific things, not a general “Ugh, my voice sounds weird,” which is unproductive. 

Ask yourself: Did I have vocal range? Did I use uptalk, ending my sentences like questions? Did I bring the right energy level? Your answers will point the way to improvement. If you are feeling particularly brave, you can send a sample to a friend or trusted advisor.

Voice memos are another great way to get more comfortable with the act of speaking on cue. You can send them to friends or clients. While you do this, use the wave form of voice memos or another app. Pay attention to your vocal range; you want a normal roller coaster, not high mountains.

Just because you are not used to your voice doesn’t mean there is anything wrong with it. The sooner you can move past the judgment around your voice, the sooner you can focus on what you’re saying and how you’re using your voice to spread your knowledge, be seen as a leader, or, well, just chat.

→ Baca artikel asli

#10611. Midterm voters to decide on these key ballot measures, putting Trump’s agenda to the test

A battle over abortion rights is playing out in several states Tuesday as voters decide 145 proposed laws and constitutional amendments, including some that could affect voting for future elections.

The ballot measures provide a political undercard for a high-profile midterm election that will determine which party controls Congress, state legislatures and gubernatorial offices in many states.

Some measures put President Donald Trump’s priorities to a public test by gauging support for his attempts to reshape congressional districts, limit transgender athletes in sports and strengthen voter identification requirements.

Abortion ban faces an unusual redo in Missouri

Two years ago, Missouri voters became the first in the nation to pass a ballot measure undoing an existing ban on most abortions.

Now voters are deciding whether to repeal the 2024 abortion rights amendment and replace it with a new prohibition — this time allowing exceptions for medical emergencies and fetal anomalies, and in cases of rape or incest up to 12 weeks of pregnancy. The proposed ban was placed on the ballot by the Republican-led Legislature. Abortion rights advocates, led by national progressive groups, have greatly outspent abortion foes in a bid to defeat the measure.

In three other states, ballot measures would create a right to abortion using standards similar to what was in place nationally before the Supreme Court overturned Roe v. Wade in 2022 and opened the door to state bans.

Ballot measures in Idaho and Nevada would block bans on abortion before fetal viability, usually considered after 21 weeks. Nevada voters already approved the measure once but have to do so a second time for it to become official. In Virginia, an amendment would ensure abortion is allowed for the first two trimesters.

Redistricting measures could affect 2028 elections

Nine states are using new congressional districts in the midterm elections after a rare spate of mid-decade redistricting encouraged by Trump.

Missouri’s Republican lawmakers also had adopted new House districts designed to help the GOP win an additional seat. But courts ruled they cannot be used unless first approved by voters in a November referendum. If passed, the districts would take effect for the 2028 election.

Voters in Democratic-led Maryland are deciding on a constitutional amendment that also could pave the way for new congressional districts in the 2028 election. Democratic proponents, backed by former President Barack Obama, have urged a “yes” vote as way to “stop Trump.”

Some Democrats also want to redistrict in Colorado. But a citizen initiative on the ballot would prohibit any potential 2028 redistricting from favoring one political party and require approval from both an independent commission and the state Supreme Court.

Transgender restrictions are proposed in several states

Voters in Arizona, Colorado, Missouri, Nebraska and Washington are considering measures restricting transgender athletes or medical procedures. That comes as Trump’s administration has been pushing for restrictions impacting transgender people, especially youth.

Most Republican-led states already bar transgender women and girls from female school sports teams. Citizen-led ballot initiatives propose similar restrictions in the Democratic-led states of Colorado and Washington. The Arizona measure would extend public school sports restrictions to private schools. And Nebraska’s measure would add existing restrictions to the state constitution.

A second ballot question in Colorado would ban some transgender medical treatments for minors. The Missouri abortion amendment also would add existing transgender medical restrictions to the state constitution.

Five state measures seek voter ID mandates

Though Trump’s push for nationwide voter ID mandates remains stalled in Congress, citizen activists bypassed Democratic-led state legislatures to place new voter ID requirements on the ballot in California, Colorado and Nevada.

Those measures would require government-issued identification — like driver’s licenses or partial Social Security numbers — to cast ballots. The Nevada amendment already won approval in 2024 but needs a second vote to take effect.

Measures referred to the ballot by Republican-led legislatures in North Carolina and Ohio would enhance existing voter ID requirements by placing them in the state constitution.

Six states consider citizen voting measures

Ballot measures in Alaska, Arizona, Arkansas, Kansas, South Dakota and West Virginia would reinforce current election practices limiting voting to U.S. citizens.

Critics argue the measures are largely symbolic and unnecessary, because voting by noncitizens is rare and already forbidden in federal elections. But noncitizens are allowed to vote in some local elections in California, Maryland, Vermont and the District of Columbia.

Two years ago, similar citizen voting amendments won approval in all eight states where they appeared on ballots.

Elections themselves are up for a vote in some states

A Massachusetts ballot measure would end separate party primaries in favor of open primaries where the top two vote recipients of any party advance to the general election. In Alaska, voters are deciding for the second straight election whether to scrap their system of open primaries and ranked choice voting.

Proposed amendments in North Dakota and South Dakota would make it harder to amend their state constitutions by requiring 60% approval from voters instead of a simple majority. A similar 60% threshold is proposed in Utah for citizen initiatives that raise taxes.

Meanwhile, a Missouri amendment would make it harder for lawmakers to change voter-approved citizen initiatives by requiring revisions to get an 80% vote in the state House and Senate.

Colorado amendment would aid immigration enforcement

Trump’s immigration enforcement efforts could get a boost in Democratic-led Colorado under a proposed constitutional amendment that would chip away at state laws restricting cooperation with federal immigration agents.

The proposed amendment, placed on the ballot by conservative activists, would require law enforcement officers to notify the U.S. Department of Homeland Security when someone with an illegal or unknown immigration status is charged with a violent crime or faces a new charge following a prior felony conviction.

Arizona measure would limit traffic cameras

Voters in Arizona are deciding on a proposal limiting the use of traffic cameras to catch drivers speeding or running red lights. The Republican-led Legislature referred the measure to voters after Democratic Gov. Katie Hobbs vetoed a ban on the cameras.

Hobbs said the cameras enhance roadway safety, but opponents contend they infringe on people’s privacy and point to instances of misuse by certain law enforcement officers.

The proposal would bar new contracts for traffic camera systems starting in 2027 and require local governments to hold elections on whether to continue existing systems.

About half the states, including Arizona, have communities that use cameras to enforce speeding or traffic signal laws, according to the Insurance Institute for Highway Safety.

Associated Press writer Geoff Mulvihill contributed to this report.

—David A. Lieb, Associated Press

#10609. U.S. job postings rose sharply in September

U.S. employers ramped up their job postings in September as stronger economic growth may be encouraging more hiring. Yet voters are headed to the polls Tuesday, largely gloomy over an economy beset by higher prices.

U.S. job openings jumped to 7.5 million from a revised 7.19 million in August, the Labor Department reported Tuesday. It is the largest number of openings since May and up from a recent low of 6.9 million in March.

The department’s Job Openings and Labor Turnover Survey (JOLTS) also showed that gross hiring—job gains before subtracting people who quit or lost jobs—rose and layoffs fell in September.

The increases are a hopeful sign that hiring could improve, but the trend would have to continue for several months to suggest real progress. Hiring has been sluggish for nearly three years, though layoffs have also been historically low, a sign of meager “churn” in the labor market. Low churn means those who are employed are enjoying relatively high levels of job security, but it is much harder than normal for those looking for work to find a job.

Still, the number of job openings now exceeds the number of unemployed, a sharp switch from a year ago when there were more people out of work than available jobs.

“It’s an encouraging sign that despite all the headwinds right now with high diesel and gas prices and ongoing inflation problems, companies are slowly increasing their hiring,” Heather Long, chief economist at Navy Federal Credit Union, said.

Job openings rose the most in professional and business services, which includes high-paying jobs in engineering and accounting, as well as jobs in the temp industry. Manufacturers, shipping firms, and state and local governments also advertised for more workers.

Nicole Bachaud, a labor economist at ZipRecruiter, cautioned that with inflation still high and interest rates having increased since the summer, companies may not be able to ramp up hiring by much in the coming months.

“All of that added up together just makes it much more expensive to operate as a business and much more expensive to expand,” she said. “And so that will likely continue to be a downward pressure on hiring, until we see inflation really able to cool down.”

The government will issue the October jobs report on Friday, which is forecast to show that employers added 65,000 positions last month, according to data provider FactSet. That would be an improvement from 29,000 in September. The unemployment rate is expected to stay at a low 4.2%.

Typically, job gains of just 65,000 would be seen as a sign of a struggling economy, but with the giant baby boom generation retiring and immigration sharply reduced, the economy doesn’t need to generate as many jobs as it has in the past for unemployment to remain low.

In September, layoffs fell sharply to just 1.64 million, the fewest since March 2025. The number of people quitting their jobs rose to 3.37 million, the highest in about 18 months, and up from 3.04 million.

The rise in quits is generally a good sign because workers usually leave a job when they either have another lined up or when they are confident they can find new work.

—Christopher Rugaber, AP economics writer

#10606. Election Day could mean big things for your 401(k). Here’s what history tells us

Whether or not Tuesday’s midterm elections deliver a so-called blue wave, one thing is for certain: More volatility will likely ripple through financial markets.

Midterm years have been historically weak for the U.S. stock market, delivering annual gains of just 7.5% versus an average return of 12.4% during non-midterm years, according to figures compiled by global investment management firm BlackRock. And even though more money has been pumped into this year’s midterm campaign than any other in history—to the tune of nearly $12 billion—your 401(k) portfolio isn’t likely to benefit so much.

Despite polling that points to Democrats gaining seats, nay the majority, in Congress, investors haven’t been positioning for such an outcome. Rather, a survey of more than 100 institutional investors conducted by Wolfe Research suggests that these key market players haven’t positioned themselves for any particular result, according to CNBC.

“Investors seem to have a significant dose of skepticism about whether the final outcome is going to be quite as favorable to Democrats as the polling suggests,” Tobin Marcus, the firm’s head of U.S. policy and politics, told the outlet. 

Likewise, veteran market strategist Ed Yardeni warns that the stock market hasn’t fully factored in a blue wave, as such an outcome could lead to “much worse” partisanship in Washington, D.C. “It could be a very cold shower for the market in terms of the gridlock,” Yardeni, president of Yardeni Research, told Bloomberg. 

Why history favors investors

For long-term investors, the best course of action is to ignore election results and stay invested. BlackRock cautions that letting politics guide investment decisions has come at a significant cost in recent years. Since 2013, for instance, a $100,000 investment in the S&P 500 Index would have grown to nearly $400,000 at the start of 2026 if left untouched; by contrast, investors who moved money out of the market during that time frame based on their politics saw much lower returns.

What’s more, any kneejerk reaction to the election outcomes may not be as dire as some people are warning. 

JPMorgan’s trading desk puts the odds of a Democratic sweep at 66%, which would lead to a subdued knee-jerk response for the S&P 500. Instead, the less likely outcomes of Republicans retaining control of both the House and Senate (with odds of only 7%) or Democrats taking the House while Republicans maintain control of the Senate (with odds of 26%) could see stocks rise as much as 1.25%.

Any dips or spikes in the stock market are likely to be short-lived. Since 1970, the S&P 500 has risen by an average of 14.1% in the six months following midterm elections, according to BlackRock. However, in scenarios where one party lost majority control of both the House and the Senate—as polling suggests could happen this year—returns averaged just 10.4% during that same period.

HOW THE OUTCOME COULD AFFECT INVESTORS

Complicating matters for this particular midterm election is the sharp rise in stock prices this year, fueled by unprecedented spending to support the AI buildout. Meanwhile, the Treasury market just suffered its worst quarterly sell-off since 1994.

That’s meant a mixed bag for retirement savers as the classic 60/40 portfolio has delivered lackluster results this year. This allocation model, popularized by the likes of famed investor John Bogle, is made up of 60% stocks and 40% bonds. So far in 2026, the 60/40 strategy has delivered a return of nearly 7.1%, well below the 10.1% annualized return over the last 10 years, according to estimates by PortfoliosLab, a portfolio analytics and educational platform.

A Democratic sweep of Congress could put more pressure on stocks—and particularly the high-flying AI stocks that have driven most of the market’s rally this year. That’s because Democrats are seen as being more likely to pump the brakes on the data center projects that are crucial to the AI buildout and, hence, the market’s continued gains. 

Because of how central the AI trade has become to the overall market, investors will closely watch a few key governor’s races—particularly Texas, which Republican Gov. Greg Abbott has called the “epicenter of AI development.” A surprise loss for the incumbent governor has the “potential to spook” the stock market in the short term, as Wolfe Research’s Marcus told CNBC.

On the other hand, if Republicans somehow manage to retain control of both the House and Senate, it would have the most dramatic implications for the bond market, according to Reuters. That’s because the Republican policy agenda could continue, further expanding the deficit and therefore pushing Treasury yields even higher. 

But no matter what happens, Wednesday will at least bring an end to the deluge of political advertisements and texts to voters—as well as the pre-election anxiety that can grip markets, as Ryan Detrick, chief market strategist at the wealth advisory network Carson Group, told CNBC. 

“Once you are through it, it’s a big relief and markets tend to do pretty well,” he said.

#10605. Cambridge scientists create a $10 chip that could put a medical lab on your wrist

A new optical sensor costing just $10 could bring laboratory-level precision to wearable health monitors, potentially allowing people to track blood glucose without needles. The technology could also transform food testing, industrial quality control, and environmental monitoring.

Researchers at the University of Cambridge and GlitterinTech (a start-up founded by the same research group) have developed a new type of optical spectrometer that combines exceptional accuracy with a remarkably small size and low cost. Measuring just centimeters across, the device could be incorporated into portable electronics and wearable health technology. Its potential applications range from monitoring important health markers in real time to identifying materials and detecting changes in chemical composition.

Optical spectrometers work by analyzing light to reveal information about the substances it interacts with. They are essential tools in medicine, manufacturing, chemical testing, and environmental research. However, making these instruments smaller has traditionally meant compromising their performance. Compact spectrometers often have limited measurement ranges, reduced resolution, or lower accuracy, making them better suited to identifying substances than performing precise measurements.

The researchers believe their new design could overcome these limitations. Known as a convolutional spectrometer, it uses a mathematical principle called the convolution theorem to analyze light in a fundamentally different way. According to the team, this approach delivers performance that surpasses existing types of spectrometers in several key areas, including designs based on light dispersion, Fourier transforms, and computational reconstruction.

A New Way to Analyze Light With Extraordinary Precision

Traditional spectrometers generally separate incoming light into its component wavelengths or use complex calculations to reconstruct its spectrum. The new device takes another approach by performing a mathematical operation called convolution directly on the incoming light.

To accomplish this, the researchers arranged optical components with repeating spectral responses in a sequence. These components can include unbalanced Mach-Zehnder interferometers or micro-ring resonators. By adjusting them in proportion to one another, the system can shift its response to different wavelengths in a controlled, linear fashion. Fast Fourier transforms are then used to accurately recover the light spectrum.

"The key insight was to go back to the mathematics and ask whether there was a fundamentally cleaner way to retrieve spectra," said Dr. Chunhui Yao, a lead author of the study. "By using the convolution theorem directly in the optical domain, we avoid many of the limitations that have held miniaturized spectrometers back. This gives us high precision, strong noise tolerance, and very low computational overhead, all in a compact and low-cost system."

The researchers built the spectrometer using a silicon nitride photonic platform and integrated it with onboard electronics. It operates across a broad range of near-infrared wavelengths (1200-1700 nm) and can collect and process measurements in less than a second.

Another important advantage is its ability to expand its measurement capabilities without major hardware changes. Because the system uses repeating optical patterns, its spectral bandwidth can theoretically be extended almost without limit. Adding more optical components in sequence can also increase its resolution exponentially.

A $10 Sensor That Could Transform Food Testing and Healthcare

To evaluate the device outside purely theoretical measurements, the researchers tested it on a variety of materials and substances. The results demonstrated its potential for applications that normally depend on larger, more expensive laboratory instruments.

When used to identify plastics, pharmaceuticals, coffee, flour, and tea, the spectrometer achieved a 100% classification success rate in the reported tests. It also measured concentrations in water-based and organic solutions with accuracies of approximately 0.01%, exceeding the performance of commercial benchtop spectrometers in those experiments.

The healthcare demonstrations were particularly promising. Under conditions designed to reflect real physiological environments, the device accurately measured several human biomarkers, including skin moisture, blood alcohol, blood lactate, and blood glucose.

The researchers also demonstrated noninvasive glucose tracking over an extended period in one participant. Although further testing would be needed before the technology could become a practical medical product, the finding suggests a possible route toward monitoring blood sugar without repeatedly piercing the skin.

"These biomedical demonstrations are particularly exciting," said Prof. Qixiang Cheng, who led the project. "What makes this work stand out is not just performance in the lab, but technical readiness. Dr. Chunhui Yao's contribution was crucial in translating a mathematical concept into a fully packaged, robust system that operates reliably across temperature extremes and real-world conditions. That combination is what opens the door to practical deployment."

The sensor also demonstrated impressive resistance to temperature changes. It continued operating reliably between -20°C and 80°C, an unusually wide temperature range for a miniature spectrometer. This durability could be especially valuable for wearable devices, factory equipment, and sensors operating outdoors.

Why a Simpler Design Could Be a Major Breakthrough

The new spectrometer's advantages extend beyond its accuracy, size, and price. Its internal design also reduces the amount of computing power needed to analyze incoming light.

Many existing miniature spectrometers depend on complicated calibration procedures and demanding algorithms to reconstruct spectral information. These methods can become unreliable when measurements contain noise, and they often require substantial processing resources.

The Cambridge team's convolution-based approach avoids much of this complexity. Because the measurements follow a linear mathematical relationship, the system can recover spectra rapidly and consistently while using relatively little computational power.

"This is a beautiful example of how elegant engineering can unlock real impact," said Prof. Richard Penty, who contributed to the photonic system design and integration. "The architecture is simple, scalable, and manufacturable, yet it delivers precision that rivals or exceeds much larger instruments. Dr. Chunhui Yao played a central role in bringing together the photonic design, system integration, and experimental validation that made this possible."

Its relatively simple construction could also make the technology easier to manufacture on a large scale, potentially bringing sophisticated optical measurements to devices where conventional spectrometers would be too bulky or expensive.

Bringing Laboratory-Grade Measurements to Everyday Devices

The researchers envision a future in which accurate optical sensing becomes a routine feature of everyday technology rather than something largely confined to specialized laboratories.

In industrial settings, inexpensive spectrometers could be installed throughout manufacturing facilities to continuously check material composition and product quality. Similar sensors could monitor food freshness, identify substances, and analyze environmental conditions on a much larger scale than is currently practical.

Healthcare could see equally significant changes. Compact sensors might eventually enable wearables to measure hydration levels, detect signs of alcohol intoxication, track biomarkers related to physical fitness, or continuously monitor glucose levels for people managing diabetes.

"Our vision is to make spectrometry as ubiquitous as temperature or motion sensing," added Prof. Qixiang Cheng. "This work shows that high-quality spectral information doesn't have to be confined to the laboratory -- it can be embedded directly into the technologies people use every day."

As photonic technology becomes smaller and more advanced, the convolutional spectrometer provides a promising example of how mathematical insights and practical engineering can make sophisticated scientific instruments simpler, cheaper, and more accessible. By combining high precision with a device that costs around $10, the researchers have opened a potential path toward a new generation of smart sensors capable of measuring the hidden chemistry of our bodies and surroundings.

Materials provided by University of Cambridge. Original written by Michael Shuff. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10603. The next big subscription service could be sunlight. And it might cost $5,000 an hour

A startup wants to fill the night sky with ultrapowerful beams of redirected sunlight. What could go wrong?

According to astronomers, almost everything. Reflect Orbital’s plans to launch 50,000 satellites equipped with giant mirrors is akin to “a bullet in your head,” says one astrophysicist, citing threats to both the natural world and the future of astronomy.

Selling the sun

By offering “sunlight as a service,” Reflect aims to deploy a constellation of satellites to beam down 5-kilometer-wide solar rays on demand, ranging in brightness from “noon light to moonlight.”

Reflect’s website suggests potential use cases such as powering solar panels, illuminating disaster zones and search-and-rescue missions, and lighting up worksites, cities, and events. But with a price tag of $5,000 for an hour of sunlight, the product would be prohibitively expensive for most customers—leaving critics to assume that only militaries and titans of industry are likely to make use of Reflect’s services, despite its altruistic branding.

So far, Reflect hasn’t actually launched any satellites. In August, it received approval from the Federal Communications Commission to launch a single demonstration satellite (much to the chagrin of environmental groups, which urged the FCC to reverse its decision). But moving forward, Reflect won’t need to seek approval from the U.S. government at all: It recently booked two dedicated rocket flights with Skyroot Aerospace in India, which will be liable for any unintended consequences of satellites launched from within its borders under the Outer Space Treaty.

The end of astronomy

The prospect of messing with the day-night cycle immediately raised concerns with environmentalists and astronomers alike, who are already contending with an increasingly crowded night sky, thanks to spacecraft like SpaceX’s fleet of Starlink satellites.

Gáspár Bakos, an astrophysicist at Princeton University, told Sky & Telescope that Reflect’s plans for “sunlight on demand” are “essentially ignoring all the progress we have made for the past many decades in bringing awareness to and decreasing light pollution.”

Bakos and other astronomers worry that the beams would cause light diffusion throughout the night sky, making it incredibly difficult for ground-based telescopes to view objects in space. Amateur astronomers would also be at risk of permanent eye damage if they happened to see one of Reflect’s beams through a telescope greater than 12 inches in aperture.

Lighting up dark environments at night could also pose a threat to plants and animals that depend on the day-night cycle for biological regulation. That includes humans, whose own circadian rhythms dictate sleep patterns, hormones, and digestion among other bodily functions.

Light bulb moment

In an interview with Payload, Reflect Orbital CEO Ben Nowack responded to frequent concerns about his company’s plans. If astronomers are concerned about light pollution, Reflect will be sure to avoid getting in the way of their telescopes, he reasoned.

“If there’s an astronomer or a place that doesn’t want to see light, and they have a really hard brightness requirement . . . we will make sure that we don’t serve customers close [by],” Nowack said.

As for anxieties around the impact of life on Earth, Nowack chalks them up to a general fear of new technology. “Similar concerns and fears were brought up when light bulbs were introduced to the world, and people were worried about circadian rhythm and, like, is this godly?” he said. 

Never mind the fact that Reflect’s beams of sunlight would be just a touch brighter than light bulbs, casting light for up to 30 kilometers from their targets. Each satellite would also appear in the sky as a point source four magnitudes brighter than the moon, according to a report by Bakos and his colleagues.

“This is not a ‘concern’—this is like a bullet in your head,” Bakos said. “It’s the end of ground-based astronomy.”

#10600. Scientists overcome a hidden obstacle to more powerful quantum computers

Silicon spin qubits could play a major role in the future of quantum computing because they can be produced using the same semiconductor manufacturing methods that make today's computer chips. However, researchers have faced a significant challenge. When multiple silicon qubits are controlled at the same time, their operations tend to become less accurate. Building practical, large quantum computers will require scientists to control many qubits simultaneously while keeping errors to a minimum.

Researchers at the RIKEN Center for Emergent Matter Science and their collaborators have now made important progress toward solving this problem. In a study published in Nature Physics, the team demonstrated highly accurate control of several silicon spin qubits operating simultaneously. Their experiments used an array of five qubits connected to a shared microwave control line. By identifying a major cause of errors during simultaneous operation, the scientists developed a technique to reduce those errors and maintain precision.

Leon Camenzind, a RIKEN research scientist and co-first author who supervised the experiments, explains the significance of the results: "This shows that high-fidelity control can be maintained even when several qubits are operated at the same time and represents an important step toward scaling up silicon-based quantum processors."

Scientists Identify a Hidden Source of Quantum Computing Errors

Unlike conventional computers, which store information as bits representing either 0 or 1, quantum computers use quantum bits, or qubits, that can exist in combinations of both states. Silicon spin qubits store this information using the spin of individual electrons confined within silicon. Performing reliable quantum calculations requires extremely precise control over these electron spins.

Microwave signals provide that control, but operating multiple qubits simultaneously can create unwanted effects that interfere with their accuracy. The researchers traced much of this problem to a phenomenon called the AC Stark shift.

This effect occurs when microwave control signals alter a qubit's effective energy levels, producing changes in the phase of its quantum state. Those phase shifts can disrupt the intended operation of individual qubits, particularly when several are being controlled at once.

To counteract the problem, the team created a calibration method that compensates for these unwanted shifts. Rather than separately calibrating every possible combination of qubits, the technique relies on calibrations performed between pairs of qubits. This makes the approach more practical for systems containing larger numbers of quantum bits.

"Rather than requiring an entirely separate calibration procedure for every possible combination of qubits, our method uses calibrations between pairs of qubits to compensate for the unwanted phase shifts," explains Yi-Hsien Wu, the first author who carried out the experiments as part of his PhD research.

Quantum Qubits Achieve Accuracy Above 99.99%

The researchers combined their calibration technique with an improved device design and carefully adjusted microwave control pulses. Their results showed that exceptionally high accuracy could be maintained even when multiple qubits were operating simultaneously.

When the five qubits were tested individually, each achieved single-qubit gate fidelity well above 99.99%, with the highest approaching 99.999%. Fidelity describes how closely a quantum operation matches its intended result.

The team then tested how well the system performed when several qubits were active together. With as many as three qubits operating simultaneously, fidelity remained above 99.99%. Even when all five qubits were controlled at once, accuracy stayed at approximately 99.9%, approaching the levels needed for practical fault-tolerant quantum computing.

Although the calibration method greatly reduced errors, it did not eliminate them entirely. The researchers found that the remaining loss of accuracy was linked to drive-induced decoherence, an effect caused by the greater microwave power required to operate multiple qubits simultaneously.

Unlike the unwanted phase shifts corrected by the new technique, decoherence involves the loss of the delicate quantum coherence needed for reliable operations.

According to Camenzind, "This is a limitation, but further improvements to device design could help mitigate this source of error."

A New Path Toward Larger Quantum Processors

The findings show that controlling several silicon spin qubits at the same time does not necessarily require a major sacrifice in accuracy. By correcting a key source of errors, the researchers have demonstrated a promising approach for operating more qubits through shared microwave control lines.

This is especially important for the development of large quantum processors. Giving every qubit its own independent control system would make increasingly complex devices difficult to manage. Shared control lines could offer a more practical way to expand these systems while maintaining the precision needed for quantum calculations.

"By addressing a key source of control errors through pairwise calibration," says Camenzind. "Our study supports the use of shared control lines as a practical approach to scaling up quantum processors. Combined with silicon's compatibility with established semiconductor manufacturing, we believe that this advance could help pave the way toward larger quantum processors capable of performing increasingly complex computations."

The broader objective is to develop quantum computers that can reliably perform calculations despite the errors and disturbances that affect quantum systems. Such machines, known as fault-tolerant quantum computers, will require precise, dependable control over large numbers of qubits.

"Our long-term goal is to build a fault-tolerant quantum computer based on silicon spin qubits, and reliable parallel control is an essential step toward that goal," adds Seigo Tarucha, project manager of a silicon quantum computing project under JST's Moonshot Goal 6.

Materials provided by RIKEN. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10598. Hinge is making a surprising change to its subscriptions. Here’s who stands to benefit

Hinge users may soon have access to a more affordable subscription tier. The app’s parent company, Match Group, announced on November 2 that it’s testing a new feature geared toward women.

During its third-quarter 2026 earnings call, Match Group said the new tier will give users more control through additional filters and custom location boundaries.

“It’s designed for women in particular, but also for price-sensitive users,” Match Group CFO Steve Bailey said during the call. 

Hinge currently offers three tiers: a free version with limits on filters and daily likes; and the more customizable Hinge+ and HingeX, priced at $89.99 and $99.99 for three months, respectively. 

Tinder has similar subscription tiers, with Tinder Plus and Tinder Gold offering perks like unlimited swipes and top picks for around $25 and $35 per month, respectively.

“Hinge is one of the highest-priced products we have. The revenue per payer, for example, at Hinge is almost twice that of Tinder,” Bailey said. “It also has one of the lower payer penetrations. Its payer penetration is only slightly more than half that of Tinder.”

The company did not respond to Fast Company’s request for additional details about the new subscription tier. 

The lower-priced offering comes as consumers grow increasingly frustrated with the dating ecosystem. In a February 2026 Harris Poll, 72% of Americans said romance feels more like a transaction due to dating apps, while 77% said dating has become a marketplace.

Despite those sentiments, Hinge’s monthly active user numbers are up 12% year over year.

While Tinder is still bigger, with around 8.9 million paid users, its base is shrinking, with its paid users down 4% from last year. Tinder has since leaned on a different strategy to keep users engaged, including facilitating in-person experiences to shift the focus away from swiping.

Match Group owns a number of major dating platforms in addition to Match, Tinder, and Hinge, including OkCupid and Plenty of Fish.

#10596. The 2026 midterm elections could change everything for Trump. Here’s what’s at stake

The midterm elections have the potential to upend everything from politics to business to the stock market. But the person most impacted by Tuesday’s results could be President Donald Trump, and that, in effect, could determine the course of his final two years in office.

On the political front, the battle to control Congress is key, with a number of seats in play, including 435 in the House of Representatives and 35 in the Senate.

Should the Democrats gain control of the House (and, perhaps, even the Senate), the voting public will have broken the Trump administration and Republicans’ stronghold over Congress.

“The midterms matter in general because they determine whether there is alignment between the executive and the legislative branches,” Harvard Business School professor Vincent Pons said in an interview earlier this year. “Oftentimes, voters vote against the party of the president. Some voters have a preference for divided government, to have checks and balances. If the Republican party was to lose the midterms, either the House or both the House and the Senate, it could have dramatic consequences in terms of circumventing the power of [the administration].”

A Democratic upset could check Trump’s power, paving the way for a lame-duck presidency for the remainder of his term. A blue sweep could also give Dems the votes they need to investigate the president’s finances, his administration’s immigration policies, and bring another impeachment.

In August, Michigan Democrat and Senate candidate Abdul El-Sayed told CNN, “The first thing we’re going to do when [the Democrats] win this Senate seat? Hold Trump accountable. Then, on to the real work—taking on big corporations, taxing billionaires their wealth, and guaranteeing healthcare through Medicare for All.”

Back in January, Democratic Representative Micah Lasher of New York posted a road map for Congressional oversight should the Democrats regain control of the House: “Check the Trump administration’s lawlessness by making clear that illegal activity and corruption will be investigated, and by spurring criminal prosecutions or impeachments; focus the American public on the illegal and corrupt activity that gets lost in the daily barrage of news and distraction out of the White House; and shape the narrative of the 2028 elections and help Democrats win back the White House and grow our majority.”

In California, Democratic Representative Robert Garcia said he plans to look into the White House’s immigration policies, and “demand accountability” for the tactics used by Immigration and Customs Enforcement—the fallout of which would likely influence the 2028 presidential election.

In July, House Minority Leader Hakeem Jeffries, a New York Democrat, said, “When you put us back in charge of the United States House of Representatives, you’ll get a Congress that speaks up for everyday Americans and a Congress that stands up to an out-of-control president.” Jeffries emphasized that to beat Republicans, Democrats were focused on healthcare costs, bringing down the high cost of living, and ending the war in Iran.

A divided Congress, with Republicans maintaining their majority in at least one branch, could result in major political gridlock for the remainder of Trump’s term, which could have major repercussions for business.

If Republicans can hold on to their seats, that could empower Trump to accelerate his domestic agenda and policies on key issues like defense and AI—with those government-connected sectors seeing continued growth, and banks and cryptocurrencies retaining lighter regulation.

#10594. 7 Checks That Make Form Bugs Easier to Reproduce Across Teams

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A designer reports that a form’s error message overlaps its submit button. The developer opens the same page and sees no problem. Both are using the same code, but one has a narrow viewport, a translated interface, and an unusually long validation message.

A screenshot records what appeared on screen. A reproducible example also preserves the inputs, responses, permissions, and environment that produced it. That distinction matters when people review work at different times or join a project without knowing its undocumented conventions.

This article uses a workspace-invitation form as an example. The form accepts an email address and a role, submits a request, and displays either a confirmation or an error. The seven checks below turn its awkward states into something developers and designers can inspect together.

“The form looks broken” leaves too much for the next person to discover.

A useful report specifies the starting state, action, observed result, and expected behavior:

Include the browser, application revision, and relevant account permissions. Explain whether “360 pixels” means the browser’s layout viewport or the dimensions of an exported screenshot.

Avoid using real customer addresses in shared fixtures. A synthetic address such as member@example.test is sufficient to exercise the interface.

This report gives a designer a clear layout requirement and gives a developer a precise state to recreate.

A form tested only with successful submissions is missing most of its interesting behavior.

Create a small set of fixtures that cover the product’s actual rules:

Keep fixture values deterministic. Random addresses or constantly changing timestamps make visual comparisons harder to interpret.

Record expected outcomes alongside the data. A response containing a message does not establish where the interface should display it, whether focus should move, or which fields should remain editable.

Treat those as product decisions and test them explicitly.

Some failures can be reproduced by passing props to a component. Others depend on the request lifecycle.

Storybook args can describe inputs for isolated component states. An error-state story might supply a message, current field values, and a pending flag to a presentational form component.

However, passing pending: true does not prove that the real application enters or exits that state correctly.

For network-dependent behavior, use Mock Service Worker or your test runner’s request interception to supply a controlled response. Preserve the distinction between these two checks:

Avoid mocking the same endpoint in several overlapping layers. When a response is unexpected, the team should be able to identify which fixture produced it.

Also keep at least some integration coverage against the real API. A frontend mock that returns an outdated error shape can make the interface look reliable while hiding a broken contract.

Open the failing state at the recorded viewport. Inspect the error container, its parent layout, and the submit button.

Look for constraints such as fixed heights, absolute positioning, hidden overflow, and a flex or grid item that refuses to shrink.

A possible layout for a message beside an action is:

This pattern allows the message column to shrink and stacks the elements at a narrow width. It is not a universal fix: choose a breakpoint and arrangement that suit the surrounding form.

If the error belongs to a particular field, placing it beside the submit button may be the wrong design regardless of how well it wraps.

SitePoint’s browser-based CSS debugging guide explains how to inspect applied rules and layout behavior. Its guide to troubleshooting CSS with Inspect Element provides additional background.

Use DevTools experiments to identify the cause, then make the lasting change in the source. Check a wide viewport afterward so a narrow-screen fix does not introduce a desktop regression.

A screenshot can show that an error is present. It cannot, by itself, prove that the form preserved the email address or prevented an unintended navigation.

The following Playwright example assumes an existing application route, configured baseURL, and accessible form labels. Adapt the endpoint, response schema, and selectors to your project.

The example expects a native select for the role and a named form landmark. A custom role picker requires different interactions.

The behavior assertions precede the visual comparison. The screenshot then helps reviewers spot overlap, clipping, or unintended spacing changes.

Playwright’s visual comparison documentation explains baseline screenshots. Generate and compare them in a consistent environment; operating-system and font differences can affect rendering.

Review the first baseline manually. A screenshot test can faithfully preserve a bug if the initial image is approved without inspection. Disabling animation for a snapshot also does not test the accessibility of motion itself.

As a project grows, a component’s behavior should remain discoverable without a private walkthrough.

Poland has also become a popular choice for companies looking to expand their development teams without limiting their search to local talent.

The practical requirement for distributed contributors is a review process that carries its own context. Attach the exact fixture, preview location, reproduction command, and expected behavior to the issue or pull request.

Keep the open decision separate from the implemented behavior. This prevents reviewers from assuming that a visual change has already resolved a product question.

Name the person or team responsible for the component, and identify where design decisions are recorded. A stable fixture is useful only if someone maintains it as the component evolves.

A form that no longer overlaps can still be difficult to use.

Verify visible labels, keyboard navigation, focus visibility, and the relationship between errors and their fields. For field-level errors, consider aria-invalid and aria-describedby where appropriate.

The alert in the test example assumes a newly displayed form-level error. Do not add several competing live regions or announce every keystroke. Check the resulting experience with assistive technology.

SitePoint’s accessible form layout guide is relevant background for the relationship between structure and usability.

A focused toolset can support the workflow:

Use the smallest set that answers the current question. Adding another tool is useful when it closes a testing gap, not merely because it produces another report.

A good handoff lets someone unfamiliar with the original conversation recreate the failure, understand the expected behavior, and verify the fix. When the fixture and regression check remain with the code, that work continues to help the next contributor.

#10593. Tiny magnetic vortices could help computers work more like your brain

Tiny magnetic structures called skyrmions could help pave the way for a new generation of computers that process information more like the human brain while using far less electricity. Although most people have never heard of skyrmions, these microscopic magnetic vortices may eventually play an important role in how computers store and process data.

A study published in the journal Nature Nanotechnology provides new insights into how skyrmions behave and how scientists can control them. The findings could help researchers develop smaller, more efficient computing systems and advance emerging quantum technologies.

Most of the research was conducted at the Institute of Physics at Johannes Gutenberg University in Mainz, Germany, with contributions from a professor at the Norwegian University of Science and Technology (NTNU).

"Skyrmions are small, magnetic vortices that can store information in a completely new way," explained Asle Sudbø, who leads NTNU's Centre for Quantum Spintronics (QuSpin).

Unlike conventional technologies, which require increasing amounts of space and energy to handle growing quantities of data, skyrmions could offer a much more efficient alternative.

"Instead of the current technology, where we constantly need more space and energy to process data, skyrmions could make it possible to build much smaller and more energy-efficient data storage systems," explained Sudbø.

Tiny Magnetic Vortices Could Enable Brain-Like Computers

The potential applications of skyrmions extend well beyond conventional data storage. They are also important to spintronics, which explores ways to use the magnetic properties of electrons in electronic devices, and to research involving topological quantum effects.

One particularly promising possibility is the development of computers that operate in ways similar to biological brains.

"These nanoscale vortices could help develop computers that resemble biological brains," said Sudbø.

Rather than relying on conventional transistor-based computing, such systems could use magnetism to process information. In principle, this approach could dramatically reduce electricity consumption, potentially allowing future computers to perform complex tasks with very little energy.

Turning that possibility into practical technology, however, requires scientists to understand exactly how skyrmions behave and how to manipulate them reliably.

The latest research represents an important advance toward that goal.

Scientists Film Magnetic Skyrmion Lattices Melting for the First Time

Skyrmions do not always exist as isolated magnetic structures. Under certain conditions, they organize themselves into repeating patterns known as lattices, resembling the orderly arrangement of atoms in a crystal, but on a much smaller scale.

Scientists can manipulate these arrangements, a capability that could be essential for using skyrmions in future computing devices.

In the new study, researchers achieved something that had not been demonstrated before. They recorded the melting of a magnetic skyrmion lattice as it happened while simultaneously controlling individual skyrmions.

"For the first time, researchers have managed to film how a magnetic skyrmion lattice melts in real time, while simultaneously being able to manipulate each individual skyrmion directly as they film it. For example, both their shape and size can be changed 'on the fly'," said Sudbø.

The ability to observe this process directly provides a clearer picture of how skyrmions respond when their organized structures break apart. It also allows researchers to study how changing the size and shape of individual magnetic vortices affects their behavior.

A New Step Toward Energy-Efficient Computing

Understanding how skyrmions behave during the breakdown of their lattices could help scientists develop more precise ways to control them. That control is particularly important if these magnetic structures are to become useful components in future computer systems.

The findings therefore offer more than a new way to observe an unusual magnetic phenomenon. They provide information that could help researchers overcome challenges involved in developing practical skyrmion-based technologies.

"The findings give us new insight into how these types of particles behave. This could therefore become important for the development of more energy-efficient computer technology in the future," said Sudbø.

Although substantial development is still needed before skyrmions can be used in everyday computing devices, the research highlights their potential for improving data storage, enabling new computing architectures, and supporting advances in quantum technology.

By learning to observe and manipulate these tiny magnetic vortices in real time, scientists are gaining capabilities that could eventually help reshape the design of computers.

"This could revolutionize the way we think about computers," concluded Sudbø.

Materials provided by Norwegian University of Science and Technology. Original written by Steinar Brandslet. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10591. The Six Pillars Of AI Governance: From Restriction To Enabler

Nino Letteriello is a data and project management leader, DAMA Award winner, WEF author, UN advisor, MIT lecturer & FIT Group founder.

​What many of you are sensing is really happening: Employees are moving faster than their organizations when it comes to AI adoption. This is an unexpected scenario, radically different from what we witnessed more than 20 years ago with the advent of big data. Things are changing rapidly, and organizations aren’t ready. AI governance is therefore becoming a necessity rather than an opportunity, making it urgent for every company to implement it as soon as possible. Without further ado, here’s how I believe AI governance should be structured in any organization.

Effective governance starts with visibility. Organizations need to understand what data they hold, where it comes from, how reliable and relevant it is and how it’s classified from a security perspective. They must also identify the AI tools and services already being used across the business, including informal or unauthorized applications sometimes described as “shadow AI.”

Without this baseline, leaders can’t assess exposure, set meaningful priorities or determine where AI can create value. An inventory of data and AI use is therefore the starting point for informed decisions.

AI systems can make organizational knowledge easier to retrieve and use, but this also makes access management essential. Not every employee should be able to access every document, data source or business process through an AI assistant.

Governance must translate security requirements into practical controls, including permissions, access rights and safeguards for sensitive information. The objective isn’t to block innovation indiscriminately but to protect the organization’s information assets while enabling appropriate and secure uses of AI.

Policies and technical safeguards are insufficient if people don’t understand how AI works or how to use it appropriately. Employees need to recognize both the opportunities and the limitations of AI, understand the risks of sharing information with external services and know how to evaluate generated outputs.

AI literacy should go beyond compliance training: It should help people make informed choices in everyday work. Clear guidance, practical examples and role-specific training can reduce avoidable risks while increasing confidence and supporting responsible adoption.

AI can generate recommendations, support analysis and automate tasks, but responsibility for decisions must remain clearly assigned. Organizations should define who can approve an AI use case, who validates its outputs and who is accountable for decisions influenced by the system.

Human oversight is particularly important when outputs may affect people, business-critical processes or significant decisions. The level of review should reflect the context and potential consequences. AI should strengthen human judgment, not become an excuse for leaving responsibility undefined.

AI governance can’t be treated as a one-off project. Models, data, business needs and risks change over time, and systems that perform adequately today may produce unreliable or unsuitable results tomorrow.

Organizations need ongoing monitoring, periodic reviews and clear mechanisms for identifying and addressing problems. These activities should assess whether AI systems remain reliable, appropriate and aligned with their intended purposes. Lessons from data governance are directly relevant: Effective control depends on continuous measurement, review and improvement rather than policies that are written once and then forgotten.

Technology decisions shape the security, cost, compliance, scalability and flexibility of AI adoption. Organizations must evaluate available platforms, commercial solutions, locally deployed systems and alternative architectures in light of their needs and risk profile. The right choice depends on the data involved, the intended use, access requirements and operational constraints.

Technology shouldn’t be selected in isolation or treated as a purely technical matter; it must support the governance model and the organization’s wider objectives. Sound architectural choices create a foundation for innovation that can scale responsibly.​

Once these six pillars are understood and put into practice, an organization can develop an AI policy grounded in its actual operating environment. That policy should set out clear principles, responsibilities and good practices, including expectations for ethical use. Starting with a policy alone, without first understanding the data, tools, skills, controls and technical landscape, risks producing rules that are difficult to apply or enforce.

The six pillars are mutually reinforcing. Knowledge makes risks visible, security protects information, literacy helps people act responsibly, accountability keeps decisions answerable to humans, monitoring supports continuous improvement and technology provides the foundations on which AI operates. Together, they turn governance from a restrictive compliance exercise into an enabler of organizational learning, process improvement and innovation.​

Data management and data governance professionals can play a central role in this transition because they already understand that technology delivers lasting value only when it’s supported by sound practices, clear responsibilities and a holistic view of the organization.

Forbes Technology Council is an invitation-only community for world-class CIOs, CTOs and technology executives. Do I qualify?

→ Baca artikel asli

#10590. iPad Mini review: A pricey delight

For a device with such a straightforward name, the iPad Mini has had many lives over its 14 years. Yes, it’s always been a small iPad. But at various times, it’s also been a cheap iPad with a lesser display, an iPad as technologically sophisticated as any you could buy, and a midrange iPad.

Last week, Apple announced a new iPad—one of the remarkably large number of products the company introduced during what may have been its most productive October ever. It remains a small iPad. But the new version replaces the LCD screen of past versions with OLED, a technology previously available only in the iPad Pro. That’s one of several upgrades that take the new Mini to a tier of its own—not quite on the level of the iPad Pro, but in some respects above the iPad Air models the previous Mini closely resembled.

The new iPad Mini also costs $100 more than its predecessor. That version went up $100 itself in June, when Apple raised many of its prices in reaction to the AI-induced RAM shortage. Now the Mini starts at an imposing $699 for the Wi-Fi version with 128 GB of storage, a 40% increase in a little over four months, and just $50 less than the cheapest iPad Air. (The basic, entry-level iPad also went up $100, but I still consider it a bargain at $449.)

Even if the iPad Mini is no longer an impulse item for most of us, it remains the present-day iPad that reminds me most of Steve Jobs’s keynote introduction of the original 2010 iPad. In it, he outlined the need for the device and did much of his demo from the comfort of a cushy chair. That niche—bigger than a phone, more intimate than a laptop—is alive, well, and still evolving in the new Mini.

The Mini’s roomy screen lets it do many things well that an iPhone can’t, unless it’s an iPhone Duo. Unlike its larger iPad siblings, however, it’s not trying to encroach on the traditional territory of a MacBook or other PCs. It’s a tablet in the category’s purest sense. (I am aware that third-party manufacturers have long made keyboard cases for iPad Minis and will surely offer ones for this model—I just can’t envision even that setup replacing anyone’s laptop.)

Since the beginning, the Mini has been all about size, weight, and the pleasure of spending time with a device that you can hold for extended periods—in one hand, even—without it seeming to grow mysteriously heavier over time. It’s also been the easiest iPad to grab and take almost anywhere, maybe in a jacket pocket.

After all these years, Apple is still finding ways to wring heft out of the new Mini’s design. Compared to the previous version, the most striking improvements are the 16% reduction in thickness and (for the Wi-Fi model) 13.8% reduction in weight. But Apple also shrank the display’s border by 25%, which allowed it to increase the screen size from 8.3 inches to 8.5 inches, even though the new model is slightly narrower. It’s about 45% lighter than the 11-inch iPad Air and 59% lighter than the 13-inch Air.

Consequently, this tablet is a delight for tasks where more display real estate than a (non-folding) phone is desirable, but 11 or 13 inches may be overkill—long-form reading on the web or in an e-reader app, gaming, viewing and wrangling photos, lightweight productivity such as email triage. It’s also an extremely pleasant way to watch video, assuming you’re not trying to re-create the experience of watching a blockbuster movie in the theater. And its support for Apple Pencil Pro makes it a beguiling digital sketchbook when you’re out and about and lugging a larger iPad would feel like a burden.

As for the new OLED screen, it delivers on the technology’s promise by providing deep blacks, rich colors, and peak brightness of 1,200 nits, compared to 500 nits for the previous model. Some people—such as Multicore’s Sam Byford—regret that the display doesn’t include Apple’s ProMotion technology, which uses a variable refresh rate to make scrolling and input smoother and more responsive. I don’t find its omission glaring. (The 256 GB and 512 GB versions do offer Apple’s glare-reducing nano-texture screen—previously exclusive to the iPad Pro—as a $100 option.)

The new Mini is the first iPad to incorporate an audio system called Acoustic Display, which moves the speakers behind the screen. The resulting output sounds crisper to me than what I’m used to on my 13-inch iPad Pro. Just as notable, the new location allowed Apple to seal up the tablet, making it the first iPad ever to achieve an IP67 rating for water and dust resistance. It should be able to withstand being immersed in 3 feet of water for 30 minutes—a feature that makes sense considering it’s the iPad you’re most likely to take to the beach or pool, or use in a bathtub.

Another change brings the iPad Mini in line with other current iPads: Its front-facing camera is on the tablet’s landscape edge rather than the portrait one. If you’re using a larger iPad in a keyboard case, that’s unquestionably the proper spot, since it centers the camera above the screen rather than putting it on the side, which can leave you looking shifty-eyed on video calls.

I have always used iPad Minis mostly in portrait mode, so I’m not as sure the camera relocation is an improvement in this case. But it’s not an issue as long as you remember to rotate the tablet into landscape mode for FaceTime, Zoom, and the like.

Despite the new Mini’s more upscale feel, Apple has stuck with a Touch ID sensor rather than equipping it with Face ID, which remains exclusive to iPhones and the iPad Pro. It’s built into the power button and, in my experience, works close to 100% of the time. But it requires more thought to use than Face ID, especially since the sensor’s location depends on how you’re holding the Mini. It’s the one area where using the Mini feels like less than a first-class experience.

Then there’s the Mini’s processor, which I haven’t mentioned until now because so many of the tasks this tablet is well suited to don’t cry out for raw performance. (Fancy 3D games and on-device AI are exceptions.)

Apple didn’t skimp: It chose the A20 Pro chip, a processor that is itself new, having debuted in September with the iPhone 18 Pro. The company touts benchmark numbers such as up to 60% faster CPU performance and up to 270% faster GPU performance than the previous Mini. That version used the A17 Pro, a chip that wasn’t new even when that tablet was released two years ago.

Even if you don’t care how much faster a new iPad is than its predecessor, the fact that the tablet has Apple’s latest chip is a boon. It will extend the tablet’s useful life before it struggles to run new apps or is no longer supported by iPadOS updates. That may be more important for iPads than other devices: Anecdotally, at least, people tend to hold on to them for years.

One other thing about the new iPad Mini is worth mentioning—not because it’s changed, but because it hasn’t. The tablet runs iPadOS 27, the latest version of the iPad’s operating system. That sports the same multitasking interface introduced in iPadOS 26—a Mac-esque world with floating windows, menus, and traffic-light-like buttons for expanding, minimizing, and closing windows. Even on the iPad Pro I use as a work computer for hours a day, I am not enthralled with the clutter and complexity it brings. On a Mini, actually trying to use these features feels like suffocating overkill.

When setting up a new iPad Mini, you can avoid this experience altogether by choosing the “Full Screen Apps” option. That limits you to one on-screen app at a time, just like the iPads of yore. That, I suspect, will be plenty for a high percentage of users. If you expect to be one of them, you can stop reading now.

Still, there is value in letting a Mini-size device do a certain amount of multitasking, particularly when you’re holding it in landscape orientation and performing tasks that involve multiple apps. As it happens, Apple just took a fresh shot at creating an interface tuned to this scenario.

That would be the one offered by the iPhone Duo, whose 7.6-inch interior display is close enough in size to the iPad Mini that many people instinctively talk about the Duo as if it were a Mini you could fold up and put in your pocket. It offers far fewer options than iPad multitasking, but provides a more intuitive, foolproof way to perform the one form of multitasking that’s most useful on a small tablet: putting two apps side by side without letting them overlap or float.

Now, the iPhone Duo is definitely an iPhone. It’s obvious why it doesn’t borrow iPadOS 26/27-style multitasking, which would be bizarre on a device so many people use for quick hits of information when they’re walking around. The fact that the Duo can’t run iPad apps may even be a stance on the company’s part: An iPhone with an iPad Mini-like interior screen size isn’t that close a relative of the iPad Mini after all.

I do hope, however, that Apple is open to the possibility of the Duo’s influence rubbing off on future versions of iPadOS. The new iPad Mini’s hardware proves that the Mini is as relevant as ever. It’s still getting better at fulfilling the iPad’s original vision. The same should be true of the software it runs.

#10589. No GPU left behind: Unlocking Efficiency with Co-located vLLM in TRL

#10588. Texas wanted to power America’s AI boom. Its new governor could change everything

Less than a year ago, Texas Governor Greg Abbott stood beside Sundar Pichai, the Google CEO, to celebrate the company’s $40 billion investment in the state’s cloud and AI infrastructure. Three new data center campuses would help make Texas, Abbott declared, the “epicenter of AI development.” 

On Tuesday, Abbott, a Republican, lost his reelection bid to Gina Hinojosa, a Democrat who campaigned on halting new data center development until state lawmakers pass stronger protections for residents. At a press conference over the summer, Hinojosa accused corporations of taking advantage of Texans and operating with little oversight, blaming Abbott for creating the “Wild West of data centers.”

Her stunning victory makes her the first Democrat elected governor of Texas since Ann Richards in 1990. And given that Texas is home to the country’s second-largest data center market, behind Virginia, the political upheaval could have profound implications for the artificial intelligence boom.

The scale of Texas’s AI bet

Texas’s $2.9 trillion economy is the nation’s second largest; if it were a country, it would rank eighth worldwide. It boasts abundant energy resources, a ton of relatively inexpensive land, and a historically light regulatory touch—all of which have enticed AI companies (and companies in seemingly every other sector, for that matter). Researchers at Rice University’s Baker Institute estimate that annual data center investment in Texas rose from $3.4 billion in 2022 to a projected $66.2 billion in 2026.

And much more is in the pipeline. Texas accounts for roughly 26% of planned American data center capacity—nearly three times the amount planned in Virginia—according to an analysis by the investment bank Jefferies, as reported by The Wall Street Journal. Jefferies estimated that Hinojosa’s proposed moratorium could put 13.3 gigawatts of announced Texas data center projects at risk, potentially delaying construction or forcing developers to abandon their plans. Little wonder that Bank of America called the Texas governor’s race the most consequential contest of the 2026 midterms for utility and power markets.

The stakes extend well beyond Texas. The companies racing to develop AI depend on enormous infrastructure investments that take years to plan and require far more than financing. They need electricity, water, land, permits—and, increasingly, the support of the communities where they hope to build.

Who gets the benefits? And who pays the costs?

Hinojosa has proposed a moratorium until lawmakers enact lasting safeguards. She also wants to prohibit nondisclosure agreements that keep communities in the dark, give residents greater say over new developments, and require companies to prove their projects won’t raise household water or electricity bills. And she wants to eliminate the government subsidies that have helped make Texas so attractive to developers.

One of those subsidies is particularly lucrative. Qualifying data centers can avoid Texas’s 6.25% sales tax on equipment and other purchases for up to 20 years. The state comptroller projected that the exemption would cost $3.2 billion in foregone revenue over two years, potentially an underestimate. The industry argues the incentives attract investment, jobs, and other tax revenue. Critics question the public return when facilities can also place substantial demands on utilities and natural resources.

The advantages didn’t end with tax breaks. A Texas Tribune analysis found that about half of planned data centers are slated for unincorporated areas, where counties generally lack the zoning authority cities use to regulate development. That can leave nearby residents with little say over projects that could transform their communities.

And the scale of Texas’s AI ambitions is putting growing pressure on its resources. University of Texas researchers estimate that data centers currently account for less than 1% of the state’s water use, but could account for as much as 9% by 2040. Meanwhile, proposed facilities are seeking so much power that Texas’s grid operator has had to rethink how it assesses requests to connect new projects.

The backlash was bigger than Abbott

These concerns have fueled a remarkably broad backlash. In a recent University of Houston poll, 79% of respondents supported limits on data center construction, while 76% expected new facilities to drive up electricity bills. Abbott saw such figures and, sure enough, had begun to retreat on the issue. In September, after years of courting developers, he halted new permitting approvals pending audits of projects’ water and electricity demands, and promised to pursue the elimination of tax incentives. But his restrictions were tied to those audits, whereas Hinojosa wants to keep development on hold until lawmakers create more permanent protections.

In any case, Abbott’s reversal on data centers wasn’t enough to save him. But interpreting Hinojosa’s victory as a referendum on data centers alone would be a mistake. Texans were also deeply dissatisfied with the cost of living, political corruption, and a host of other issues. And Republicans were contending with a difficult national political environment under President Donald Trump and his deeply unpopular Iran War.

A broader Democratic breakthrough also delivered a historic victory for James Talarico, who defeated Ken Paxton, the state attorney general and a Republican, to become the first Democrat elected to the U.S. Senate from Texas since 1988. Talarico has also called for tougher data center standards. But Hinojosa will have more immediate influence over the industry’s future in Texas.

Of course, winning the governor’s mansion doesn’t give Hinojosa carte blanche. A governor can set priorities and direct state agencies within existing law, but permanently changing tax exemptions or expanding counties’ zoning powers would require cooperation from the state legislature. And yes, a statewide moratorium could also encounter legal obstacles. Still, developers contemplating billions of dollars in new investments must now reckon with the possibility of tougher rules that could remain in place for years.

​​Developers can, and in some cases will, look elsewhere. But other states face their own constraints on electricity, water, and land—and their own increasingly skeptical communities. Texas may be an especially consequential example of the political difficulties confronting the industry, but it is hardly an isolated one.
